## Chapter 04: Trigonometry, Coordinate Systems & 2D Vectors — Extended Engineering & Competition Problems {#chapter-04}

*Accompanies [Chapter 04 of Thinking in the Stack](../manuals/sc32/04-trig-vectors-polar.md) (29 Worked Engineering Problems)*

### Workbook Problem 4.1: Oblique Triangle Law of Sines Angle Determination

In triangle with $a = 45.0\text{ m}$, $b = 62.0\text{ m}$, angle $A = 32.5^\circ$. Find angle $B$.

$$\frac{\sin B}{b} = \frac{\sin A}{a} \implies B = \arcsin\left(\frac{b \sin A}{a}\right)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_12:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_12:value}}^\circ}$.

In geodetic triangulation, the ratio $a/\sin A = 2R_{\text{circ}}$ equals the diameter of the triangle's circumscribing circle. Entering side $b = 62.0\text{ m}$ and multiplying by the sine projection of angle $A = 32.5^\circ$ (`62 {{key:ENTER}} 32.5 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 33.3126$) forms the altitude projection $b\sin A$; dividing by opposing side $a = 45.0\text{ m}$ (`45 {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.7403$) yields the dimensionless chord ratio $\sin B = 0.7403$. Extracting the arcsine directly in place (`{{key:ASIN}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 47.7552$) resolves the acute interior vertex angle $B = \mathbf{47.76^\circ}$—and if you also need the obtuse ambiguous-case supplement $B' = 180^\circ - B = 132.24^\circ$, simply key `180 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}}`.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.2: Static Equilibrium of Crane Boom and Cable

Crane boom weight $W = 28.0\text{ kN}$ at angle $\theta = 35.0^\circ$ from horizontal, supported by tie cable at angle $\phi = 25.0^\circ$ to boom. Cable tension:
$$T = \frac{W \cos\theta}{\sin\phi} = \frac{28.0 \cos(35^\circ)}{\sin(25^\circ)}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_13:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_13:value}}\text{ kN}}$.

Rotational equilibrium about the boom pivot balances the overturning gravitational moment $W L \cos\theta$ against the restoring cable moment $T L \sin\phi$, where the boom length $L$ cancels out. Loading boom weight $W = 28.0\text{ kN}$ and scaling by the inclination cosine (`28 {{key:ENTER}} 35 {{key:COS}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 22.9363$) isolates the perpendicular gravity force; dividing by the shallow tie-angle sine $\sin(25^\circ) \approx 0.4226$ (`25 {{key:SIN}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 54.2718$) reveals how the shallow $25^\circ$ attachment geometry amplifies guy-cable tension to nearly twice the boom weight, $T \approx \mathbf{54.27\text{ kN}}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.3: Snell's Law of Optical Refraction

Light ray incident from air ($n_1 = 1.000$) into dense flint glass ($n_2 = 1.660$) at incidence angle $\theta_1 = 55.0^\circ$. Find refracted angle $\theta_2$:
$$\sin\theta_2 = \frac{n_1 \sin\theta_1}{n_2} \implies \theta_2 = \arcsin\left(\frac{\sin(55^\circ)}{1.660}\right)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_14:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_14:value}}^\circ}$.

By Fermat's principle of least time, the tangential phase-velocity projection $n \sin\theta$ is conserved across any planar dielectric boundary. Evaluating the incident optical invariant (`55 {{key:SIN}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.8192$), dividing by the dense flint glass index $n_2 = 1.660$ (`1.660 {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.4935$), and extracting the inverse sine (`{{key:ASIN}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 29.5686$) bends the ray toward the surface normal to $\theta_2 \approx \mathbf{29.57^\circ}$. Because `{{key:ASIN}}` preserves $\sin\theta_2 = 0.4935$ in `LASTX`, you can immediately compute the angular deviation $\Delta\theta = \theta_1 - \theta_2 = 25.43^\circ$ or recall the refractive index $1.660$ earlier in the chain.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.4: Traverse Linear Misclosure Precision Ratio

A traverse boundary has total perimeter $P = 1,450.0\text{ m}$. Sum of departures $\Sigma x = +0.038\text{ m}$, sum of latitudes $\Sigma y = -0.024\text{ m}$. Calculate closure precision $1:K$:
$$\text{Misclosure} = \sqrt{(\Sigma x)^2 + (\Sigma y)^2}, \quad K = \frac{P}{\text{Misclosure}}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_17:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_17:value}}`**

When a closed boundary survey returns to its starting benchmark, accumulated angular and EDM range errors manifest as a residual displacement vector $(\Sigma x, \Sigma y)$. Computing the Euclidean misclosure (`0.038 {{key:x²}} 0.024 {{key:x²}} {{key:+}} {{key:√x}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0449$, or even faster via `0.024 {{key:ENTER}} 0.038 {{key:▸θ,r}}`) isolates the $44.9\text{ mm}$ error vector. Entering total perimeter $P = 1,450\text{ m}$, swapping registers, and dividing (`1450 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 32262.0766$) establishes a relative closure precision of **$1:32,262$**, comfortably surpassing first-order geodetic survey standards ($1:25,000$).

---

### Workbook Problem 4.5: Torque Vector Cross Product Magnitude

Lever arm $r = 0.850\text{ m}$, applied force $F = 240.0\text{ N}$ at angle $\theta = 65.0^\circ$.

$$\tau = r \cdot F \cdot \sin\theta$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_21:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_21:value}}\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}}$.

Only the tangential force component $F\sin\theta$ perpendicular to the wrench lever arm $\vec{r}$ generates rotational moment $|\vec{r} \times \vec{F}|$; the radial component $F\cos\theta$ merely compresses the bolt shaft. Multiplying lever arm by applied force (`0.850 {{key:ENTER}} 240 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 204.0000$) gives the maximum orthogonal torque $204.0\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}$, and scaling by $\sin(65^\circ)$ (`65 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 184.8868$) yields the effective tightening torque $\tau \approx \mathbf{184.89\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.6: Double-Slit Optical Interference Fringe Spacing

Slit separation $d = 0.250\text{ mm} = 2.5 \times 10^{-4}\text{ m}$, laser wavelength $\lambda = 632.8\text{ nm} = 6.328 \times 10^{-7}\text{ m}$, screen distance $L = 1.80\text{ m}$. Find fringe spacing $\Delta y$:
$$\Delta y = \frac{\lambda L}{d}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_22:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_22:value}}\text{ mm}}$.

In Thomas Young's double-slit interferometer, constructive interference maxima occur where the optical path difference $d\sin\theta = m\lambda$; in the paraxial far-field regime ($\sin\theta \approx \tan\theta = y/L$), adjacent bright fringes are spaced uniformly by $\Delta y = \lambda L / d$. Evaluating the He-Ne laser diffraction product $\lambda L$ (`6.328 {{key:E}} 7 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 1.80 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.1390 \times 10^{-6}$), dividing by slit pitch $d = 2.5 \times 10^{-4}\text{ m}$ (`2.5 {{key:E}} 4 {{key:+/-}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0046$), and scaling to millimeters (`1000 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 4.5562$) resolves fringe spacing $\Delta y \approx \mathbf{4.56\text{ mm}}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.7: Vector Sum of Three Concurrent Forces via `{{key:Σ+}}`

Three coplanar cable forces on a gusset plate:
* $F_1 = 50.0\text{ kN}$ at $\theta_1 = 30.0^\circ$
* $F_2 = 75.0\text{ kN}$ at $\theta_2 = 110.0^\circ$
* $F_3 = 60.0\text{ kN}$ at $\theta_3 = 225.0^\circ$

**Keystrokes:**
1. Clear stats: `{{key:CLΣ}}`
2. Vector 1: `30 {{key:ENTER}} 50 {{key:▸y,x}} {{key:Σ+}}`
3. Vector 2: `110 {{key:ENTER}} 75 {{key:▸y,x}} {{key:Σ+}}`
4. Vector 3: `225 {{key:ENTER}} 60 {{key:▸y,x}} {{key:Σ+}}`
5. Recall sums and resolve:
   `{{key:SUMS}}` $\to \Sigma x = -24.7731\text{ kN}$, $\Sigma y = 53.0506\text{ kN}$
   `{{key:x⇄y}} {{key:▸θ,r}}`

This workflow is one of the crowning architectural idioms of the SC-32: because `{{key:▸y,x}}` deposits $(Y: r\sin\theta, X: r\cos\theta)$ and `{{key:Σ+}}` simultaneously accumulates Register $X$ into $\Sigma x$ and Register $Y$ into $\Sigma y$, you can sum an arbitrary number of 2D vectors without consuming the 4-level stack! Clearing statistical registers (`{{key:CLΣ}}`) and piping each polar cable force through `{{key:▸y,x}} {{key:Σ+}}` accumulates the three gusset loads into hardware memory; recalling the Cartesian totals (`{{key:SUMS}}`, $\Sigma x = -24.7731\text{ kN}, \Sigma y = 53.0506\text{ kN}$) and converting back to polar (`{{key:x⇄y}} {{key:▸θ,r}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 115.01^\circ, X: 58.5444\text{ kN}$) delivers resultant magnitude $R = \mathbf{58.54\text{ kN}}$ at bearing $\theta = \mathbf{115.01^\circ}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.8: Solar Zenith Angle and Air Mass Coefficient

Sun at elevation $\alpha = 42.0^\circ$. Solar zenith angle $\theta_z = 90^\circ - 42^\circ = 48.0^\circ$.

$$\text{Air Mass } AM = \frac{1}{\cos\theta_z}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_24:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_24:value}}} \approx AM\,1.5$.

Solar photovoltaic modules are rated worldwide under the ASTM G173 **AM1.5** terrestrial spectrum—the optical path length of sunlight through a planar atmosphere when the sun sits at zenith angle $\theta_z = 48.19^\circ$ ($\sec 48.19^\circ = 1.50$). Evaluating the zenith cosine (`48 {{key:COS}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.6691$) and inverting in place (`{{key:1/x}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.4945$) yields the secant optical path $\text{AM} \approx \mathbf{1.49}$, confirming nearly exact AM1.5 standard irradiance conditions.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.9: Catenary Hanging Cable Tension at Support Tower

Cable unit weight $w = 12.5\text{ N/m}$, parameter $a = 600.0\text{ m}$, span $L = 400.0\text{ m}$ (half-span $x = 200.0\text{ m}$).

$$T_0 = w \cdot a = 12.5 \times 600 = 7500.0\text{ N}$$
$$T = T_0 \cosh\left(\frac{x}{a}\right) = 7500 \cosh\left(\frac{200}{600}\right) = 7500 \cosh(0.333333)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_25:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_25:value}}\text{ N}} \approx 7.92\text{ kN}$.

Unlike a suspension bridge cable loaded by a flat horizontal roadway (which hangs in a parabola), an unencumbered power line supporting its own distributed weight $w$ hangs in a hyperbolic cosine catenary $y = a\cosh(x/a)$, where horizontal tension $T_0 = wa$ is constant and total tension grows as $T(x) = T_0 \cosh(x/a) = T_0 + w s_{\text{sag}}$. Forming the dimensionless half-span ratio $x/a$ (`200 {{key:ENTER}} 600 {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.3333$), evaluating the hyperbolic cosine (`{{key:HYP}} {{key:COS}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.0561$), and scaling by $T_0 = 7,500\text{ N}$ (`7500 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 7920.5390$) yields tower anchor tension $T \approx \mathbf{7,920.54\text{ N}}$ ($7.92\text{ kN}$).

---

### Workbook Problem 4.10: Polar Conic Equation for Elliptical Satellite Orbit

Semi-latus rectum $p = 7,200.0\text{ km}$, orbital eccentricity $e = 0.150$. Find orbital radius at true anomaly $\theta = 60.0^\circ$.

$$r = \frac{p}{1 + e \cos\theta}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_26:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_26:value}}\text{ km}}$.

Kepler's first law expresses every bound two-body trajectory as a polar conic section $r(\theta) = p / (1 + e\cos\theta)$ measured from perigee ($\theta = 0^\circ$). Evaluating the denominator modulation (`60 {{key:COS}} 0.150 {{key:×}} 1 {{key:+}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.0750$), loading semi-latus rectum $p = 7,200\text{ km}$, and dividing via `7200 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}` (or `{{key:1/x}} 7200 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 6697.6744$) determines the instantaneous geocentric radius $r = \mathbf{6,697.67\text{ km}}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.11: Nautical Waypoint Bearing and Distance

Course from Station A ($x_1=0, y_1=0$) to Station B ($\Delta x = 45.2\text{ nmi}$ East, $\Delta y = 78.5\text{ nmi}$ North).

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_27:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_27:value}}`**

Notice the elegant duality between Cartesian math angles (measured counter-clockwise from East, $\theta_{\text{math}} = \text{atan2}(\Delta y, \Delta x)$) and nautical compass bearings (measured clockwise from True North, $\text{Az} = \text{atan2}(\Delta x, \Delta y)$). Loading northing $\Delta y = 78.5$ into $Y$ and easting $\Delta x = 45.2$ into $X$ and pressing `{{key:▸θ,r}}` (`78.5 {{key:ENTER}} 45.2 {{key:▸θ,r}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 60.0668^\circ, X: 90.5831\text{ nmi}$) delivers rhumb-line distance $r = \mathbf{90.58\text{ nmi}}$ in $X$ and Cartesian angle $60.07^\circ$ in $Y$ (nautical bearing $90^\circ - 60.07^\circ = \mathbf{029.93^\circ}$)—and if you instead enter Easting into $Y$ and Northing into $X$ (`45.2 {{key:ENTER}} 78.5 {{key:▸θ,r}}`), Register $Y$ outputs the true compass azimuth $\mathbf{029.93^\circ}$ directly!

---

### Workbook Problem 4.12: Circular Arch Chord Length and Segment Arc Length

Circular arch of radius $R = 25.0\text{ m}$ spanning central angle $\theta = 70.0^\circ$.

$$\text{Chord } c = 2 R \sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right), \quad \text{Arc } s = R \frac{\theta \pi}{180}$$

**Keystrokes for Chord:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_30:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_30:value}}\text{ m}}$

Historically, ancient astronomers tabulated the chord function $\text{crd}(\theta) = 2R\sin(\theta/2)$ before Indian mathematicians introduced the half-chord *jyā-ardha* ($\sin(\theta/2)$). Computing the arch diameter (`25 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 50.0000$) and multiplying by the half-angle sine (`35 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 28.6788$) resolves the horizontal abutment span $c \approx \mathbf{28.68\text{ m}}$, while the curved masonry extrados arc $s = 25 \times 70\pi/180 = 30.54\text{ m}$ exceeds the chord by $1.86\text{ m}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.13: Brewster's Angle of Polarization

Laser beam incident on crown glass window ($n = 1.520$ in air).

$$\theta_B = \arctan(n)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_32:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_32:value}}^\circ}$.

At Brewster's angle $\theta_B + \theta_2 = 90^\circ$, the oscillating dipole electrons inside the dielectric glass point directly along the reflected ray vector; because Hertzian dipoles cannot radiate along their own oscillation axis, $p$-polarized reflection vanishes completely ($R_p = 0$). Since Snell's law gives $n\sin\theta_B = \sin(90^\circ - \theta_B) = \cos\theta_B$, entering $n = 1.520$ and pressing `{{key:ATAN}}` ($T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 56.6593$) directly yields the lossless laser-window incidence angle $\theta_B \approx \mathbf{56.66^\circ}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.14: Aircraft Coordinated Turn Radius and G-Load

Aircraft turning at $v = 150.0\text{ kts} = 77.1667\text{ m/s}$ at bank angle $\phi = 45.0^\circ$ ($g = 9.80665\text{ m/s}^2$).

$$R = \frac{v^2}{g \tan\phi}, \quad \text{Load Factor } n = \frac{1}{\cos\phi}$$

**Keystrokes for Radius:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_35:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_35:value}}\text{ meters}}$

In a coordinated level turn, aerodynamic lift $L$ must tilt by bank angle $\phi$ so that $L\cos\phi = mg$ supports aircraft weight while $L\sin\phi = mv^2/R$ turns the flight path vector. Squaring true airspeed (`77.1667 {{key:x²}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 5954.6996$) and dividing by $g\tan(45^\circ)$ (`9.80665 {{key:ENTER}} 45 {{key:TAN}} {{key:×}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 607.2104$) yields turn radius $R \approx \mathbf{607.21\text{ m}}$, while the structural load factor $n = L/(mg) = \sec(45^\circ) = \sqrt{2} \approx \mathbf{1.41\,G}$ increases stall speed by $\sqrt{n} \approx 19\%$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.15: Roof Truss Member Forces via Method of Joints

Ridge joint with vertical downward roof load $P = 15.0\text{ kN}$ balanced by two symmetrical rafters inclined at $\theta = 30.0^\circ$ to horizontal.

$$2 F_{\text{rafter}} \sin\theta = P \implies F_{\text{rafter}} = \frac{P}{2 \sin\theta}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_38:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_38:value}}\text{ kN}}$ compression.

At a symmetrical gable ridge pin, each rafter carries half the vertical snow load $P/2 = F_{\text{rafter}}\sin\theta$; because $\sin(30^\circ) = 0.5000$, the vertical projection factor $2\sin(30^\circ)$ equals unity! Loading the ridge point load $P = 15.0\text{ kN}$ (`15 {{key:ENTER}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 15.0000, X: 15.0000$) and dividing by $2\sin(30^\circ)$ (`2 {{key:ENTER}} 30 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 15.0000$) confirms axial rafter compression $F_{\text{rafter}} = \mathbf{15.00\text{ kN}}$ (and keying `30 {{key:COS}} {{key:×}}` immediately gives the $12.99\text{ kN}$ horizontal collar-tie tension).

### Workbook Problem 4.16: Helical Compression Spring Wahl Stress Factor

Spring index $C = D / d = 7.50$.

$$K_W = \frac{4C - 1}{4C - 4} + \frac{0.615}{C}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_39:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_39:value}}`**

In a coiled helical spring, the inner wire fibers have a shorter radius of curvature than the coil centerline and carry both torsional shear and direct transverse shear, elevating inner-wall shear stress by A. M. Wahl's correction factor $K_W$. Computing the curvature numerator $4C - 1 = 29.0$ (`4 {{key:ENTER}} 7.5 {{key:×}} 1 {{key:-}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 29.0000$), dividing by $(4C - 4) = 26.0$ (`4 {{key:ENTER}} 7.5 {{key:×}} 4 {{key:-}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.1154$), and adding the transverse shear term $0.615/C$ (`0.615 {{key:ENTER}} 7.5 {{key:÷}} {{key:+}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.1974$) establishes $K_W = \mathbf{1.1974}$ (nearly $20\%$ higher stress than straight-bar torsion).

### Workbook Problem 4.17: Coriolis Acceleration Vector on Artillery Projectile

Projectile traveling North at $v = 820.0\text{ m/s}$ at latitude $\phi = 42.0^\circ\text{ N}$. Earth's angular rotation rate $\Omega = 7.2921 \times 10^{-5}\text{ rad/s}$:
$$a_{\text{coriolis}} = 2 \cdot v \cdot \Omega \cdot \sin\phi$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_42:keys}}` $\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_42:value}}\text{ m/s}^2}$ Eastward.

In a rotating planetary reference frame, a projectile moving with velocity $\vec{v}$ experiences apparent Coriolis acceleration $\vec{a}_c = -2(\vec{\Omega} \times \vec{v})$, where the local vertical component of Earth's spin vector $\Omega\sin\phi$ deflects Northern Hemisphere trajectories to the right. Forming $2v$ (`2 {{key:ENTER}} 820 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1640.0000$), multiplying by sidereal spin rate $\Omega$ (`7.2921 {{key:E}} 5 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.1196$), and scaling by $\sin(42^\circ)$ (`42 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0800$) yields $a_{\text{coriolis}} \approx \mathbf{0.0800\text{ m/s}^2}$ Eastward—enough to deflect a $30\text{-second}$ artillery shell by $\frac{1}{2}a_c t^2 = 36\text{ meters}$!

### Workbook Problem 4.18: Involute Gear Tooth Base Pitch and Pressure Angle

Module $m = 4.0\text{ mm}$, tooth count $N = 28$, standard pressure angle $\phi = 20.0^\circ$. Calculate pitch circle diameter $D$, base circle diameter $D_b$, and circular base pitch $p_b$:
$$D = m \cdot N, \quad D_b = D \cos\phi, \quad p_b = \pi \cdot m \cos\phi$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_43:keys}}`$\to D = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_43:value}}\text{ mm}}$

By the fundamental law of conjugate gear-tooth action, the common normal at the contact point of two involute profiles always lies tangent to both base circles $D_b = D\cos\phi$ at constant pressure angle $\phi = 20.0^\circ$. Multiplying module by tooth count ($T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 112.0000$) yields pitch diameter $D = \mathbf{112.00\text{ mm}}$; scaling by `20 {{key:COS}} {{key:×}}` gives the involute generating base circle $D_b = \mathbf{105.25\text{ mm}}$, and dividing by `28 {{key:÷}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}}` yields base pitch $p_b = \mathbf{11.81\text{ mm}}$.

### Workbook Problem 4.19: Supersonic Oblique Shock Wave Angle

Supersonic flight at Mach $M_1 = 2.50$ over a compression wedge with deflection angle $\theta = 15.0^\circ$. Determine shock angle $\beta$ (approximate weak shock solution $\beta \approx 36.94^\circ$):
$$\tan\theta = 2 \cot\beta \left[\frac{M_1^2 \sin^2\beta - 1}{M_1^2 (\gamma + \cos(2\beta)) + 2}\right]$$

Across an oblique shock inclined at wave angle $\beta$, tangential velocity is conserved while the normal Mach component $M_{n1} = M_1 \sin\beta$ must exceed unity ($M_{n1} > 1$) to compress the flow. Evaluating the normal Mach number for $\beta = 36.94^\circ$ (`2.50 {{key:ENTER}} 36.94 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.5024$) and forming the excess normal kinetic energy factor $M_{n1}^2 - 1$ (`{{key:x²}} 1 {{key:-}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.2573$) yields the $\theta$-$\beta$-$M$ numerator delta $1.2573$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.20: Wind Turbine Blade Angle of Attack and Relative Velocity

Blade station radius $r = 32.0\text{ m}$, rotational speed $\Omega = 1.80\text{ rad/s}$ ($u_{\text{rot}} = \Omega r = 57.6\text{ m/s}$), free-stream wind speed $v_{\text{wind}} = 11.5\text{ m/s}$ with axial induction factor $a = 0.30$ ($v_{\text{axial}} = 11.5(1 - 0.30) = 8.05\text{ m/s}$):
$$W_{\text{rel}} = \sqrt{u_{\text{rot}}^2 + v_{\text{axial}}^2}, \quad \phi_{\text{flow}} = \arctan\left(\frac{v_{\text{axial}}}{u_{\text{rot}}}\right)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_44:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_44:value}}`**

In blade-element momentum (BEM) theory, an airfoil section at radius $r = 32\text{ m}$ sees the vector sum of the Betz-decelerated axial wind $v_{\text{axial}} = 8.05\text{ m/s}$ and the much faster tangential sweep velocity $u_{\text{rot}} = \Omega r = 57.6\text{ m/s}$. Loading $(Y: v_{\text{axial}}, X: u_{\text{rot}})$ and pressing `{{key:▸θ,r}}` (`8.05 {{key:ENTER}} 57.6 {{key:▸θ,r}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 7.9560^\circ, X: 58.1598\text{ m/s}$) resolves both the relative aerodynamic inflow speed $W_{\text{rel}} \approx \mathbf{58.16\text{ m/s}}$ and the inflow pitch angle $\phi_{\text{flow}} \approx \mathbf{7.96^\circ}$ in a single keystroke.

### Workbook Problem 4.21: Surveying Triangulation Intersection from Two Stations

Baseline between Stations $A$ and $B$ is $b = 1,250.0\text{ m}$. Angles observed to unknown landmark $P$: $\angle A = 58.4^\circ$, $\angle B = 71.2^\circ$. Angle at $P$: $\angle P = 180^\circ - (58.4^\circ + 71.2^\circ) = 50.4^\circ$:
$$\text{Distance } AP = b \frac{\sin B}{\sin P} = 1250 \frac{\sin(71.2^\circ)}{\sin(50.4^\circ)}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_45:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_45:value}}\text{ meters}}$.

Before electronic distance measurement, continental geodetic networks were extended across mountain ranges by measuring a single taped baseline $b$ in a flat valley and sighting horizontal theodolite angles to distant peaks. Scaling baseline $b = 1,250\text{ m}$ by the opposing angle sine (`1250 {{key:ENTER}} 71.2 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1183.3116$) and dividing by the vertex parallax sine $\sin(50.4^\circ)$ (`50.4 {{key:SIN}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1535.7446$) resolves range $AP \approx \mathbf{1,535.74\text{ m}}$—and if you also need range $BP$, `1250 {{key:ENTER}} 58.4 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}} 50.4 {{key:SIN}} {{key:÷}}` completes the fix.

### Workbook Problem 4.22: Celestial Coordinate Conversion (RA/Dec to Alt/Az)

Observer at latitude $\phi = 38.0^\circ\text{ N}$. Star with declination $\delta = 22.5^\circ$ and local hour angle $H = 40.0^\circ$.

$$\sin a = \sin\phi \sin\delta + \cos\phi \cos\delta \cos H$$

**Keystrokes:**
`{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_46:keys}}`

Transforming equatorial star catalog coordinates $(\delta, H)$ into local horizon altitude $a$ for an alt-azimuth telescope mount solves the same spherical cosine law used in marine sextant sight reduction. Computing the polar sine product (`38 {{key:SIN}} 22.5 {{key:SIN}} {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.2356$), adding the meridian hour-angle cosine term (`38 {{key:COS}} 22.5 {{key:COS}} {{key:×}} 40 {{key:COS}} {{key:×}} {{key:+}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.7933$), and extracting the inverse sine (`{{key:ASIN}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 52.4954$) yields horizon altitude $a \approx \mathbf{52.50^\circ}$ (zenith distance $z = 90^\circ - a = 37.50^\circ$).

### Workbook Problem 4.23: Gyroscopic Precession Angular Rate

Rotor mass moment of inertia $I_s = 0.0125\text{ kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$, spin speed $\omega_s = 24,000\text{ rpm} = 2513.27\text{ rad/s}$. Applied disturbance torque $\tau = 0.450\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}$:
$$\Omega_p = \frac{\tau}{I_s \cdot \omega_s}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_47:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_47:value}}\text{ rad/s}} = 0.8207^\circ/\text{s}$.

Because torque equals the time rate of change of angular momentum ($\vec{\tau} = d\vec{L}/dt = \vec{\Omega}_p \times \vec{L}$), a high-speed gyroscope rotor responds to an orthogonal torque $\tau$ by precessing perpendicular to both the spin and torque axes at rate $\Omega_p = \tau / (I_s \omega_s)$. Loading disturbance torque (`0.450 {{key:ENTER}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.4500, X: 0.4500$) and dividing by the spin angular momentum $L = I_s \omega_s = 31.4159\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}\cdot\text{s}$ (`0.0125 {{key:ENTER}} 24000 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 60 {{key:÷}} {{key:×}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.4500, Z: 0.4500, Y: 0.4500, X: 0.0143$) yields precession rate $\Omega_p \approx \mathbf{0.0143\text{ rad/s}}$ ($0.82^\circ/\text{s}$).

---

### Workbook Problem 4.24: Quarter-Wave Plate Retardance and Jones Matrix

Ellipticity angle $\chi$ produced by linear polarizer at angle $\theta = 22.5^\circ$ through quarter-wave plate.

$$\sin(2\chi) = \sin(2\theta) \sin\delta$$
For a quarter-wave plate ($\delta = 90.0^\circ$), $\sin\delta = 1.0$:
$$\chi = \frac{1}{2} \arcsin(\sin(45^\circ)) = \frac{45^\circ}{2} = \mathbf{22.50^\circ}$$.

On the Poincaré polarization sphere, a birefringent retarder with phase delay $\delta$ rotates the Stokes polarization vector about the fast-axis equator by angle $\delta$, mapping azimuth $2\theta$ to latitude $2\chi$ via $\sin(2\chi) = \sin(2\theta)\sin\delta$. Doubling the incident polarizer angle (`22.5 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 45.0000$), applying the quarter-wave round-trip (`{{key:SIN}} {{key:ASIN}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 45.0000$), and halving (`2 {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 22.5000$) confirms ellipticity angle $\chi = \mathbf{22.50^\circ}$ ($\tan\chi = 0.4142$ minor-to-major field ratio).

---

### Workbook Problem 4.25: Acoustic Directivity Index of Circular Piston Speaker

Loudspeaker piston radius $a = 0.10\text{ m}$ radiating at frequency $f = 2,500\text{ Hz}$ in air ($c = 343\text{ m/s}$):

$$k = \frac{2\pi f}{c} = \frac{2\pi(2500)}{343} \approx 45.7958\text{ m}^{-1}$$
$$k a = 45.7958 \times 0.10 = 4.5796$$
Directivity index for $k a \gg 1$:
$$D_I \approx 10 \log_{10}(ka)^2 = 20 \log_{10}(ka)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_48:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_48:value}}\text{ dB}}$.

When the acoustic Helmholtz number $ka = 2\pi a/\lambda$ exceeds unity, a baffled circular loudspeaker cone transitions from an omnidirectional monopole into a directional Airy-Bessel beam whose on-axis intensity gain scales as $(ka)^2$. Entering $ka = 4.5796$, taking the common logarithm, and scaling by $20$ ($T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 13.2166$) yields an on-axis directivity index $D_I \approx \mathbf{13.22\text{ dB}}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.26: Ballistic Reentry Trajectory Flight Path Angle

Atmospheric entry interface velocity $v_E = 7,800\text{ m/s}$, radial velocity $v_r = -210\text{ m/s}$, tangential speed $v_\theta = \sqrt{7800^2 - 210^2} \approx 7797.17\text{ m/s}$. Find flight path entry angle $\gamma$:
$$\sin\gamma = \frac{v_r}{v_E} \implies \gamma = \arcsin\left(\frac{-210}{7800}\right)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_49:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_49:value}}^\circ}$.

Spacecraft atmospheric entry at $120\text{ km}$ altitude requires threading a narrow flight-path corridor: too shallow and the capsule skips back into space; too steep and peak deceleration exceeds crew structural limits. Forming the sink-rate ratio $v_r/v_E$ (`210 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 7800 {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -0.0269$) and extracting the arcsine (`{{key:ASIN}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -1.5428$) establishes the shallow reentry corridor angle $\gamma \approx \mathbf{-1.54^\circ}$ below the local horizontal.

### Workbook Problem 4.27: Circular Roadway Curve Sight Distance

Curve radius $R = 250.0\text{ m}$, obstacle setback distance from centerline $m = 8.5\text{ m}$. Calculate available stopping sight distance $S$:

$$m = R \left(1 - \cos\left(\frac{28.65^\circ S}{R}\right)\right) \implies S = \frac{R}{28.65^\circ} \arccos\left(1 - \frac{m}{R}\right)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_50:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_50:value}}\text{ meters}}$.

In highway geometric design, trees or bridge abutments on the inside of a horizontal curve limit the driver's line-of-sight chord; the middle ordinate setback $m = R(1 - \cos\frac{\theta}{2})$ is the classical **versine** of the half-central angle, where $28.65^\circ \approx \frac{90^\circ}{\pi}$ converts radians to half-angle degrees. Evaluating the inverse-versine argument $1 - m/R$ and taking its arccosine (`8.5 {{key:ENTER}} 250 {{key:÷}} {{key:+/-}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:ACOS}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 14.9836$), then scaling by $R/28.65^\circ$ (`250 {{key:×}} 28.65 {{key:÷}}`—or recalling $R = 250$ from `LASTX` earlier—$T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 130.7467$) determines stopping sight distance $S \approx \mathbf{130.75\text{ m}}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 4.28: Resection Surveying Point Fix (Kaestner Method)

Two known landmarks $L_1 (0, 1000)$ and $L_2 (800, 600)$. Measured subtended angle $\alpha = 42.0^\circ$. Baseline length $b$:

$$b = \sqrt{800^2 + (1000 - 600)^2} = \sqrt{640000 + 160000} = \sqrt{800000} = 894.4272\text{ m}$$
Radius of circumscribing position circle:
$$R_{\text{circ}} = \frac{b}{2 \sin\alpha} = \frac{894.4272}{2 \sin(42^\circ)}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_51:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_51:value}}\text{ meters}}$.

By Euclid's inscribed angle theorem, all observer positions that sight a fixed subtended angle $\alpha$ between two landmarks $L_1$ and $L_2$ lie on a circular arc of radius $R_{\text{circ}} = b / (2\sin\alpha)$ (the Collins auxiliary circle used in three-point resection). Computing the half-baseline chord $b/2$ (`800000 {{key:√x}} 2 {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 447.2136$) and dividing by $\sin(42^\circ)$ (`42 {{key:SIN}} {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 668.3502$) resolves the position-circle radius $R_{\text{circ}} \approx \mathbf{668.35\text{ m}}$.

### Workbook Problem 4.29: Elliptical Polarization Axial Ratio from Phasors

Major ellipse axis $E_{\max} = 12.4\text{ V/m}$, minor axis $E_{\min} = 4.2\text{ V/m}$. Calculate axial ratio in decibels:

$$\text{AR}_{\text{dB}} = 20 \log_{10}\left(\frac{E_{\max}}{E_{\min}}\right)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_52:keys}}`$\to \mathbf{{{res:sc32_04_trig_vectors_polar_seq_52:value}}\text{ dB}}$.

Satellite communication antennas rely on circular polarization ($\text{AR} = 0\text{ dB}$) to prevent Faraday rotation fading through the ionosphere; any cross-polar mismatch elongates the polarization locus into an ellipse quantified by the voltage axial ratio $E_{\max}/E_{\min}$. Forming the semi-axis ratio (`12.4 {{key:ENTER}} 4.2 {{key:÷}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.9524$), taking the base-10 logarithm, and scaling by $20$ (`{{key:LOG}} 20 {{key:×}}`, $T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 9.4034$) yields $\text{AR}_{\text{dB}} \approx \mathbf{9.40\text{ dB}}$.

## Chapter 05: Complex Numbers, AC Phasors & Euler's Identity — Extended Engineering & Competition Problems {#chapter-05}

*Accompanies [Chapter 05 of Thinking in the Stack](../manuals/sc32/05-complex-numbers-phasors.md) (21 Worked Engineering Problems)*

### Workbook Problem 5.1: Principal Value of Natural Logarithm of Negative Real: $\ln(-5)$

In complex analysis, $\ln(z) = \ln|z| + i \operatorname{Arg}(z)$. For $z = -5 + 0i$, $|z| = 5$ and $\operatorname{Arg}(z) = \pi$:

$$\ln(-5) = \ln(5) + i\pi \approx 1.609438 + 3.141593i$$

While the real-valued logarithm `{{key:LN}}` halts on negative inputs, extending $\ln(z)$ to the complex Riemann surface reveals that a negative real number $-5 = 5 e^{i\pi}$ has log-modulus $\ln(5)$ as its real part and the half-turn phase angle $\pi$ radians as its imaginary part. Load $z = -5 + 0i$ into $(Y, X)$ and evaluate the principal complex logarithm via `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_10:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_10:value}}`**, transitioning the stack to $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 1.609438, X: 3.141593)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 5.2: Complex Reciprocal of High-Q Inductor Admittance

An RF inductor has impedance $\mathbf{Z}_L = 0.5 + 120.0i\,\Omega$. Calculate its complex admittance $\mathbf{Y} = 1/\mathbf{Z}$:

$$\mathbf{Y} = \frac{1}{0.5 + 120i} = \frac{0.5 - 120i}{0.5^2 + 120^2} = \frac{0.5 - 120i}{0.25 + 14400} = 3.4722 \times 10^{-5} - 8.3333 \times 10^{-3}i\,\text{S}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_11:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_11:value}}`**

In parallel RF tank circuits, series resistance $R_s = 0.5\,\Omega$ and reactance $X_L = +120\,\Omega$ ($Q = 240$) cannot be inverted term-by-term; complex inversion `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:1/x}}` automatically divides the conjugate $R - jX$ by $|Z|^2 = R^2 + X^2$ using overflow-safe Smith scaling. Load the inductor impedance via `0.5 {{key:ENTER}} 120`, establishing $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0.5, X: 120)$. Press `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:1/x}}` to invert the complex phasor into admittance $\mathbf{Y} = G + jB$ in Siemens, arriving at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 3.4722 \times 10^{-5}, X: -8.3333 \times 10^{-3})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 5.3: Series RLC Circuit Total Impedance

At angular frequency $\omega = 1,000\text{ rad/s}$, a series circuit has $R = 50\,\Omega$, $L = 80\text{ mH}$ ($X_L = \omega L = 80\,\Omega$), and $C = 20\,\mu\text{F}$ ($X_C = 1/(\omega C) = 50\,\Omega$):
$$\mathbf{Z} = R + j(X_L - X_C) = 50 + j(80 - 50) = 50 + 30i\,\Omega$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_12:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_12:value}}`**

In a series RLC branch, inductive reactance $+j\omega L$ (where voltage leads current by $90^\circ$) and capacitive reactance $-j/(\omega C)$ (where voltage lags current by $90^\circ$) directly oppose one another along the imaginary axis. Load the RL branch $\mathbf{Z}_1 = 50 + 80i$ with `50 {{key:ENTER}} 80 {{key:ENTER}}`, then key the capacitor $\mathbf{Z}_2 = 0 - 50i$ with `0 {{key:ENTER}} 50 {{key:+/-}}`, priming the 4-level stack to $(T: 50, Z: 80, Y: 0, X: -50)$. Tap `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:+}}` to execute dyadic phasor addition, leaving $(T: 50, Z: 50, Y: 50.0000, X: 30.0000)$, confirming net inductive impedance **$50.0 + 30.0i\,\Omega$**.

### Workbook Problem 5.4: RLC Circuit Current Phasor via Ohm's Law

The circuit from Workbook Problem 5.3 ($\mathbf{Z} = 50 + 30i\,\Omega$) is driven by voltage $\mathbf{V} = 120 + 0i\text{ V}$. Find current phasor $\mathbf{I} = \mathbf{V} / \mathbf{Z}$:
$$\mathbf{I} = \frac{120}{50 + 30i} = \frac{120(50 - 30i)}{2500 + 900} = \frac{6000 - 3600i}{3400} = 1.7647 - 1.0588i\text{ A}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_13:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_13:value}}`**

If you are continuing directly from Workbook Problem 5.3 with $\mathbf{Z} = 50 + 30i$ already sitting in $(Y, X)$, you can invert and scale without re-entering $\mathbf{Z}$: key `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:1/x}} 120 {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:CMPLX}} {{key:×}}`! Or starting fresh, enter numerator $\mathbf{V}$ via `120 {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:ENTER}}` followed by denominator $\mathbf{Z}$ via `50 {{key:ENTER}} 30`, giving $(T: 120, Z: 0, Y: 50, X: 30)$. Press `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:÷}}` to evaluate complex Ohm's law, yielding lagging current phasor $(T: 120, Z: 120, Y: 1.764706, X: -1.058824)$, confirming **$1.7647 - 1.0588i\text{ Amperes}$** ($|\mathbf{I}| = 2.058\text{ A}\angle -30.96^\circ$).

### Workbook Problem 5.5: AC Apparent, Real, and Reactive Power Complex Vector

Complex apparent power is $\mathbf{S} = \mathbf{V} \cdot \mathbf{I}^*$. For $\mathbf{V} = 120 + 0i\text{ V}$ and $\mathbf{I} = 1.7647 - 1.0588i\text{ A} \implies \mathbf{I}^* = 1.7647 + 1.0588i\text{ A}$:
$$\mathbf{S} = 120 \times (1.7647 + 1.0588i) = 211.7647 + 127.0560i\text{ VA}$$
Real power $P = 211.76\text{ Watts}$, Reactive power $Q = 127.06\text{ VAR}$.

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_14:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_14:value}}`**

Why does AC power engineering define complex power as $\mathbf{S} = \mathbf{V}\mathbf{I}^*$ rather than $\mathbf{V}\mathbf{I}$? Conjugating the current phasor ensures that the phase difference $\theta_v - \theta_i$ is time-invariant, making $+Q = |\mathbf{V}||\mathbf{I}|\sin(\theta_v - \theta_i) > 0$ positive for lagging inductive loads. Continuing directly from Workbook Problem 5.4 where $\mathbf{I}$ sits in $(Y, X)$, simply press `{{key:+/-}}` to conjugate $\mathbf{I} \to \mathbf{I}^*$ in place and multiply by $\mathbf{V}$! From scratch, enter voltage $\mathbf{V}$ via `120 {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:ENTER}}` and conjugate current $\mathbf{I}^*$ via `1.764706 {{key:ENTER}} 1.058824`, loading $(T: 120, Z: 0, Y: 1.764706, X: 1.058824)$. Press `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:×}}` to evaluate $\mathbf{S} = P + jQ$, resulting in $(T: 120, Z: 120, Y: 211.7647, X: 127.0589)$, confirming **$P = 211.76\text{ W}, Q = 127.06\text{ VAR}$**.

### Workbook Problem 5.6: Power Factor from Complex Apparent Power

For complex power $\mathbf{S} = 211.7647 + 127.0589i\text{ VA}$ (in radian mode), compute magnitude $|\mathbf{S}|$ and power factor $\cos\theta$:

$$|\mathbf{S}| = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2} \approx 247.0\text{ VA}, \quad \text{PF} = \frac{P}{|\mathbf{S}|} = \frac{211.7647}{247.0588} \approx 0.8572$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_15:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_15:value}}`**

Bridging `{{key:CMPLX}}` rectangular registers $(Y: \text{Real}, X: \text{Imag})$ to the 2D polar converter `{{key:▸θ,r}}` $(Y: y, X: x)$ requires a single `{{key:x⇄y}}` swap before pressing `{{key:▸θ,r}}`. Key `211.7647 {{key:ENTER}} 127.0589` into $(Y, X)$ (or continue directly from Workbook Problem 5.5's stack!), swap to Euclidean $(Y=Q, X=P)$, and convert to polar form via `{{key:x⇄y}} {{key:▸θ,r}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0.540420, X: 247.0588)$, revealing phase angle $\theta = 0.5404\text{ rad}$ in $Y$ and apparent power $|\mathbf{S}| = 247.06\text{ VA}$ in $X$. Tap `{{key:x⇄y}} {{key:COS}}` to extract the lagging power factor, ending with $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 247.0588, X: 0.857167)$, confirming **$\text{PF} = 0.8572\text{ (lagging)}$** with $|\mathbf{S}|$ preserved in Register $Y$.

### Workbook Problem 5.7: Second Root of Unity from de Moivre

From Textbook Showcase Problem 5.5, the real root is $z_1 = -2.0 + 0.0i$, and the third root is $z_2 = 1.0 - 1.732051i$. Confirm $z_1^3$:
Keystrokes: `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_20:keys}}`$\implies$ **$-8.0000 + 0.0000i$**.

By de Moivre's theorem, the three cube roots of $-8$ sit at the vertices of an equilateral triangle inscribed in the circle $|z| = 2$ at angles $\theta_k = \frac{\pi + 2\pi k}{3} \in \{60^\circ, 180^\circ, 300^\circ\}$. Enter the real root $z_1 = 2 e^{i\pi} = -2.0 + 0.0i$ into $(T, Z)$ and the power $3.0 + 0.0i$ into $(Y, X)$ via `2 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:ENTER}} 0`, creating $(T: -2, Z: 0, Y: 3, X: 0)$. ### Workbook Problem 5.8: Smith Chart Bilinear Transformation

Compute normalized load $z = r + jx$ from reflection coefficient $\Gamma = 0.2 + 0.4i$:

$$z = \frac{1 + \Gamma}{1 - \Gamma} = \frac{1 + 0.2 + 0.4i}{1 - 0.2 - 0.4i} = \frac{1.2 + 0.4i}{0.8 - 0.4i}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_24:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_24:value}}`**

Phillip H. Smith's 1939 transmission-line chart is a conformal Möbius transformation $z = (1+\Gamma)/(1-\Gamma)$ that maps the right-half impedance plane $\text{Re}(z) \ge 0$ onto the interior of the unit reflection disk $|\Gamma| \le 1$, turning circles of constant resistance and reactance into orthogonal families of circles. Load numerator $1 + \Gamma = 1.2 + 0.4i$ via `1.2 {{key:ENTER}} 0.4 {{key:ENTER}}` and denominator $1 - \Gamma = 0.8 - 0.4i$ via `0.8 {{key:ENTER}} 0.4 {{key:+/-}}`, loading $(T: 1.2, Z: 0.4, Y: 0.8, X: -0.4)$. Striking `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:÷}}` evaluates the inverse bilinear mapping to $z = 1.0 + 1.0i$, with the stack resting at $(T: 1.2, Z: 1.2, Y: 1.000000, X: 1.000000)$ (a matched real resistance $R = Z_0$ in series with inductive reactance $X = +Z_0$).

### Workbook Problem 5.9: Acoustic Wave Impedance at Boundary

Characteristic impedance of air $Z_1 = 415\text{ Pa}\cdot\text{s/m}$, porous fiberglass absorber $Z_2 = 600 - 450i\text{ Pa}\cdot\text{s/m}$. Find acoustic reflection coefficient:
$$\Gamma_a = \frac{Z_2 - Z_1}{Z_2 + Z_1} = \frac{(600 - 415) - 450i}{(600 + 415) - 450i} = \frac{185 - 450i}{1015 - 450i}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_26:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_26:value}}`**

Plane sound waves striking an architectural fiberglass treatment obey the exact same Fresnel reflection equation $\Gamma_a = (Z_2 - Z_1)/(Z_2 + Z_1)$ as electromagnetic waves on a transmission line, where the porous absorber's compliance introduces a negative (capacitive) acoustic reactance $-450i\text{ Rayls}$. Key numerator differential impedance $(Z_2 - Z_1) = 185 - 450i$ with `185 {{key:ENTER}} 450 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}}` and denominator $(Z_2 + Z_1) = 1015 - 450i$ with `1015 {{key:ENTER}} 450 {{key:+/-}}`, yielding $(T: 185, Z: -450, Y: 1015, X: -450)$. ### Workbook Problem 5.10: Mutual Inductive Coupling in Transformer

Primary inductance $L_1 = 50\,\mu\text{H}$, secondary $L_2 = 20\,\mu\text{H}$, coupling factor $k = 0.8$. Mutual inductance $M = k\sqrt{L_1 L_2}$:

$$M = 0.8 \sqrt{50 \times 20} = 0.8 \sqrt{1000} \approx 25.2982\,\mu\text{H}$$
Keystrokes: `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_27:keys}}`$\implies$ **$25.2982\,\mu\text{H}$**.

In coupled-coil RF transformers, magnetic flux leakage limits the mutual inductance $M$ to the fraction $k \in [0, 1]$ of the geometric mean $\sqrt{L_1 L_2}$, introducing a mutual transfer impedance $Z_m = j\omega M$ between primary and secondary mesh equations. Enter `50 {{key:ENTER}} 20 {{key:×}}` to form the inductive product $1000\,\mu\text{H}^2$ in $X$ $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 1000)$, take the geometric mean with `{{key:√x}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 31.6228)$, and scale by coupling coefficient with `0.8 {{key:×}}`, resolving $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 25.2982)$.

### Workbook Problem 5.11: Waveguide Attenuation and Propagation Constant ($\gamma$)

Propagation constant $\gamma = \alpha + j\beta$. In cutoff band: $\alpha = 14.5\text{ Np/m}, \beta = 0\text{ rad/m}$. Attenuation in decibels per meter:

$$\text{Atten} = 20 \log_{10}(e) \times \alpha = 8.68589 \times 14.5 = \mathbf{125.945\text{ dB/m}}$$

Below a rectangular waveguide's cutoff frequency $f_c$, the propagation constant $\gamma = \sqrt{k_c^2 - k^2} = \alpha + j0$ becomes purely real: rather than propagating as a traveling wave, the field decays evanescently as $e^{-\alpha z}$ at $20\log_{10}(e) \approx 8.68589\text{ dB}$ per Neper (the basis of waveguide-below-cutoff precision attenuators). Key `1 {{key:eˣ}} {{key:LOG}} 20 {{key:×}}` to generate the exact Nepers-to-dB constant ($8.685890\text{ dB/Np}$) on the stack $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 8.685890)$, then multiply by $\alpha = 14.5\text{ Np/m}$ with `14.5 {{key:×}}`, resolving total attenuation $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 125.945405)$, confirming **$125.945\text{ dB/m}$**.

### Workbook Problem 5.12: Complex Impedance of Parallel RLC Tank at Resonance

Tank circuit with $R = 10\text{ k}\Omega, L = 10\,\mu\text{H}, C = 100\text{ pF}$. Resonant frequency $\omega_0 = 1/\sqrt{LC} = 31.6228\text{ Mrad/s}$. At resonance, reactive admittances cancel ($j\omega C + 1/(j\omega L) = 0$), leaving pure resistance:
$$\mathbf{Z}_{\text{tank}} = R = 10,000.0 + 0.0i\,\Omega$$

In a parallel RLC bandpass resonator at $\omega_0 = 1/\sqrt{LC}$, energy sloshes losslessly between the magnetic field of the inductor and the electric field of the capacitor with equal and opposite susceptances ($\pm j\sqrt{C/L} = \pm j\,3.1623\text{ mS}$), so the parallel tank presents a maximum, purely resistive impedance $\mathbf{Z}_{\text{tank}} = R$ to the driving RF stage. Evaluate the reactive product with `10 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 100 {{key:E}} 12 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 1.0 \times 10^{-15})$, extract the resonant angular frequency with `{{key:√x}} {{key:1/x}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 3.1623 \times 10^7)$, then load the resonant tank impedance `10000 {{key:ENTER}} 0` across $(Y, X)$, confirming purely resistive impedance $(T: 0, Z: 3.1623 \times 10^7, Y: 10000.0000, X: 0.0000)$ of **$10,000.0 + 0.0i\,\Omega$**.

### Workbook Problem 5.13: Complex Root Extraction via de Moivre ($z^4 = -16$)

Roots of $z^4 = -16 = 16 e^{i\pi}$: $r = 16^{1/4} = 2.0$. First root $\theta = \pi/4 = 45^\circ$:

$$z_0 = 2(\cos 45^\circ + i\sin 45^\circ) = \sqrt{2} + i\sqrt{2} \approx 1.414214 + 1.414214i$$
Keystrokes: `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_28:keys}}`$\implies$ **$1.414214$ in $X$ and $Y$**.

The four fourth roots of $-16$ form the $45^\circ$-rotated square vertices $2 e^{i(\pi/4 + k\pi/2)}$—the exact pole pattern of a 2nd-order Butterworth filter! Key the polar angle and radial modulus `45 {{key:ENTER}} 2` into $(Y, X)$ $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 45, X: 2)$, then convert from polar to rectangular via `{{key:▸y,x}}`, extracting principal root $z_0 = 1.414214 + 1.414214i$ across $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 1.414214, X: 1.414214)$.

### Workbook Problem 5.14: Skin Effect in Solid Copper Conductor

Skin depth $\delta = \sqrt{2/(\omega \mu \sigma)}$. For copper ($\sigma = 5.8 \times 10^7\text{ S/m}$) at $f = 10\text{ MHz}$ ($\omega = 6.283185 \times 10^7\text{ rad/s}$):
$$\delta = \sqrt{\frac{2}{6.283185\cdot 10^7 \times 4\pi\cdot 10^{-7} \times 5.8\cdot 10^7}} \approx 2.089 \times 10^{-5}\text{ m} = \mathbf{20.89\,\mu\text{m}}$$

At radio frequencies, alternating magnetic flux inside a conductor induces eddy currents that oppose current flow in the core and crowd the current density $J(r) = J_0 e^{-(1+j)d/\delta}$ into a thin surface annulus of thickness $\delta$. Form free-space permeability $\mu_0$ with `4 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 1 {{key:E}} 7 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 1.2566 \times 10^{-6})$, multiply by angular frequency and conductivity `6.283185 {{key:E}} 7 {{key:×}} 5.8 {{key:E}} 7 {{key:×}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 4.5795 \times 10^9)$, invert into ratio $2/(\omega\mu\sigma)$ with `2 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 4.3639 \times 10^{-10})$, and take the square root `{{key:√x}}`, arriving at skin depth $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 2.0890 \times 10^{-5})$, confirming **$\delta = 20.89\,\mu\text{m}$**.

### Workbook Problem 5.15: Dispersion Relation in Fiber Optics (Group Velocity Dispersion)

Complex propagation constant $\beta(\omega) = \beta_0 + \beta_1(\omega - \omega_0) + \frac{1}{2}\beta_2(\omega - \omega_0)^2$. Pulse dispersion parameter $D = -2\pi c \beta_2 / \lambda^2$. For $\beta_2 = -20\text{ ps}^2/\text{km}$ at $\lambda = 1.55\,\mu\text{m}$:
$$D = \frac{-2\pi(3\cdot 10^8)(-20\cdot 10^{-27})}{(1.55\cdot 10^{-6})^2} \approx \mathbf{15.68\text{ ps/(nm}\cdot\text{km)}}$$

In single-mode silica telecom fiber operating in the $1550\text{ nm}$ C-band, negative group-velocity dispersion ($\beta_2 = -20\text{ ps}^2/\text{km}$, the anomalous dispersion regime where optical solitons form) causes shorter-wavelength blue spectral components to travel faster than red components by $D \approx 15.68\text{ ps}$ per nanometer of spectral width per kilometer. Compute numerator $2\pi c |\beta_2|$ with `2 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 3 {{key:E}} 8 {{key:×}} 20 {{key:E}} 27 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 3.7699 \times 10^{-17})$, square wavelength with `1.55 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:+/-}} {{key:x²}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 3.7699 \times 10^{-17}, X: 2.4025 \times 10^{-12})$, and divide and scale via `{{key:÷}} 1 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:×}}`, arriving at dispersion parameter $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 15.6841)$, confirming **$15.68\text{ ps/(nm}\cdot\text{km)}$**.

### Workbook Problem 5.16: Fractional Pole Filter: $s^{0.5}$ Fractional Calculus Impedance

Warburg impedance in electrochemistry: $\mathbf{Z}_W = \frac{A_W}{\sqrt{\omega}} (1 - j)$.
For $A_W = 200\,\Omega\cdot\text{s}^{-1/2}$ at $\omega = 100\text{ rad/s}$:
$$\mathbf{Z}_W = \frac{200}{10} (1 - j) = \mathbf{20.0000 - 20.0000i\,\Omega}$$

In electrochemical impedance spectroscopy (EIS) of lithium-ion battery electrodes, semi-infinite Fickian ion diffusion obeys a half-order fractional differential equation whose Laplace transform $(j\omega)^{-1/2} = \frac{1-j}{\sqrt{2\omega}}$ produces a constant $-45^\circ$ phase line on a Nyquist plot (the Warburg element). Evaluate $\sqrt{\omega}$ with `100 {{key:√x}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 10)$, compute scale factor $A_W/\sqrt{\omega} = 20\,\Omega$ with `200 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 20)$, and duplicate and negate (`{{key:ENTER}} {{key:+/-}}` or `20 {{key:ENTER}} 20 {{key:+/-}}`), confirming Warburg impedance $(T: 20.0000, Z: -20.0000, Y: 20.0000, X: -20.0000)$.

### Workbook Problem 5.17: Complex Poynting Vector & Electromagnetic Radiation Density

$\mathbf{S}_{\text{cpx}} = \frac{1}{2} \mathbf{E} \times \mathbf{H}^*$. For $\mathbf{E} = 10.0 + 5.0i\text{ V/m}$ and $\mathbf{H} = 0.02 + 0.01i\text{ A/m}$:
$$\mathbf{H}^* = 0.02 - 0.01i \implies \mathbf{E} \cdot \mathbf{H}^* = (10+5i)(0.02-0.01i) = (0.2 + 0.05) + (-0.1 + 0.1)i = 0.25 + 0i$$
Average radiated power: $\text{Re}(\mathbf{S}_{\text{cpx}}) = 0.5 \times 0.25 = \mathbf{0.125\text{ W/m}^2}$.

The complex Poynting theorem separates electromagnetic energy flux into time-averaged radiated power density $\text{Re}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{E} \times \mathbf{H}^*)$ and reactive near-field stored energy density $\text{Im}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{E} \times \mathbf{H}^*)$. Because $\mathbf{E}$ and $\mathbf{H}$ here share the exact same phase angle $\arctan(0.5) = 26.57^\circ$ (with wave impedance $E/H = 500\,\Omega$), their cross-product $\mathbf{E}\mathbf{H}^*$ is purely real! Load electric field $\mathbf{E}$ via `10 {{key:ENTER}} 5 {{key:ENTER}}` and conjugate magnetic field $\mathbf{H}^*$ via `0.02 {{key:ENTER}} 0.01 {{key:+/-}}`, placing $(T: 10, Z: 5, Y: 0.02, X: -0.01)$. Execute `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:×}}` to obtain $0.25 + 0i$ $(T: 10, Z: 10, Y: 0.25, X: 0)$, swap the real part into Register $X$, and divide by 2 with `{{key:x⇄y}} 2 {{key:÷}}`, giving average radiation density $(T: 10, Z: 10, Y: 0, X: 0.1250)$, confirming **$0.125\text{ W/m}^2$**.

### Workbook Problem 5.18: Sagnac Ring Interferometer Phase Shift

Fiber optic gyroscope: $\Delta\Phi = \frac{8\pi A N}{c \lambda} \Omega$. For coil area $A = 0.01\text{ m}^2$, $N = 1000\text{ turns}$, $\lambda = 1.31\,\mu\text{m}$, rotation rate $\Omega = 1.0\text{ rad/s}$:
$$\Delta\Phi = \frac{8\pi(0.01)(1000)}{(3\cdot 10^8)(1.31\cdot 10^{-6})} \approx \mathbf{0.6395\text{ radians}}$$

In Georges Sagnac's relativistic ring interferometer, counter-propagating laser beams traversing a rotating fiber coil of total enclosed area $N A = 10\text{ m}^2$ experience a non-reciprocal transit-time difference $\Delta t = 4AN\Omega/c^2$, shifting their interference phasor $e^{i\Delta\Phi}$ by $\Delta\Phi = \frac{8\pi AN}{c\lambda}\Omega$. Form the optical area-turns product with `8 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 0.01 {{key:×}} 1000 {{key:×}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 251.3274)$, compute $c\lambda$ with `3 {{key:E}} 8 {{key:ENTER}} 1.31 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 251.3274, X: 393.0000)$, and divide with `{{key:÷}}`, resolving Sagnac phase shift $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 0.6395)$.

### Workbook Problem 5.19: Transformer T-Model Core Loss Branch

Parallel magnetizing branch: $R_c = 50\text{ k}\Omega \parallel X_m = 15\text{ k}\Omega$. Total complex excitation admittance:

Impedance: $\mathbf{Z}_m = 1/\mathbf{Y}_m = \mathbf{4,128.44 + 13,761.47i\,\Omega}$.

In a power transformer's Steinmetz equivalent circuit, eddy-current/hysteresis core loss is modeled by shunt resistance $R_c = 50\text{ k}\Omega$ in parallel with magnetizing reactance $+j X_m = +j\,15\text{ k}\Omega$. Converting this parallel excitation branch into its equivalent series impedance $\mathbf{Z}_m = R_s + j X_s$ is effortless on the stack: key core conductance `50000 {{key:1/x}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 2.0 \times 10^{-5})$ and inductive susceptance `15000 {{key:1/x}} {{key:+/-}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 2.0 \times 10^{-5}, X: -6.6667 \times 10^{-5})$, then take the complex reciprocal with `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:1/x}}`, arriving at equivalent branch impedance $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 4128.4404, X: 13761.4679)$, confirming **$4,128.44 + 13,761.47i\,\Omega$** (or use `50000 {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:ENTER}} 15000 {{key:CMPLX}} {{key:x∥y}}` directly!).

When two orthogonal dipole elements are driven in quadrature ($90^\circ$ phase offset) with unequal field amplitudes $E_x = 10\text{ V/m}$ and $E_y = 6\text{ V/m}$, the tip of the electric field vector traces an upright polarization ellipse of axial ratio $a/b = 10/6$. **Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_29:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_05_complex_numbers_phasors_seq_29:value}}`**

This closing sprint links Euler's exponential map $e^{i\theta} = \cos\theta + i\sin\theta$ directly to de Moivre root extraction on the live stack. Enter the pure imaginary exponent $0 + i(\pi/2)$ via `0 {{key:π}} 2 {{key:÷}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 1.570796)$, evaluate the complex exponential with `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:eˣ}}` $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0.000000, X: 1.000000)$, and extract the principal complex square root with `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:√x}}`, producing $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0.707107, X: 0.707107)$ in 4 seconds flat!

Stack outputs: **$Y = 0.707107$**, **$X = 0.707107$** in 4 seconds flat!

## Chapter 06: Analytic & Solid Geometry, Surface Area & Mensuration — Extended Engineering & Competition Problems {#chapter-06}

*Accompanies [Chapter 06 of Thinking in the Stack](../manuals/sc32/06-geometry-surface-area.md) (15 Worked Engineering Problems)*

### Workbook Problem 6.1: Elliptical Pressure Vessel Manway Area & Perimeter

A vessel inspection hatch is elliptical with semimajor axis $a = 250.0\text{ mm}$ and semiminor axis $b = 150.0\text{ mm}$.
1. Area $A = \pi a b = \pi \times 250 \times 150 = 37,500\pi \approx \mathbf{117,809.72\text{ mm}^2}$
2. Ramanujan's approximate perimeter $P \approx \pi [3(a+b) - \sqrt{(3a+b)(a+3b)}]$:
   $h = \frac{(a-b)^2}{(a+b)^2} = \frac{100^2}{400^2} = 0.0625 \implies P \approx \pi(a+b)(1 + \frac{3h}{10 + \sqrt{4 - 3h}}) \approx \mathbf{1,276.26\text{ mm}}$

**Keystrokes (Area):** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_03:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_03:value}}`**

Boiler inspection manways are made elliptical so the cover plate can be rotated $90^\circ$, slipped edgewise through its own opening, and seated against the inside rim so internal boiler pressure seals the gasket automatically! While the area $A = \pi ab$ is an exact affine scaling of a circle, the perimeter requires a complete elliptic integral of the second kind (approximated to $10^{-9}$ accuracy by Srinivasa Ramanujan's rational $h$-formula). Push semimajor axis $a = 250$ via `250 {{key:ENTER}}`, multiply by semiminor axis $b = 150$ via `150 {{key:×}}` to obtain planar product $37,500.0$, and apply `{{key:π}} {{key:×}}` to resolve exact manway opening area at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 117809.7245)$, confirming **$117,809.72\text{ mm}^2$** ($1,178.10\text{ cm}^2$).

---

### Workbook Problem 6.2: Torus Volume and Surface Area (Hydraulic O-Ring)

An elastomer O-ring has major mean radius $R = 45.0\text{ mm}$ and cross-sectional circular radius $r = 3.5\text{ mm}$.
1. Volume $V = 2\pi^2 R r^2 = 2 \pi^2 (45) (3.5^2) = 2 \pi^2 (45) (12.25) \approx \mathbf{10,880.89\text{ mm}^3}$
2. Surface Area $A = 4\pi^2 R r = 4 \pi^2 (45) (3.5) \approx \mathbf{6,217.85\text{ mm}^2}$

**Keystrokes (Volume):** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_05:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_05:value}}`**

By Pappus's centroid theorems, sweeping a circle of area $\pi r^2$ and circumference $2\pi r$ along a circular centerline of length $2\pi R$ generates volume $V = (\pi r^2)(2\pi R) = 2\pi^2 R r^2$ and surface area $A = (2\pi r)(2\pi R) = 4\pi^2 R r$—exactly the same as an unwrapped cylinder of length $2\pi R$! Evaluate $2\pi^2$ via `{{key:π}} {{key:x²}} 2 {{key:×}}`, scale by major centerline radius $R = 45\text{ mm}$ via `45 {{key:×}}`, and multiply by cross-sectional radius squared $r^2 = 3.5^2$ via `3.5 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}}`, establishing torus body volume at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 10880.8872)$.

**Keystrokes (Surface Area):** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_06:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_06:value}}`**

Notice that $A / V = 2 / r$: if you already have $V = 10,880.89\text{ mm}^3$ in Register $X$, keying `2 {{key:×}} 3.5 {{key:÷}}` yields $A = 6,217.85\text{ mm}^2$ in three keystrokes! Or from scratch, compute the $4\pi^2$ envelope constant via `{{key:π}} {{key:x²}} 4 {{key:×}}`, then chain major and minor radii via `45 {{key:×}} 3.5 {{key:×}}` to yield total contact surface area at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 6217.8508)$, confirming **$V = 10,880.89\text{ mm}^3$**, **$A = 6,217.85\text{ mm}^2$**.

### Workbook Problem 6.3: Truncated Pyramidal Monument Volume

A stone pedestal is a truncated square pyramid: lower base side $a = 3.20\text{ m}$, upper base side $b = 1.80\text{ m}$, height $h = 4.50\text{ m}$.
Base areas: $A_1 = 3.2^2 = 10.24\text{ m}^2$, $A_2 = 1.8^2 = 3.24\text{ m}^2$:
$$V = \frac{h}{3} (A_1 + A_2 + \sqrt{A_1 A_2}) = \frac{4.5}{3} (10.24 + 3.24 + \sqrt{33.1776}) = 1.5 \times (13.48 + 5.76) = 1.5 \times 19.24 = \mathbf{28.86\text{ m}^3}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_08:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_08:value}}`**

Recorded in the 1850 BCE Egyptian *Moscow Mathematical Papyrus* (Problem 14), the truncated square pyramid formula $V = \frac{h}{3}(a^2 + ab + b^2) = \frac{h}{3}(A_1 + A_2 + \sqrt{A_1 A_2})$ is one of the oldest jewels of solid geometry. Store lower base $A_1 = 10.24$ into Register 1 via `3.2 {{key:x²}} {{key:STO}} 1`, and upper base $A_2 = 3.24$ into Register 2 via `1.8 {{key:x²}} {{key:STO}} 2`. Add them via `{{key:+}}` for sum $13.48$. Compute geometric mean $\sqrt{A_1 A_2} = 5.76$ via `{{key:RCL}} 1 {{key:RCL}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:√x}}`, sum with base areas via `{{key:+}}`, and multiply by $h/3$ via `4.5 {{key:×}} 3 {{key:÷}}`, yielding pedestal volume at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 28.8600)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 6.4: Regular Icosahedron Dice Volume and Area

A 20-sided gaming die has edge length $a = 18.0\text{ mm}$.
1. Surface Area $A = 5\sqrt{3} a^2 = 5 \sqrt{3} (18^2) = 5\sqrt{3}(324) \approx \mathbf{2,805.9223\text{ mm}^2}$
2. Volume $V = \frac{5(3 + \sqrt{5})}{12} a^3 = \frac{5(5.236068)}{12} (5832) \approx \mathbf{12,723.65\text{ mm}^3}$

**Keystrokes (Volume):** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_09:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_09:value}}`**

The vertices of a regular icosahedron lie on three mutually orthogonal golden rectangles of aspect ratio $\varphi = (1+\sqrt{5})/2$, so its volume constant $\frac{5(3+\sqrt{5})}{12} = \frac{5\varphi^2}{6}$ is governed directly by the square of the golden ratio! Evaluate icosahedral volume factor $\frac{5(3+\sqrt{5})}{12} \approx 2.181695$ via `5 {{key:√x}} 3 {{key:+}} 5 {{key:×}} 12 {{key:÷}}`. Cube die edge $a = 18\text{ mm}$ via `18 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:yˣ}}` and multiply via `{{key:×}}`, arriving at total polyhedron volume at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 12723.6452)$, confirming **$V = 12,723.65\text{ mm}^3$**, **$A = 2,805.92\text{ mm}^2$**.

---

### Workbook Problem 6.5: Regular Dodecahedron Volume

12-sided solid with edge $a = 12.0\text{ mm}$:

$$V = \frac{15 + 7\sqrt{5}}{4} a^3 \approx \frac{15 + 15.652476}{4} (1728) = 7.663119 \times 1728 = \mathbf{13,241.87\text{ mm}^3}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_10:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_10:value}}`**

Dual to the icosahedron, the regular dodecahedron can be constructed by erecting roof-like "tents" on the six faces of an inscribed cube of edge $\varphi a$, giving volume constant $\frac{15+7\sqrt{5}}{4} \approx 7.663119$. Compute this constant via `5 {{key:√x}} 7 {{key:×}} 15 {{key:+}} 4 {{key:÷}}`, cube facet edge $a = 12\text{ mm}$ via `12 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:yˣ}}`, and multiply with `{{key:×}}`, yielding $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 13241.8694)$, confirming **$13,241.87\text{ mm}^3$**.

### Workbook Problem 6.6: 3D Euclidean Distance Between Aerospace Telemetry Nodes

Station $A$ at coordinates $(1250, -420, 850)\text{ m}$, Station $B$ at $(2100, 680, 1400)\text{ m}$:

$$\Delta x = 2100 - 1250 = 850\text{ m}, \quad \Delta y = 680 - (-420) = 1100\text{ m}, \quad \Delta z = 1400 - 850 = 550\text{ m}$$
$$d = \sqrt{850^2 + 1100^2 + 550^2} = \sqrt{722500 + 1210000 + 302500} = \sqrt{2235000} \approx \mathbf{1,494.9916\text{ meters}}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_12:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_12:value}}`**

Computing a 3D Euclidean norm $\sqrt{\Delta x^2 + \Delta y^2 + \Delta z^2}$ on the stack never requires more than two active registers when each coordinate difference is squared and added immediately to the running sum of squares in Register $Y$. Evaluate $\Delta x^2 = 850^2 = 722,500$ via `2100 {{key:ENTER}} 1250 {{key:-}} {{key:x²}}`, accumulate $\Delta y^2 = 1100^2 = 1,210,000$ with `680 {{key:ENTER}} 420 {{key:+/-}} {{key:-}} {{key:x²}} {{key:+}}`, and add $\Delta z^2 = 550^2 = 302,500$ via `1400 {{key:ENTER}} 850 {{key:-}} {{key:x²}} {{key:+}}`. Extract the square root via `{{key:√x}}` to resolve 3D spatial separation at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 1494.9916)$, confirming **$1,494.99\text{ meters}$**.

### Workbook Problem 6.7: Vector Cross Product in 3-Space ($\vec{A} \times \vec{B}$)

$\vec{A} = (3, -2, 4)$, $\vec{B} = (1, 5, -2)$:

$$\vec{C} = \vec{A} \times \vec{B} = (A_y B_z - A_z B_y)\hat{i} + (A_z B_x - A_x B_z)\hat{j} + (A_x B_y - A_y B_x)\hat{k}$$
* $C_x = (-2)(-2) - (4)(5) = 4 - 20 = \mathbf{-16}$
* $C_y = (4)(1) - (3)(-2) = 4 - (-6) = \mathbf{10}$
* $C_z = (3)(5) - (-2)(1) = 15 - (-2) = \mathbf{17}$
$$\vec{C} = (-16, 10, 17) \implies |\vec{C}| = \sqrt{256 + 100 + 289} = \sqrt{645} \approx \mathbf{25.3969}$$

**Keystrokes (Magnitude):** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_14:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_14:value}}`**

Geometrically, the cross product $\vec{C} = \vec{A} \times \vec{B}$ is normal to the plane spanned by $\vec{A}$ and $\vec{B}$, and its Euclidean magnitude $|\vec{C}| = |\vec{A}||\vec{B}|\sin\theta = \sqrt{645}$ equals the area of the 3D parallelogram formed by the two vectors (and half of $|\vec{C}|$ is the area of the spatial triangle). Square each Cartesian cross-product component sequentially: `16 {{key:x²}} 10 {{key:x²}} {{key:+}} 17 {{key:x²}} {{key:+}}` produces sum of squares $645.0$ in Register $X$. Taking the square root via `{{key:√x}}` delivers vector magnitude $|\vec{C}|$ at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 25.3969)$, confirming **$\vec{C} = (-16, 10, 17), |\vec{C}| = 25.40$**.

---

### Workbook Problem 6.8: Dihedral Angle Between Two Intersecting Planes

Plane 1: $2x + y - 2z = 5 \implies \vec{n}_1 = (2, 1, -2)$. Plane 2: $x - 2y + 2z = 3 \implies \vec{n}_2 = (1, -2, 2)$:

$$\cos\theta = \frac{|\vec{n}_1 \cdot \vec{n}_2|}{|\vec{n}_1| |\vec{n}_2|} = \frac{|(2)(1) + (1)(-2) + (-2)(2)|}{\sqrt{4+1+4} \sqrt{1+4+4}} = \frac{|2 - 2 - 4|}{3 \times 3} = \frac{4}{9} \approx 0.444444$$
$$\theta = \arccos(4/9) \approx \mathbf{63.6122^\circ}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_15:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_15:value}}`**

The acute dihedral fold angle $\theta$ between two intersecting sheet-metal or roof planes equals the angle between their outward surface normals $\vec{n}_1$ and $\vec{n}_2$ (which here are both Pythagorean triples of length $\sqrt{2^2 + 1^2 + 2^2} = 3$). Evaluate cosine quotient $4/9 \approx 0.444444$ via `4 {{key:ENTER}} 9 {{key:÷}}`. Taking inverse cosine via `{{key:ACOS}}` extracts the dihedral intersection angle in degrees, settling the stack at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 63.6122)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 6.9: Radius of Gyration ($k$) of Rotating Turbine Disk

Rotor disk with moment of inertia $I = 14.8\text{ kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$ and mass $M = 42.0\text{ kg}$:

$$k = \sqrt{\frac{I}{M}} = \sqrt{\frac{14.8}{42.0}} = \sqrt{0.352381} = \mathbf{0.593617\text{ meters}} = 593.6\text{ mm}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_18:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_06_geometry_surface_area_seq_18:value}}`**

The radius of gyration $k = \sqrt{I/M}$ is the radial distance at which the entire mass $M$ of a complex profiled turbine rotor could be concentrated into an infinitesimally thin hoop ($I = M k^2$) without altering its rotational kinetic energy $\frac{1}{2}I\omega^2$. Divide moment of inertia $I = 14.8$ by mass $M = 42.0$ via `14.8 {{key:ENTER}} 42 {{key:÷}}` to form $k^2 = 0.352381\text{ m}^2$. Extracting the square root via `{{key:√x}}` gives radius of gyration $k$ in meters at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 0.593617)$, confirming **$k = 0.5936\text{ m}$** ($593.6\text{ mm}$).

---

### Workbook Problem 6.10: Parabolic Solar Reflector Surface Area

Deep parabolic dish $z = \frac{x^2 + y^2}{4f}$ with focal length $f = 1.20\text{ m}$ and rim radius $R = 2.0\text{ m}$. Surface area of revolution:

$$A = \frac{8\pi f^2}{3} \left[ \left(1 + \frac{R^2}{4f^2}\right)^{3/2} - 1 \right]$$
For $f = 1.2, R = 2.0$: $\frac{R^2}{4f^2} = \frac{4}{4(1.44)} = \frac{1}{1.44} \approx 0.694444$.
$1 + 0.694444 = 1.694444 \implies (1.694444)^{1.5} \approx 2.205739$:
$$A = \frac{8\pi(1.44)}{3} [2.205739 - 1] = 3.84\pi \times 1.205739 \approx \mathbf{14.5458\text{ m}^2}$$

Integrating the surface-of-revolution element $2\pi r\sqrt{1 + (dz/dr)^2}\,dr$ for a paraboloid $z = r^2/(4f)$ yields an exact $u$-substitution in $u = 1 + r^2/(4f^2)$ that requires no elliptic integrals. Evaluate base factor $1 + R^2/(4f^2) \approx 1.694444$ via `2 {{key:x²}} 4 {{key:÷}} 1.2 {{key:x²}} {{key:÷}} 1 {{key:+}}`. Raise to power $1.5$ and subtract unity via `1.5 {{key:yˣ}} 1 {{key:-}}` to obtain $1.205739$. Form leading coefficient $\frac{8\pi f^2}{3} \approx 12.063716$ via `8 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 1.2 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}} 3 {{key:÷}}` and multiply via `{{key:×}}` to resolve reflector mirror area at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 14.5458)$ (compared to flat aperture $\pi R^2 = 12.5664\text{ m}^2$).

---

### Workbook Problem 6.11: Hydraulic Mean Depth & Wetted Perimeter

Trapezoidal irrigation canal: bottom width $b = 4.0\text{ m}$, water depth $y = 1.8\text{ m}$, side slope $z = 1.5$ ($1.5\text{ H} : 1\text{ V}$):
* Flow area $A = (b + z y) y = (4.0 + 1.5 \times 1.8) \times 1.8 = (4.0 + 2.7) \times 1.8 = 6.7 \times 1.8 = \mathbf{12.06\text{ m}^2}$
* Wetted perimeter $P = b + 2 y \sqrt{1 + z^2} = 4.0 + 2(1.8)\sqrt{1 + 2.25} = 4.0 + 3.6\sqrt{3.25} \approx 4.0 + 6.49 = \mathbf{10.4892\text{ m}}$
* Hydraulic radius $R_h = A / P = 12.06 / 10.4892 = \mathbf{1.1498\text{ meters}}$

In open-channel hydraulics, the hydraulic radius $R_h = A/P$ measures the ratio of gravity-driven cross-sectional flow area $A$ to boundary-shear wetted perimeter $P$, driving Manning's velocity $V = \frac{1}{n} R_h^{2/3} S^{1/2}$. Evaluate flow area $A = (b+zy)y = 12.06\text{ m}^2$ with `1.5 {{key:ENTER}} 1.8 {{key:×}} 4 {{key:+}} 1.8 {{key:×}}`, then compute wetted perimeter $P = b + 2y\sqrt{1+z^2} \approx 10.4892\text{ m}$ inside-out via `1.5 {{key:x²}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:√x}} 2 {{key:×}} 1.8 {{key:×}} 4 {{key:+}}`, using only two active stack registers so $A = 12.06$ stays safely in Register $Y$ while $P$ forms in Register $X$. Pressing `{{key:÷}}` divides the two directly to establish hydraulic radius at $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.1498)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 6.12: Circular Segment Area (Partially Filled Pipe)

Storm sewer diameter $D = 1.20\text{ m}$ ($r = 0.60\text{ m}$). Water depth $d = 0.40\text{ m}$. Subtended angle:

$$\theta = 2 \arccos\left(\frac{r - d}{r}\right) = 2 \arccos\left(\frac{0.2}{0.6}\right) = 2 \arccos(0.333333) = 2(70.5288^\circ) = 141.0575^\circ = 2.461914\text{ rad}$$
$$\text{Area } A = \frac{1}{2} r^2 (\theta - \sin\theta) = 0.5(0.36)(2.461914 - \sin(141.0575^\circ)) = 0.18(2.461914 - 0.628539) = 0.18(1.833375) = \mathbf{0.330008\text{ m}^2}$$

Notice how stack duplication eliminates re-typing the pipe radius $r = 0.60\text{ m}$ and subtended angle $\theta$: duplicating $r = 0.60$ via `0.6 {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}} 0.4 {{key:-}} {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}} {{key:ACOS}} 2 {{key:×}}` determines subtended angle $\theta = 141.0576^\circ$ while typing $0.6$ once (or work directly in {{key:RAD}} mode where $\theta - \sin\theta$ requires no $\pi/180$ conversion!). In {{key:DEG}} mode, duplicate $\theta$ into Register $Y$ and convert Register $X$ to radians via `{{key:ENTER}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 180 {{key:÷}}`, subtract $\sin\theta$ via `{{key:x⇄y}} {{key:SIN}} {{key:-}}` to obtain the chord-segment factor $1.833375$, and scale by $r^2/2$ via `0.6 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:÷}}` to deliver the wetted flow area at $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.3300)$.

### Workbook Problem 6.13: Slosh Baffle Circular Perforation Net Area

Circular baffle plate $R = 400.0\text{ mm}$ perforated with 48 circular holes of diameter $d = 35.0\text{ mm}$ ($r_h = 17.5\text{ mm}$):
* Solid plate area: $\pi(400^2) = 160,000\pi \approx 502,654.82\text{ mm}^2$
* Perforations total: $48 \times \pi(17.5^2) = 48 \times 306.25\pi = 14,700\pi \approx 46,181.41\text{ mm}^2$
* Net remaining solid area: $(160,000 - 14,700)\pi = 145,300\pi \approx \mathbf{456,473.41\text{ mm}^2}$

In liquid-propellant rocket tanks, perforated anti-slosh ring baffles damp propellant sloshing modes via orifice vortex shedding while minimizing structural mass. Evaluating gross solid plate area $\pi R^2 \approx 502,654.82\text{ mm}^2$ with `400 {{key:x²}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}}` and subtracting the 48 perforation holes $48 \times \pi r_h^2 \approx 46,181.41\text{ mm}^2$ via `17.5 {{key:x²}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 48 {{key:×}} {{key:-}}` leaves net solid baffle area at $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 456473.4126)$, confirming **$456,473.41\text{ mm}^2$** (a $9.19\%$ open-area porosity ratio)—or factor $\pi$ out to the end via `400 {{key:x²}} 17.5 {{key:x²}} 48 {{key:×}} {{key:-}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}}` to save two keystrokes!

---

### Workbook Problem 6.14: Inscribed and Circumscribed Spheres of Regular Octahedron

Octahedron with edge length $a = 40.0\text{ mm}$:
* Circumscribed radius: $R = \frac{a}{\sqrt{2}} = \frac{40}{\sqrt{2}} \approx \mathbf{28.2843\text{ mm}}$
* Inscribed radius: $r = \frac{a}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{40}{\sqrt{6}} \approx \mathbf{16.3299\text{ mm}}$
Ratio $R/r = \sqrt{3} \approx 1.732051$.

**Keystrokes:** `40 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:√x}} {{key:÷}} 40 {{key:ENTER}} 6 {{key:√x}} {{key:÷}} {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow$ **`1.7321`**

In any regular octahedron (and in its dual, the cube!), the ratio of the circumscribed sphere radius $R$ (passing through the 6 vertices) to the inscribed sphere radius $r$ (tangent to the 8 equilateral-triangle faces) is the scale-invariant constant $R/r = \sqrt{6}/\sqrt{2} = \sqrt{3}$. Evaluate circumradius $R = a/\sqrt{2} \approx 28.2843\text{ mm}$ via `40 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:√x}} {{key:÷}}`, then inradius $r = a/\sqrt{6} \approx 16.3299\text{ mm}$ via `40 {{key:ENTER}} 6 {{key:√x}} {{key:÷}}` (leaving both radii visible in $Y$ and $X$). Dividing the two via `{{key:÷}}` yields exact sphere ratio $R/r = \sqrt{3} \approx 1.7321$ at $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: 1.7321)$, and squaring via `{{key:x²}}` confirms the dimension-free surface-area ratio $(R/r)^2 = 3.0000$.

### Workbook Problem 6.15: Prolate Spheroid Volume and Eccentricity

Streamlined airship envelope with semimajor axis $a = 60.0\text{ m}$, equatorial radius $b = 15.0\text{ m}$:

$$V = \frac{4}{3} \pi a b^2 = \frac{4}{3} \pi (60)(15^2) = 80 \pi (225) = 18,000\pi \approx \mathbf{56,548.67\text{ m}^3}$$
$$e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} = \sqrt{1 - \frac{225}{3600}} = \sqrt{1 - 0.0625} = \sqrt{0.9375} \approx \mathbf{0.968246}$$

**Keystrokes:** `4 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:÷}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 60 {{key:×}} 15 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}}` $\rightarrow$ **`56548.6678`**; `15 {{key:x²}} 60 {{key:x²}} {{key:÷}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}} {{key:√x}}` $\rightarrow$ **`0.9682`**

A prolate spheroid of fineness ratio $a/b = 60/15 = 4:1$ approaches the ideal low-drag Prandtl-Glauert hull shape for subsonic airships and submarine hulls. Evaluate volume constant $\frac{4\pi}{3} \approx 4.188790$ via `4 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:÷}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}}` and multiply by $a \cdot b^2 = 60 \times 15^2$ via `60 {{key:×}} 15 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}}`, establishing envelope volume $56,548.67\text{ m}^3$ in Register $X$. For eccentricity, form squared ratio $b^2/a^2 = 0.0625$ with `15 {{key:x²}} 60 {{key:x²}} {{key:÷}}`, subtract from unity, and extract the square root via `1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}} {{key:√x}}` to settle prolate eccentricity at $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 56548.6678, X: 0.9682)$.

## Chapter 07: Polynomials, Brent-Dekker Solver & Root Finding — Extended Engineering & Competition Problems {#chapter-07}

*Accompanies [Chapter 07 of Thinking in the Stack](../manuals/sc32/07-polynomials-brent-solver.md) (26 Worked Engineering Problems)*

### Workbook Problem 7.1: Structural Column Buckling Transcendental Mode

For a slender elastic column of length $L$ and flexural rigidity $EI$ clamped at its base and pinned against lateral sway at its top, enforcing zero bending moment at the pinned support on the fourth-order Euler-Bernoulli beam-column differential equation $EI w'''' + P w'' = 0$ (with wavenumber $k = \sqrt{P/EI}$) yields the transcendental buckling eigenvalue equation:

$$\tan(x) - x = 0 \quad (\text{with } x = k L)$$

Set the angular baseline mode to `{{key:RAD}}` and program the characteristic equation into `{{key:EQN}}` under label `BUCK`: `X {{key:TAN}} X {{key:-}}`.

Keying `4.5 {{key:STO}} X` loads the initial trial eigenvalue estimate $x_0 = 4.5000\text{ rad}$ (just below the vertical asymptote at $3\pi/2 \approx 4.7124$) into variable $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 4.5000)$. Striking `{{key:SOLVE}} X` executes Brent's hybrid root solver, evaluating $\tan(x) - x$ and rapidly contracting the interval via inverse quadratic interpolation to isolate the first non-trivial buckling root $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 4.5000, X: \mathbf{4.493409})$ in Register $X$ ($\approx 1.4303\pi$, corresponding to an effective column length factor $K = \pi / x = 0.69916$).

### Workbook Problem 7.2: Blackbody Planck Peak Wavelength (Wien's Law Derivation)

Differentiating Max Planck's (1900) quantum blackbody spectral radiance $B_\lambda(\lambda, T) = \frac{2hc^2}{\lambda^5 (e^{hc/\lambda k_B T} - 1)}$ with respect to wavelength $\lambda$ and setting $\partial B_\lambda / \partial \lambda = 0$ under the dimensionless photon-energy substitution $x = \frac{hc}{\lambda k_B T}$ yields the universal transcendental root governing Wien's displacement law:

$$5(1 - e^{-x}) - x = 0 \quad (\text{where } x = h c / (\lambda k_B T))$$

Program the equation into `{{key:EQN}}` under label `WIEN`: `1 X {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}} {{key:-}} 5 {{key:×}} X {{key:-}}`.

Entering `5.0 {{key:STO}} X` stores initial estimate $x_0 = 5.0$ (the high-energy Wien asymptotic limit where $e^{-x} \ll 1$) into variable $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 5.0000)$. Striking `{{key:SOLVE}} X` executes Brent's solver; within 6 iterations of guarded inverse quadratic interpolation, the root converges to $10^{-12}$ precision $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 5.0000, X: \mathbf{4.965114})$ in Register $X$, establishing Wien's displacement constant $b = \frac{hc}{x k_B} = 2.897772 \times 10^{-3}\text{ m}\cdot\text{K}$.

### Workbook Problem 7.3: Adiabatic Flame Temperature Equilibrium

During constant-pressure adiabatic combustion of methane in air, integrating the temperature-dependent molar heat capacity $C_p(T) = c_0 + c_1 T$ of the flue gas products ($\text{CO}_2, \text{H}_2\text{O}, \text{N}_2$) against the lower heating value enthalpy release yields a quadratic first-law energy balance:

$$H_{\text{prod}}(T) - H_{\text{react}} = 0 \implies 0.05 T^2 + 120 T - 280,000 = 0$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_11:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_11:value}}`**

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `0.05 {{key:A}} 120 {{key:B}}` registers enthalpy temperature coefficients $A$ and $B$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0500, X: 120.0000)$. Entering `280000 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} 0 {{key:D}}` loads enthalpy release $C = -280,000$ and depressed constant $D = 0.0$ $(T: 0.0500, Z: 120.0000, Y: -280000.0000, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:EXEC}}` computes Cardano roots; keying `{{key:RCL}} 5` extracts the physical positive adiabatic flame temperature $(T: 120.0000, Z: -3853.3000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{1453.299832}\text{ K})$ in Register $X$ (with unphysical negative rebound root $-3,853.30\text{ K}$ stored in variable 1), confirming **$T_{\text{flame}} = 1,453.30\text{ Kelvin}$**.
