### Workbook Problem 7.4: Bungee Jumper Maximum Cord Stretch

A jumper of mass $m = 75\text{ kg}$ steps from a bridge tethered to an elastic bungee cord of unstretched length $L = 30\text{ m}$ and Hookean spring stiffness $k = 40\text{ N/m}$. At the instant of maximum dynamic arrest, kinetic energy vanishes and total lost gravitational potential energy $mg(L + x)$ equals stored elastic strain energy $\frac{1}{2}kx^2$:

$$20 x^2 - 735.75 x - 22,072.5 = 0$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_14:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_14:value}}`**

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `20 {{key:A}} 735.75 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` loads quadratic coefficient $A = 20.0$ and linear coefficient $B = -735.75$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 20.0000, X: -735.7500)$. Entering `22072.5 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}}` stores constant term $C = -22,072.5$ (with cubic constant $D$ defaulting to $0.0$ automatically for depressed quadratic solving, or explicitly keyed as `0 {{key:D}}`) $(T: 20.0000, Z: -735.7500, Y: -22072.5000, X: 0.0000)$. Striking `{{key:EXEC}}` computes Cardano roots; keying `{{key:RCL}} 5` extracts the physical positive maximum dynamic cord elongation $(T: -735.7500, Z: -19.579333, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{56.366833}\text{ m})$ in Register $X$ (with unphysical negative rebound root $-19.5793\text{ m}$ stored in variable 1), confirming **$x = 56.3668\text{ meters}$** (total fall distance $L + x = 86.37\text{ m}$).

### Workbook Problem 7.5: Chemical Equilibrium Dissociation Constant ($K_c$)

Dinitrogen tetroxide dissociates endothermically into nitrogen dioxide gas ($N_2 O_4 \rightleftharpoons 2 NO_2$) in a sealed reactor with molar equilibrium constant $K_c = 0.040$ and initial concentration $[N_2 O_4]_0 = 0.50\text{ M}$. By the Guldberg-Waage Law of Mass Action, an ICE (Initial-Change-Equilibrium) reaction extent $x$ yields $[NO_2] = 2x$ and $[N_2 O_4] = 0.50 - x$, satisfying the quadratic equilibrium relation $\frac{(2x)^2}{0.50 - x} = K_c$:

$$4 x^2 + 0.040 x - 0.020 = 0$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_15:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_15:value}}`**

In `{{key:POLY}}`, entering `4 {{key:A}} 0.040 {{key:B}}` loads stoichiometry coefficients $A$ and $B$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 4.0000, X: 0.0400)$. Entering `0.020 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}}` loads constant $C = -0.020$ (with $D$ defaulting to $0.0$ automatically for depressed quadratic embedding, or explicitly keyed as `0 {{key:D}}`) $(T: 4.0000, Z: 0.0400, Y: -0.0200, X: 0.0000)$. Striking `{{key:EXEC}}` evaluates quadratic roots; keying `{{key:RCL}} 5` isolates the positive dissociated equilibrium extent $(T: 0.0400, Z: -0.075887, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.065887}\text{ M})$ in Register $X$ ($65.89\text{ mM}$, corresponding to $[NO_2] = 2x = 0.1318\text{ M}$ and $13.18\%$ dissociation).

### Workbook Problem 7.6: Cantilever Beam Deflection Under Distributed Load

For a statically indeterminate propped cantilever beam of span $L = 10\text{ m}$ carrying a uniform distributed load, integrating the Euler-Bernoulli elastic curve $EI v''(x) = M(x)$ yields a cubic compatibility polynomial whose root confirms zero deflection and slope at the clamped support:

$$2 x^3 - 3 L x^2 + L^3 = 0 \implies \text{for } L = 10\text{ m}: \quad 2 x^3 - 30 x^2 + 1000 = 0$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_16:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_16:value}}`**

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `2 {{key:A}} 30 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` registers cubic and quadratic flexural coefficients $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.0000, X: -30.0000)$. Entering `0 {{key:C}} 1000 {{key:D}}` sets linear coefficient $C = 0.0$ and boundary constant $D = 1000$ $(T: 2.0000, Z: -30.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1000.0000)$. Striking `{{key:EXEC}}` runs Cardano's closed-form cubic resolvent, factoring $2(x+5)(x-10)^2 = 0$; keying `{{key:RCL}} 3` extracts the physical clamped-support double root $(T: -30.0000, Z: -5.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{10.000000}\text{ m})$ in Register $X$, where the repeated multiplicity confirms both $v(L) = 0$ and $v'(L) = 0$.

### Workbook Problem 7.7: Rocket Staging Mass Ratio Optimization

A two-stage orbital launch vehicle with payload $m_p = 500\text{ kg}$ and stage structural mass fractions $\epsilon = 0.10$ must maximize total Tsiolkovsky burnout velocity $\Delta v = c_1 \ln R_1 + c_2 \ln R_2$ under a fixed gross liftoff mass constraint. Applying Lagrange multipliers to the coupled stage mass ratios yields a symmetric quadratic optimality condition:

$$\lambda^2 - 2 \mu \lambda + \mu = 0 \implies \lambda^2 - 4.828427 \lambda + 2.414214 = 0$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_03:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_03:value}}`**

In `{{key:POLY}}`, entering `1 {{key:A}} 4.828427 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` enters unit quadratic and negative coupling term $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.0000, X: -4.828427)$. Entering `2.414214 {{key:C}}` sets structural constant $C = 2.414214$ (with $D$ defaulting to $0.0$ automatically for quadratic solving, or keyed as `0 {{key:D}}`) $(T: 1.0000, Z: -4.828427, Y: 2.414214, X: 0.0000)$. Striking `{{key:EXEC}}` evaluates Cardano roots; keying `{{key:RCL}} 5` yields the optimal stage mass ratio $(T: -4.828427, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{4.261972})$ in Register $X$ (with secondary reciprocal root $\lambda_2 = 0.566455$ in variable 3), confirming **$\lambda = 4.261972$**.

### Workbook Problem 7.8: Economic Production Lot Size with Quadratic Storage Congestion

In a high-density distribution warehouse where floor congestion imposes a quadratic marginal holding cost ($h_2 = \$0.05/\text{unit}^2\cdot\text{yr}$) atop the classical Ford W. Harris (1913) linear inventory holding rate ($h_1 = \$7.35/\text{unit}\cdot\text{yr}$) and annualized setup-demand product ($5,000\text{ units}^2/\text{yr}$), differentiating total annual cost $\text{TC}(Q) = \frac{K D}{Q} + \frac{h_1 Q}{2} + \frac{h_2 Q^2}{4}$ with respect to lot size $Q$ yields the quadratic equilibrium condition:
$$0.05 Q^2 + 7.35 Q - 5,000 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `0.05 {{key:A}} 7.35 {{key:B}}` loads quadratic congestion coefficient $A = 0.05$ and linear holding coefficient $B = 7.35$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0500, X: 7.3500)$. Entering `5000 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` loads setup constant $C = -5,000.0$ (with $D = 0.0$ by default) and computes the Cardano roots $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0500, Y: -398.1571, X: 0.0000)$. Keying `{{key:RCL}} 5` recalls the positive optimal production lot size $(T: 0.0500, Z: -398.1571, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{251.157127}\text{ units})$ into Register $X$, yielding **$Q^* = 251.16\text{ units}$** (smaller than the congestion-free Wilson EOQ of $\sqrt{2(5000)/7.35} = 368.86\text{ units}$).

### Workbook Problem 7.9: Laser Resonator Cavity Gaussian Beam Waist Quadratic

A thermal lens with focal parameter $f = 350\text{ mm}$ inside a flat-flat laser resonator cavity of length $L = 600\text{ mm}$ establishes a confocal Rayleigh range squared $z_0^2 = L(2f - L) = 600(700 - 600) = 60,000\text{ mm}^2$ ($z_0 = 244.9490\text{ mm}$). By Kogelnik's $ABCD$ complex beam parameter transformation, the intracavity TEM$_{00}$ beam waist positions $z$ satisfy the quadratic resonator stability equation:
$$z^2 - L z + z_0^2 = 0 \implies z^2 - 600 z + 60,000 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `1 {{key:A}} 600 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` loads quadratic coefficient $A = 1.0$ and cavity span $B = -600.0$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.0000, X: -600.0000)$. Entering `60000 {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` sets $C = 60,000.0$ and solves the quadratic $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0000)$. Keying `{{key:RCL}} 3` extracts the primary intracavity waist position $(T: 1.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{126.794919}\text{ mm})$, and keying `{{key:RCL}} 5` recalls the conjugate symmetric waist location $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 126.7949, X: \mathbf{473.205081}\text{ mm})$ (with $z_1 + z_2 = L = 600.0\text{ mm}$ by Viète's sum of roots).

### Workbook Problem 7.10: Centrifugal Pump and Piping System Operating Point Quadratic

A centrifugal pump with backward-curved impeller blades exhibits manometric head curve $H_p(Q) = 60 + 0.15 Q - 0.002 Q^2$, while the pipeline system resistance (static lift plus Darcy-Weisbach friction loss) is $H_s(Q) = 15 + 0.005 Q^2$. Equating pump supply head to pipeline demand head determines the steady-state hydraulic operating point:
$$60 + 0.15 Q - 0.002 Q^2 = 15 + 0.005 Q^2 \implies 0.007 Q^2 - 0.15 Q - 45 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, entering `0.007 {{key:A}} 0.15 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` loads combined hydraulic resistance $A = 0.0070$ and linear Euler swirl coefficient $B = -0.1500$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0070, X: -0.1500)$. Keying `45 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` sets static head differential $C = -45.0$ and solves for the roots $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0070, Y: -70.1768, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:RCL}} 5` isolates the physical positive operating discharge $(T: 0.0070, Z: -70.1768, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{91.605369}\text{ m}^3/\text{hr})$, yielding **$Q = 91.61\text{ m}^3/\text{hr}$** at operating head $H = 56.97\text{ m}$.

### Workbook Problem 7.11: Combined Stokes-Newton Aerodynamic Drag Equilibrium

A spherical meteorological drop-sonde descending through the atmosphere experiences both linear Stokes viscous shear drag ($c_1 v = 6\pi\mu r v$, with $c_1 = 0.27\text{ N}\cdot\text{s/m}$) and quadratic Newton pressure-wake drag ($c_2 v^2 = \frac{1}{2}\rho C_D A v^2$, with $c_2 = 0.018\text{ N}\cdot\text{s}^2/\text{m}^2$) balancing its net buoyant weight $W = 31.5\text{ N}$:
$$c_2 v^2 + c_1 v - W = 0 \implies 0.018 v^2 + 0.27 v - 31.5 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, loading `0.018 {{key:A}} 0.27 {{key:B}}` enters quadratic pressure-drag coefficient $A = 0.0180$ and linear viscous coefficient $B = 0.2700$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0180, X: 0.2700)$. Keying `31.5 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` loads net weight $C = -31.5$ and solves the quadratic $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0180, Y: -50.0000, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:RCL}} 5` recalls the positive terminal equilibrium velocity $(T: 0.0180, Z: -50.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{35.000000}\text{ m/s})$, yielding **$v = 35.00\text{ m/s}$** ($126.0\text{ km/h}$).

### Workbook Problem 7.12: Non-Linear Torsional Pendulum Oscillation Frequency

A hardening Duffing-type torsional suspension exhibits cubic nonlinear restoring torque $\tau(\theta) = -k \theta - \alpha \theta^3$ under static external torque $\tau_{\text{ext}} = 5.0\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}$. For linear stiffness $k = 10\text{ N}\cdot\text{m/rad}$ and strain-hardening coefficient $\alpha = 2\text{ N}\cdot\text{m/rad}^3$, static angular deflection $\theta$ satisfies a naturally depressed cubic polynomial ($B = 0$):

$$2 \theta^3 + 10 \theta - 5 = 0$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_17:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_07_polynomials_brent_solver_seq_17:value}}`**

In `{{key:POLY}}`, entering `2 {{key:A}}` loads non-linear cubic coefficient $A = 2.0$ (with quadratic coefficient $B$ defaulting to $0.0$ automatically, or explicitly keyed as `0 {{key:B}}`) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.0000, X: 0.0000)$. Entering `10 {{key:C}} 5 {{key:+/-}} {{key:D}}` loads linear restoring torque $C = 10.0$ and external load $D = -5.0$ $(T: 2.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 10.0000, X: -5.0000)$. Striking `{{key:EXEC}}` computes Cardano roots; because $p = C/A = 5 > 0$, the cubic is strictly monotonic and the unique real equilibrium deflection angle emerges in Register $Y$ $(T: 2.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: \mathbf{0.478138}, X: 0.000000)$ with the complex conjugate pair stored in variables `3`–`6`, or recalled directly via `{{key:RCL}} 1`, confirming **$\theta = 0.478138\text{ radians}$** ($27.40^\circ$).

### Workbook Problem 7.13: Annular Clutch Cubic Torque Allocation Root

For a uniform-wear annular friction clutch of outer radius $R = 150.0\text{ mm}$ where axial contact pressure varies inversely with radius ($p(r) \propto 1/r$), normalizing the inner radius $x = r_i / R \in (0, 1)$ produces normalized frictional torque capacity $T(x) \propto x(1 - x^2)$, which peaks at $x^* = 1/\sqrt{3} \approx 0.57735$. Sizing an annular lining to deliver a specified fraction of theoretical capacity requires solving the depressed cubic polynomial ($\Delta < 0$, *casus irreducibilis* with three distinct real roots):
$$x^3 - 3 x + 1.5 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `1 {{key:A}} 0 {{key:B}}` sets leading cubic coefficient $A = 1.0$ and depressed quadratic coefficient $B = 0.0$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.0000, X: 0.0000)$. Entering `3 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} 1.5 {{key:D}} {{key:EXEC}}` loads $C = -3.0, D = 1.5$ and executes François Viète's trigonometric trisection $(T: 1.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: -1.9422, X: 0.0000)$. Keying `{{key:RCL}} 3 150 {{key:×}}` recalls the unique physical inner-to-outer radius ratio $x = 0.557875 \in (0, 1)$ and scales by $R = 150.0\text{ mm}$ $(T: -1.9422, Z: -1.9422, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{83.681205}\text{ mm})$, yielding **$r_i = 83.68\text{ mm}$**.

### Workbook Problem 7.14: Hydraulic Jump Conjugate Depth Quadratic (Bélanger Equation)

A supercritical open-channel spillway flow with upstream depth $y_1 = 0.40\text{ m}$ and Froude number $\text{Fr}_1 = 4.5$ ($\text{Fr}_1^2 = 20.25$) undergoes a turbulent stationary shock—a hydraulic jump—into tranquil subcritical flow. Although mechanical energy is dissipated across the turbulent roller, Jean-Baptiste Ch. Bélanger (1838) showed that specific momentum flux $\frac{1}{2}y^2 + \frac{q^2}{gy}$ is conserved across the jump, yielding the Bélanger quadratic equation for downstream conjugate (sequent) depth $y_2$:
$$y_2^2 + y_1 y_2 - 2 y_1^2 \text{Fr}_1^2 = 0 \implies y_2^2 + 0.40 y_2 - 6.48 = 0 \implies y_2 = \mathbf{2.353429\text{ meters}}$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, entering `1 {{key:A}} 0.40 {{key:B}}` loads unit quadratic coefficient $A = 1.0$ and upstream depth $B = 0.40\text{ m}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.0000, X: 0.4000)$. Keying `6.48 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` loads momentum constant $C = -2(0.40)^2(20.25) = -6.48$ and extracts the roots $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: -2.7534, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:RCL}} 5` recalls the positive subcritical conjugate depth $(T: 1.0000, Z: -2.7534, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{2.353429}\text{ m})$, yielding **$y_2 = 2.3534\text{ meters}$** (a $5.88\times$ depth jump dissipating $\Delta E = \frac{(y_2 - y_1)^3}{4 y_1 y_2} = 1.98\text{ m}$ of hydraulic head).

### Workbook Problem 7.15: Acoustical Helmholtz Resonator Neck-Radius Quadratic

An acoustic Helmholtz absorber chamber of volume $V = 1.93793 \times 10^{-3}\text{ m}^3$ and physical neck length $L = 50.0\text{ mm}$ ($c = 343\text{ m/s}$) is tuned to attenuate a $f_0 = 120\text{ Hz}$ tonal resonance. Because the oscillating air plug in the neck radiates piston-like acoustic inertia into both the cavity and free space, Lord Rayleigh's flanged end-correction extends the effective inertial length to $L' = L + 1.7a$, coupling neck radius $a$ (in $\text{mm}$) into both numerator area $A = \pi a^2$ and denominator mass length inside $f_0 = \frac{c}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\pi a^2}{V(L + 1.7a)}}$:
$$a^2 - \left(\frac{6.8\pi f_0^2 V}{c^2}\right) a - \left(\frac{4\pi f_0^2 V L}{c^2}\right) = 0 \implies a^2 - 5.066225 a - 149.006625 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `1 {{key:A}} 5.066225 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` loads $A = 1.0$ and Rayleigh end-correction coefficient $B = -5.066225\text{ mm}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.0000, X: -5.066225)$. Entering `149.006625 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` sets cavity constant $C = -149.006625\text{ mm}^2$ and solves the quadratic $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: -9.9338, X: 0.0000)$. Striking `{{key:RCL}} 5` isolates the physical positive neck radius $(T: 1.0000, Z: -9.9338, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{15.000000}\text{ mm})$, giving **$a = 15.00\text{ mm}$** (neck diameter $d = 2a = 30.00\text{ mm}$ and effective acoustic neck length $L' = 50.0 + 1.7(15.0) = 75.50\text{ mm}$).

### Workbook Problem 7.16: Relativistic Proton Kinetic Energy Quadratic

A synchrotron proton ($m_0 c^2 = 938.272\text{ MeV}$) circulates with relativistic momentum $p c = 2,500.0\text{ MeV}$. Substituting total relativistic energy $E = K + m_0 c^2$ into Einstein's Lorentz-invariant energy-momentum four-vector norm $E^2 - (pc)^2 - (m_0 c^2)^2 = 0$ eliminates the rest-mass square and yields a quadratic equation directly in kinetic energy $K$:
$$K^2 + 2(m_0 c^2) K - (pc)^2 = 0 \implies K^2 + 1876.544 K - 6,250,000 = 0 \implies K = \mathbf{1,732.0003\text{ MeV}}$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `1 {{key:A}} 1876.544 {{key:B}}` enters $A = 1.0$ and rest-energy coupling $B = 2 m_0 c^2 = 1876.544\text{ MeV}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.0000, X: 1876.5440)$. Entering `6250000 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` loads $C = -(pc)^2 = -6,250,000\text{ MeV}^2$ and solves for the energy branches $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: -3608.5443, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:RCL}} 5` recalls the positive-energy particle kinetic energy $(T: 1.0000, Z: -3608.5443, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.000000)$, yielding **$K = 1,732.000\text{ MeV}$** (total energy $E = K + m_0 c^2 = 2,670.272\text{ MeV}$, Lorentz factor $\gamma = E / m_0 c^2 = 2.8459$, while the lower root $r_1 = -3,608.544\text{ MeV} = -E - m_0 c^2$ in Register `3` reflects Paul Dirac's negative-energy sea).

### Workbook Problem 7.17: Heat Exchanger Implicit Terminal Temperature Root (LMTD)

A counter-flow shell-and-tube heat exchanger required to maintain Log Mean Temperature Difference $\text{LMTD} = 41.8624^\circ\text{C}$ operates with hot-end temperature approach $\Delta T_1 = 65.0^\circ\text{C}$. Because $\Delta T_2 = X$ appears simultaneously in the linear numerator difference and the logarithmic denominator ratio of $\text{LMTD} = \frac{\Delta T_1 - \Delta T_2}{\ln(\Delta T_1 / \Delta T_2)}$, isolating the cold-end terminal temperature approach requires solving the implicit transcendental LMTD equation:
$$f(X) = \frac{65 - X}{\ln(65 / X)} - 41.8624 = 0 \implies X = \Delta T_2 = \mathbf{25.0000^\circ\text{C}}$$

Program `65 X {{key:-}} 65 X {{key:÷}} {{key:LN}} {{key:÷}} 41.8624 {{key:-}}` into `{{key:EQN}}`.

Keying `30 {{key:STO}} X` seeds initial terminal approach guess $X_0 = 30.0^\circ\text{C}$ (safely below the removable $0/0$ singularity at $X = 65.0^\circ\text{C}$ where $\lim_{X\to 65} \text{LMTD} = 65.0^\circ\text{C}$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 30.0000)$. Striking `{{key:SOLVE}} X` executes Brent's hybrid solver to isolate the cold-end terminal temperature difference $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 30.0000, X: \mathbf{25.000000}^\circ\text{C})$, yielding **$\Delta T_2 = 25.00^\circ\text{C}$**.

### Workbook Problem 7.18: Aircraft Cambered Drag-Polar Quadratic Root

An aircraft wing of aspect ratio $\text{AR} = 9.5$ and Oswald span efficiency $e = 0.85$ has Prandtl lifting-line induced-drag factor $k = \frac{1}{\pi e \text{AR}} = 0.039419$, cambered airfoil minimum-drag shift $b = -0.0050$, and parasitic zero-lift drag $C_{D0} = 0.0200$. At a cruise thrust setting corresponding to total drag coefficient $C_D = 0.033405$, the operating lift coefficient $C_L$ satisfies the cambered parabolic drag polar:
$$k C_L^2 + b C_L + (C_{D0} - C_D) = 0 \implies 0.039419 C_L^2 - 0.0050 C_L - 0.013405 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, entering `0.039419 {{key:A}} 0.0050 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` loads induced-drag factor $A = 0.039419$ and camber offset $B = -0.0050$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0394, X: -0.0050)$. Keying `0.013405 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` sets net drag margin $C = -0.013405$ and extracts the roots $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0394, Y: -0.5232, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:RCL}} 5` recalls the positive cruise lift coefficient $(T: 0.0394, Z: -0.5232, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.650010})$, yielding **$C_L = 0.6500$** (where vortex-induced drag is $C_{Di} = k C_L^2 = 0.016655$ and lift-to-drag ratio is $L/D = C_L / C_D = 19.46$).

### Workbook Problem 7.19: Planetary Escaping Orbit Hyperbolic Excess Velocity Quadratic

During a Trans-Mars injection burn at low-Earth parking radius $r = 6,671\text{ km}$ ($\mu_E = 398,600\text{ km}^3/\text{s}^2$, local parabolic escape energy $\frac{2\mu_E}{r} = 119.5023\text{ km}^2/\text{s}^2$), a spacecraft reaches geocentric burnout speed $v = 11.45\text{ km/s}$. Conservation of specific orbital mechanical energy $\mathcal{E} = \frac{v^2}{2} - \frac{\mu_E}{r} = \frac{v_\infty^2}{2}$ yields the vis-viva quadratic for the asymptotic hyperbolic excess velocity $v_\infty$ (and characteristic injection energy $C_3 = v_\infty^2$):
$$v_\infty^2 - v^2 + \frac{2\mu_E}{r} = 0 \implies v_\infty = \sqrt{11.45^2 - \frac{2(398600)}{6671}} = \sqrt{131.1025 - 119.5023} = \sqrt{11.6002} = \mathbf{3.4059\text{ km/s}}$$

Pushing `11.45 {{key:x²}}` squares injection burn velocity to obtain $v^2 = 131.1025\text{ km}^2/\text{s}^2$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 131.1025)$. Keying `2 {{key:ENTER}} 398600 {{key:×}} 6671 {{key:÷}} {{key:-}}` evaluates local gravitational escape energy $2\mu_E / r = 119.5023\text{ km}^2/\text{s}^2$ and subtracts to isolate $C_3 = v_\infty^2 = 11.6002\text{ km}^2/\text{s}^2$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 11.6002)$. Striking `{{key:√x}}` extracts the positive quadratic root in place, resolving asymptotic interplanetary departure velocity $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{3.405903}\text{ km/s})$, yielding **$v_\infty = 3.4059\text{ km/s}$**.

### Workbook Problem 7.20: Planar Couette-Poiseuille Channel Velocity Profile Root

Incompressible Newtonian fluid ($\mu = 0.10\text{ Pa}\cdot\text{s}$, $\nu = 10^{-4}\text{ m}^2/\text{s}$) flows between a stationary lower wall ($y = 0$) and a parallel upper wall at $h = 0.050\text{ m}$ translating at $U = 2.0\text{ m/s}$ ($\text{Re} = U h / \nu = 1,000.0$) against an adverse stream-wise pressure gradient $dp/dx = +120\text{ Pa/m}$. Integrating the Navier-Stokes momentum balance $\mu \frac{d^2 u}{dy^2} = \frac{dp}{dx}$ superimposes linear Couette shear and parabolic Poiseuille backflow into $u(y) = 600 y^2 + 10 y$. Find the channel elevation $y$ where local velocity reaches $u(y) = 1.00\text{ m/s}$:
$$600 y^2 + 10 y - 1.00 = 0 \implies y = \mathbf{0.033333\text{ meters}}$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, loading `600 {{key:A}} 10 {{key:B}}` enters pressure-gradient curvature $A = \frac{1}{2\mu}\frac{dp}{dx} = 600.0$ and wall-shear gradient $B = 10.0$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 600.0000, X: 10.0000)$. Keying `1 {{key:+/-}} {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` loads target velocity offset $C = -1.00$ and solves the quadratic $(T: 0.0000, Z: 600.0000, Y: -0.0500, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:RCL}} 5` isolates the physical channel elevation $(T: 600.0000, Z: -0.0500, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.033333}\text{ m})$, yielding **$y = 33.33\text{ mm}$** ($2/3$ of the channel gap $h$).

### Workbook Problem 7.21: Electromagnetic Skin-Depth Attenuation Wavenumber Root

At industrial plasma RF excitation frequency $f = 13.56\text{ MHz}$ in an aluminum conductor ($\sigma = 3.5 \times 10^7\text{ S/m}$, $\mu_0 = 4\pi \times 10^{-7}\text{ H/m}$), Maxwell's curl equations in a good conductor ($\sigma \gg \omega\varepsilon$) reduce to the magnetic diffusion wave equation $\nabla^2 \mathbf{E} = j\omega\mu_0\sigma \mathbf{E}$, whose attenuation wavenumber $k = 1/\delta$ satisfies $k^2 - \pi f \mu_0 \sigma = 0$:
$$\delta = \frac{1}{k} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi f \mu_0 \sigma}} = \mathbf{2.311 \times 10^{-5}\text{ m}} = 23.11\,\mu\text{m}$$

Keying `{{key:π}} 13.56 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:×}}` computes angular factor $\pi f \approx 4.2600 \times 10^7\text{ rad/s}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 4.2600 \times 10^7)$. Keying `4 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 1 {{key:E}} 7 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}} {{key:×}}` multiplies by permeability $\mu_0 = 4\pi \times 10^{-7}\text{ H/m}$, yielding $53.5327$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 53.5327)$. Keying `3.5 {{key:E}} 7 {{key:×}}` multiplies by conductivity $\sigma = 3.5 \times 10^7\text{ S/m}$ to form $k^2 = 1.8736 \times 10^9\text{ m}^{-2}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.8736 \times 10^9)$. Striking `{{key:√x}} {{key:1/x}}` extracts the wavenumber root $k$ and inverts in place to obtain the electromagnetic skin depth $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{2.311 \times 10^{-5}}\text{ m})$ ($23.11\,\mu\text{m}$).

Across the troposphere, static pressure $P(h)$ (in $\text{kPa}$) as a function of geopotential altitude $h$ (in $\text{km}$) is modeled by the cubic barometric calibration polynomial:
$$P(h) = -0.025 h^3 + 0.95 h^2 - 12.0 h + 101.325 = ((-0.025 h + 0.95) h - 12.0) h + 101.325$$

### Workbook Problem 7.23: Thick-Walled Pressure Vessel Lamé Wall-Thickness Quadratic

A high-pressure cylindrical autoclave of outer radius $R_o = 200\text{ mm}$ ($D = 400\text{ mm}$) containing internal pressure $P = 45.0\text{ MPa}$ must limit maximum inner-bore circumferential (hoop) stress—governed by Gabriel Lamé's (1852) thick-shell elasticity solution $\sigma_\theta(R_i) = P \frac{R_o^2 + (R_o - t)^2}{R_o^2 - (R_o - t)^2}$—to allowable working stress $\sigma_{\text{allow}} = 125.0\text{ MPa}$, yielding the wall-thickness quadratic:
$$17 t^2 - 6,800 t + 360,000 = 0 \implies t = \mathbf{62.801132\text{ mm}}$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, entering `17 {{key:A}} 6800 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` loads Lamé stress sum $A = (\sigma_{\text{allow}} + P)/10 = 17.0$ and linear bore coefficient $B = -2 R_o A = -6,800.0$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 17.0000, X: -6800.0000)$. Keying `360000 {{key:C}} {{key:EXEC}}` loads pressure-radius constant $C = 360,000.0$ and extracts the roots $(T: 0.0000, Z: 17.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0000)$. Pressing `{{key:RCL}} 3` recalls the physical wall thickness $t < R_o$ $(T: 17.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{62.801132}\text{ mm})$, yielding **$t = 62.80\text{ mm}$** (inner bore radius $R_i = R_o - t = 137.20\text{ mm}$, compared to the unconservative thin-wall Barlow approximation $t_{\text{thin}} = P R_o / \sigma_{\text{allow}} = 72.0\text{ mm}$).

### Workbook Problem 7.24: Gyroscopic Rotor Nutation & Steady Precession Quadratic

A rapidly spinning axisymmetric gyroscopic rotor with transverse moment of inertia $I_t = 0.030\text{ kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$, polar spin angular momentum $H = I_p \omega_s = 18.0\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}\cdot\text{s}$, and gravitational overturning torque $M g L = 2.40\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}$ executes steady conical motion at nutation angle $\theta \approx 90^\circ$ when its precession angular velocity $\Omega$ satisfies Euler's gyroscopic quadratic:
$$I_t \Omega^2 - H \Omega + M g L = 0 \implies 0.030 \Omega^2 - 18.0 \Omega + 2.40 = 0$$

In `{{key:POLY}}`, keying `0.030 {{key:A}} 18.0 {{key:+/-}} {{key:B}}` loads transverse inertia $A = 0.0300$ and negative spin angular momentum $B = -18.0000$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0300, X: -18.0000)$. ### Workbook Problem 7.26: High-Speed Sprint: Cardano Cubic Instant Root

Solve the depressed cubic $x^3 - 7x - 6 = 0$. Factorization: $(x+1)(x+2)(x-3) = 0 \implies x_1 = -1, x_2 = -2, x_3 = 3$.
The Cardano/Viète hardware solver outputs **$x = 3.0$** in under 2 seconds! To verify root $x = 3$ in a single pass, stream the depressed cubic $x^3 - 7x - 6 = (x^2 - 7)x - 6$ through the stack via Horner's method: Executing `3 {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}} {{key:×}}` loads candidate root $x = 3$ into $Y$ and squares to obtain $x^2 = 9.0000$ in $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 3.0000, X: 9.0000)$. Striking `{{key:×}} 6 {{key:-}}` multiplies directly by the candidate root waiting in $Y$ ($3 \times 2 = 6$) and subtracts 6, yielding an exact zero residual $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.0000})$ and confirming **$x = 3.0$** ($f(3) = 0$).

### Workbook Problem 8.1: Exponential Distribution CDF Integral

In reliability engineering, the memoryless exponential distribution models the survival of electronic components subjected to random Poisson failure shocks. The cumulative probability that a component with unit hazard rate $\lambda = 1$ fails before normalized time $t = 1$ is $F(1) = \int_0^1 e^{-x} dx$:

$$\int_0^1 e^{-x} \, dx = 1 - e^{-1} \approx 1 - 0.3678794 = \mathbf{0.632120559}$$

Program the hazard density into `{{key:EQN}}` as `X {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}}`. Staging the unit lifetime interval `0 {{key:ENTER}} 1` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.0000)$ and invoking `{{key:∫}} X` constructs the Romberg tableau across the concave decay curve, depositing the certified Richardson truncation bound $4.82 \times 10^{-12}$ into Register $Y$ and the canonical $1 - e^{-1} \approx \mathbf{0.632120559}$ ($63.21\%$ one-time-constant failure probability) into Register $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 4.82\times 10^{-12}, X: 0.632120559)$.

**Analytical Stack Verification:** {{res:sc32_08_calc_03:keys}} $\implies \mathbf{{{res:sc32_08_calc_03:value}}}$.

---

### Workbook Problem 8.2: Uniform Distribution Probability Integral

A quantizer or phase-locked loop whose phase error is uniformly distributed across $[0, 10]$ has constant density $f(x) = 0.10$. Integrating over the half-span $[0, 5]$ provides an immediate diagnostic baseline for zero-curvature quadrature:

$$\int_0^5 0.10 \, dx = 0.10 \times 5 = \mathbf{0.500000}$$

Program the constant density `0.1` into `{{key:EQN}}`, stage the interval `0 {{key:ENTER}} 5` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.1000, Y: 0.0000, X: 5.0000)$, and execute `{{key:∫}} X`. Because all higher derivatives $f^{(2m)}(x)$ vanish identically in the Euler-Maclaurin expansion, the very first trapezoidal panel $R(0,0)$ is exact, returning an identically zero truncation error bound $0.0000$ in Register $Y$ and $\mathbf{0.500000000}$ in Register $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.1000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.500000000)$.

**Analytical Stack Verification:** {{res:sc32_08_calc_04:keys}} $\implies \mathbf{{{res:sc32_08_calc_04:value}}}$.

### Workbook Problem 8.3: Gaussian Normal 1-Sigma Confidence Interval ($68.27\%$)

The Gaussian density $\phi(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^2/2}$ possesses no elementary antiderivative—a theorem proved by Joseph Liouville in 1835. Integrating across the central inflection points from $z = -1.0$ to $z = +1.0$ establishes the $1\sigma$ metrological confidence band:

$$P(-1 \le Z \le 1) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-1}^1 e^{-x^2 / 2} \, dx \approx \mathbf{0.682689492}$$

With the standard normal curve `NORM` active, staging the symmetric $1\sigma$ bounds via `1 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 1` (or `1 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} {{key:+/-}}`) $(T: -1.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: -1.0000, X: 1.0000)$ and pressing `{{key:∫}} X` eliminates the inflection curvature through Richardson extrapolation, returning error bound $1.85 \times 10^{-11}$ in Register $Y$ and the $68.27\%$ confidence probability $\mathbf{0.682689492}$ in Register $X$ $(T: -1.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 1.85\times 10^{-11}, X: 0.682689492)$.

---

$$P(-2 \le Z \le 2) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-2}^2 e^{-x^2 / 2} \, dx \approx \mathbf{0.954499736}$$

Using the same `NORM` equation, stage the $2\sigma$ limits `2 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 2` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: -2.0000, X: 2.0000)$ and invoke `{{key:∫}} X`. In statistical process control (Walter Shewhart's $\pm 3\sigma$ control limits), integrating from $z = -3.0$ to $z = +3.0$ captures all but $0.27\%$ ($2,700\text{ ppm}$) of random variation:

$$P(-3 \le Z \le 3) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-3}^3 e^{-x^2 / 2} \, dx \approx \mathbf{0.997300204}$$

### Workbook Problem 8.6: Polytropic Gas Expansion Work: $P V^{1.4} = C$

An ideal diatomic gas expands reversibly in an insulated cylinder from $V_1 = 0.02\text{ m}^3$ to $V_2 = 0.08\text{ m}^3$. Initial chamber pressure $P_1 = 600.0\text{ kPa} = 600,000\text{ Pa}$. Polytropic index $n = 1.4$.
State constant $C = P_1 V_1^{1.4} = 600,000 \times (0.02)^{1.4} \approx 2,492.357\text{ Pa}\cdot\text{m}^{4.2}$.
Boundary expansion work $W = \int_{V_1}^{V_2} P(V) \, dV$:
$$W = C \int_{0.02}^{0.08} V^{-1.4} \, dV = \frac{P_1 V_1 \left[ 1 - (V_1/V_2)^{0.4} \right]}{0.4} = \frac{12,000 \left[ 1 - (0.25)^{0.4} \right]}{0.4}$$
$$(0.25)^{0.4} \approx 0.57434918 \implies 1 - 0.57434918 = 0.42565082$$
$$W = \frac{12,000 \times 0.42565082}{0.4} = 30,000 \times 0.42565082 = \mathbf{12,769.52\text{ Joules}} = \mathbf{12.7695\text{ kJ}}$$

Program the adiabatic pressure law `V 1.4 {{key:+/-}} {{key:yˣ}} 2492.357 {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}`. Stage the expansion volume bounds `0.02 {{key:ENTER}} 0.08` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0200, X: 0.0800)$ and press `{{key:∫}} V` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.18\times 10^{-10}, X: 12769.5248)$. Romberg quadrature integrates the steep inverse-power pressure decay across the $4:1$ expansion ratio, placing error bound $2.18 \times 10^{-10}$ in Register $Y$ and mechanical boundary work $12,769.5248\text{ J}$ in Register $X$, which `1000 {{key:÷}}` scales to kilojoules $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.18\times 10^{-10}, X: 12.7695)$.

### Workbook Problem 8.7: Surface Area of Paraboloid Radar Dish of Revolution

A parabolic satellite reflector dish is generated by revolving $y = 0.25 x^2$ about the vertical $y$-axis from center $x = 0$ to outer rim radius $R = 2.0\text{ m}$.
Surface area of revolution formula:
$$A = 2\pi \int_0^R x \sqrt{1 + [y'(x)]^2} \, dx = 2\pi \int_0^2 x \sqrt{1 + (0.5 x)^2} \, dx = 2\pi \int_0^2 x \sqrt{1 + 0.25 x^2} \, dx$$
Analytical evaluation via $u = 1 + 0.25 x^2, du = 0.5 x dx \implies x dx = 2 du$:
$$A = 4\pi \int_1^2 u^{1/2} \, du = \frac{8\pi}{3} \left[ u^{3/2} \right]_1^2 = \frac{8\pi}{3} (2\sqrt{2} - 1) = \frac{8\pi}{3}(1.8284271) \approx \mathbf{15.317764\text{ m}^2}$$

Program the surface-element integrand `X 0.5 {{key:×}} {{key:x²}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:√x}} X {{key:×}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}`. Staging the aperture radial bounds `0 {{key:ENTER}} 2` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.0000)$ and pressing `{{key:∫}} X` integrates the curved paraboloid shell—which exceeds the flat aperture disc area $\pi R^2 = 12.5664\text{ m}^2$ by $21.9\%$—returning error bound $2.14 \times 10^{-10}$ in Register $Y$ and surface area $\mathbf{15.317764\text{ m}^2}$ in Register $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.14\times 10^{-10}, X: 15.317764)$ (**$A = 15.3178\text{ m}^2$**).

### Workbook Problem 8.8: Thermal Heat Conduction Across Temperature-Dependent Wall

High-temperature furnace wall of thickness $L = 0.20\text{ m}$ and cross-sectional area $A = 10.0\text{ m}^2$.
Material thermal conductivity is temperature-dependent: $k(T) = 0.50 + 0.002 T\text{ W/(m}\cdot\text{K)}$.
Internal hot face $T_1 = 800.0\text{ K}$, exterior cold face $T_2 = 300.0\text{ K}$.
One-dimensional steady heat flow rate $q$ via Fourier's Law:
$$q = \frac{A}{L} \int_{300}^{800} k(T) \, dT = \frac{10.0}{0.20} \int_{300}^{800} (0.50 + 0.002 T) \, dT = 50.0 \left[ 0.50 T + 0.001 T^2 \right]_{300}^{800}$$
$$q = 50.0 \left[ 0.50(500) + 0.001(800^2 - 300^2) \right] = 50.0 [250 + 0.001(640,000 - 90,000)] = 50.0 [250 + 550]$$
$$q = 50.0 \times 800 = \mathbf{40,000.0\text{ Watts}} = \mathbf{40.000\text{ kW}}$$

Program Kirchhoff's integral transform `T 0.002 {{key:×}} 0.5 {{key:+}} 50 {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}`. Staging the cold-to-hot wall boundary temperatures `300 {{key:ENTER}} 800` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 25.0000, Y: 300.0000, X: 800.0000)$ and pressing `{{key:∫}} T` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 25.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 40000.0000)$ integrates the temperature-dependent refractory conductivity with zero truncation error ($0.0000$ in $Y$, since Trapezoidal/Simpson quadrature is exact on linear and cubic polynomials), yielding $40,000.0\text{ W}$ in $X$ before `1000 {{key:÷}}` scales the flux to kilowatts $(T: 25.0000, Z: 25.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 40.0000)$ (**$q = 40.000\text{ kW}$**).

---

### Workbook Problem 8.9: Fresnel Sine Integral in Diffraction Optics ($S(x)$)

When coherent light passes across a sharp razor edge, Augustin-Jean Fresnel (1818) showed that the near-field diffraction fringes are governed by the non-elementary Fresnel sine integral:

$$S(x) = \int_0^x \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2} t^2\right) dt$$
Evaluate $S(2.0)$ at normalized aperture coordinate $x = 2.0$.

With radian mode engaged (`{{key:RAD}}`), program the quadratic-phase chirp integrand `T {{key:x²}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:÷}} {{key:SIN}}` into `{{key:EQN}}`. Staging `0 {{key:ENTER}} 2` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.0000)$ and pressing `{{key:∫}} T` resolves the accelerating phase oscillations across two full half-cycles, returning certified error bound $1.48 \times 10^{-10}$ in Register $Y$ and the Fresnel sine coordinate $S(2.0) = \mathbf{0.343416}$ in Register $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.48\times 10^{-10}, X: 0.343416)$.

### Workbook Problem 8.10: Fresnel Cosine Integral ($C(x)$)

The companion Fresnel cosine integral defines the horizontal coordinate of Marie Alfred Cornu's clothoid spiral (1874), whose arc length equals $x$ and whose curvature grows linearly with arc length ($\kappa = \pi x$):

$$C(x) = \int_0^x \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2} t^2\right) dt$$
Evaluate $C(2.0)$ at $x = 2.0$. Together with $S(2.0) = 0.343416$ stored in Register `A` from Workbook Problem 8.9, this defines the Cornu spiral phasor position:
$$r = \sqrt{C^2(2) + S^2(2)} = \sqrt{0.488253^2 + 0.343416^2} = \sqrt{0.238391 + 0.117935} = \sqrt{0.356326} \approx \mathbf{0.596930}$$

In radian mode (`{{key:RAD}}`), with the companion cosine integrand `T {{key:x²}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:÷}} {{key:COS}}` in `{{key:EQN}}`, first store $S(2.0)$ in Register `A`, then integrate $C(2.0)$ and combine both components on the stack without re-typing intermediate floats:

Store the Fresnel sine component `0 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:∫}} T {{key:STO}} A` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.48\times 10^{-10}, X: 0.343416)$, then set integration limits `0 {{key:ENTER}} 2` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.3434, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.0000)$ and press `{{key:∫}} T` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.3434, Y: 2.22\times 10^{-10}, X: 0.488253)$ to evaluate the Fresnel cosine integral $C(2.0) = 0.488253$ with error bound $2.22 \times 10^{-10}$ in Register $Y$. Squaring, recalling $S(2.0)$ from Register `A` to square and add, and taking the square root (`{{key:x²}} {{key:RCL}} A {{key:x²}} {{key:+}} {{key:√x}}`) yields the Cornu spiral phasor amplitude $\mathbf{0.596930}$ $(T: 0.3434, Z: 0.3434, Y: 2.22\times 10^{-10}, X: 0.596930)$.

### Workbook Problem 8.11: Gravitational Attraction of Thin Rod on End Particle

A slender spacecraft boom of length $L = 5.0\text{ m}$ and uniform mass $M = 100.0\text{ kg}$ is positioned along the $x$-axis from $x = 1.0\text{ m}$ to $x = 6.0\text{ m}$.
A sensor payload of mass $m = 2.0\text{ kg}$ is positioned at the origin $x = 0$.
Linear mass density $\lambda = M/L = 100.0 / 5.0 = 20.0\text{ kg/m}$.
Gravitational constant $G = 6.6743 \times 10^{-11}\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}^2/\text{kg}^2$.
Newtonian attraction force $F$:
$$F = G m \lambda \int_1^6 \frac{dx}{x^2} = (6.6743 \times 10^{-11}) \times 2.0 \times 20.0 \left[ -\frac{1}{x} \right]_1^6 = 2.66972 \times 10^{-9} \left[ 1 - \frac{1}{6} \right]$$
$$F = 2.66972 \times 10^{-9} \times \frac{5}{6} = \mathbf{2.224767 \times 10^{-9}\text{ Newtons}} = \mathbf{2.224767\text{ nN}}$$

Program the Newtonian differential attraction `X {{key:x²}} {{key:1/x}} 2.66972 {{key:E}} 9 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}`. Staging the boom span limits `1 {{key:ENTER}} 6` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.0000, X: 6.0000)$ and pressing `{{key:∫}} X` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.15\times 10^{-16}, X: 2.224767\times 10^{-9})$ integrates the inverse-square field—demonstrating why distributed-mass gravity cannot be approximated at the geometric midpoint $x = 3.5\text{ m}$, but acts at the harmonic mean distance $\sqrt{x_1 x_2} = \sqrt{6} \approx 2.449\text{ m}$. Multiplying by $10^9$ (`1 {{key:E}} 9 {{key:×}}`) scales the self-gravity perturbation to nanonewtons $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.15\times 10^{-16}, X: 2.224767)$.

### Workbook Problem 8.12: Fourier Series 1st Harmonic Sine Coefficient ($b_1$)

An odd square wave of fundamental period $2\pi$ has value $+1$ on $(0, \pi)$ and $-1$ on $(-\pi, 0)$.
The fundamental Fourier harmonic sine coefficient $b_1$ is:
$$b_1 = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi \sin(x) \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ -\cos(x) \right]_0^\pi = \frac{2}{\pi} (1 - (-1)) = \frac{4}{\pi} \approx \mathbf{1.273240}$$

With radian mode engaged (`{{key:RAD}}`), program the orthogonal harmonic projection `X {{key:SIN}}` into `{{key:EQN}}`. Staging the half-period interval `0 {{key:π}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 3.141593)$ and pressing `{{key:∫}} X` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.82\times 10^{-11}, X: 2.000000)$ evaluates the sine arch area to $2.000000$ with error bound $1.82 \times 10^{-11}$ in $Y$. Normalizing by `2 {{key:×}} {{key:π}} {{key:÷}}` resolves the fundamental Fourier amplitude $b_1 = 4/\pi \approx \mathbf{1.273240}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.82\times 10^{-11}, X: 1.273240)$—explaining why the first harmonic of a unit square wave overshoots the flat $\pm 1$ plateau by $27.3\%$.

### Workbook Problem 8.13: Sound Exposure Level (SEL) from Transient Flyover Noise

An aircraft flyover noise pulse at an airfield boundary is modeled by acoustic pressure $p(t) = p_0 e^{-t^2 / 20}\text{ Pa}$ with peak pressure $p_0 = 2.00\text{ Pa}$ over $t \in [-15, +15]\text{ s}$.
Reference acoustic pressure $p_{\text{ref}} = 20\,\mu\text{Pa} = 2.0 \times 10^{-5}\text{ Pa}$, reference time $t_0 = 1.0\text{ s}$.
Sound exposure integral $E = \int_{-15}^{15} p^2(t) \, dt = \int_{-15}^{15} 4.0 e^{-t^2 / 10} \, dt$:
$$E \approx 4.0 \sqrt{10\pi} = 4.0 \times 5.604991 = \mathbf{22.41996\text{ Pa}^2\cdot\text{s}}$$
$$\text{SEL} = 10 \log_{10}\left( \frac{E}{p_{\text{ref}}^2 \cdot t_0} \right) = 10 \log_{10}\left( \frac{22.41996}{4.0 \times 10^{-10}} \right) = 10 \log_{10}(5.604991 \times 10^{10}) = \mathbf{107.4857\text{ dBA}}$$

Because certification standards compress an entire transient acoustic event into a 1-second equivalent energy dose, entering the squared-pressure envelope `T {{key:x²}} 10 {{key:÷}} {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}} 4 {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and sweeping across the 30-second flyover window `15 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 15` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 4.0000, Y: -15.0000, X: 15.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} T` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 4.0000, Y: 4.88\times 10^{-11}, X: 22.419964)$ integrates total sound exposure $E = 22.419964\text{ Pa}^2\cdot\text{s}$ in Register $X$ (with quadrature uncertainty $4.88 \times 10^{-11}$ in $Y$). Normalizing against the human auditory threshold $p_{\text{ref}}^2 t_0 = 4.0 \times 10^{-10}\text{ Pa}^2\cdot\text{s}$ and mapping to the decibel scale (`4 {{key:E}} 10 {{key:+/-}} {{key:÷}} {{key:LOG}} 10 {{key:×}}`) establishes the certified Sound Exposure Level $\mathbf{107.4857\text{ dBA}}$ $(T: 4.0000, Z: 4.0000, Y: 4.88\times 10^{-11}, X: 107.485743)$ (**$\text{SEL} = 107.49\text{ dBA}$**).

### Workbook Problem 8.14: Aerodynamic Circulation Line Integral & Prandtl Elliptic Spanwise Lift

An aircraft wing of span $b = 12.0\text{ m}$ ($y \in [-6, +6]\text{ m}$) flying at freestream airspeed $V_\infty = 60.0\text{ m/s}$ in air of density $\rho_\infty = 1.225\text{ kg/m}^3$ exhibits Prandtl's minimum-induced-drag elliptic bound circulation distribution with root vortex circulation $\Gamma_0 = 45.0\text{ m}^2/\text{s}$:

$$\Gamma(y) = \Gamma_0 \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{2y}{b}\right)^2} = 45.0 \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{y}{6}\right)^2}\text{ m}^2/\text{s}$$
By the Kutta-Joukowski theorem, sectional lift is $L'(y) = \rho_\infty V_\infty \Gamma(y)$ (peaking at $L'_0 = 1.225 \times 60.0 \times 45.0 = 3,307.50\text{ N/m}$ at the wing root), and total wing lift is the spanwise integral:
$$L = \rho_\infty V_\infty \int_{-6}^{6} 45.0 \sqrt{1 - (y/6)^2} \, dy = (1.225)(60.0)(45.0)\left(6\cdot\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 73.50 \times 424.1150 = \mathbf{31,172.45\text{ N}} = \mathbf{31.17\text{ kN}}$$
Equivalent sustained aircraft mass: $M = L / g = 31,172.45 / 9.80665 = \mathbf{3,178.71\text{ kg}} \approx \mathbf{3.18\text{ metric tonnes}}$.

In Ludwig Prandtl's lifting-line theory, an elliptical spanwise circulation profile produces uniform downwash across the trailing vortex sheet and minimizes induced drag. Defining `Y 6 {{key:÷}} {{key:x²}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}} {{key:√x}} 45 {{key:×}}` in `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating from wingtip to wingtip `6 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 6` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: -6.0000, X: 6.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} Y` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: 3.84\times 10^{-10}, X: 424.1150)$ yields the integrated vortex volume $\int_{-6}^6 \Gamma(y)\,dy = 135\pi \approx 424.1150\text{ m}^3/\text{s}$. Scaling by the Kutta-Joukowski momentum flux $\rho_\infty V_\infty$ (`1.225 {{key:×}} 60 {{key:×}}`, $(T: 45.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: 3.84\times 10^{-10}, X: 31172.4525)$) gives total aerodynamic lift $L = \mathbf{31,172.45\text{ N}}$ ($\mathbf{31.17\text{ kN}}$), which supports a steady-flight aircraft gross mass of $\mathbf{3.18\text{ tonnes}}$ (`9.80665 {{key:÷}} 1000 {{key:÷}}`, $(T: 45.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: 3.84\times 10^{-10}, X: 3.1787)$).

### Workbook Problem 8.15: Damped Harmonic Oscillator Energy Dissipated Per Cycle

Viscous dashpot damper force $F_d = c \dot{x}$ with damping coefficient $c = 15.0\text{ N}\cdot\text{s/m}$.
Natural frequency $\omega = 20.0\text{ rad/s}$, harmonic displacement amplitude $X_0 = 0.020\text{ m}$ ($20\text{ mm}$).
Velocity $v(t) = -\omega X_0 \sin(\omega t) = -0.40 \sin(20 t)\text{ m/s}$.
Oscillation period $T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega} = \frac{\pi}{10} \approx 0.314159\text{ s}$.
Energy dissipated per steady-state cycle:
$$\Delta E = \int_0^T c v^2(t) \, dt = 15.0 \int_0^{\pi/10} [ -0.40 \sin(20 t) ]^2 dt = 2.40 \int_0^{\pi/10} \sin^2(20 t) \, dt$$
$$\Delta E = 2.40 \times \left( \frac{T}{2} \right) = 2.40 \times \frac{\pi}{20} = 0.12 \pi \approx \mathbf{0.376991\text{ Joules}}$$

Under steady-state harmonic excitation, the area enclosed by the elliptical force-displacement hysteresis loop equals the viscous thermal dissipation over one full period $T = \pi/10\text{ s}$. With radian mode (`{{key:RAD}}`) active and the instantaneous power dissipation `T 20 {{key:×}} {{key:SIN}} {{key:x²}} 2.4 {{key:×}}` entered into `{{key:EQN}}`, staging the single-cycle bounds `0 {{key:π}} 10 {{key:÷}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.314159)$ and pressing `{{key:∫}} T` integrates the $\sin^2(\omega t)$ waveform to an error bound of $1.12 \times 10^{-11}$ in $Y$ and per-cycle thermal loss $\mathbf{0.376991\text{ Joules}}$ in $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.12\times 10^{-11}, X: 0.376991)$ (**$\Delta E = 0.3770\text{ Joules}$**).

### Workbook Problem 8.16: Continuous Present Value of Perpetual Cash Flow Stream

Continuous discount rate $r = 0.06$, cash flow intensity $C(t) = 50,000 e^{-0.02 t}$:

$$PV = \int_0^{30} 50000 e^{-0.08 t} \, dt = \frac{50000}{0.08} [1 - e^{-2.4}] = 625,000 [1 - 0.090718] = \mathbf{\$568,301.25}$$

For a depleting petroleum reservoir or royalty trust whose production decays exponentially at $2\%/\text{yr}$ while capital is discounted continuously at $6\%/\text{yr}$, the net present value is governed by the combined hazard rate $\lambda + r = 0.08\text{ yr}^{-1}$. ### Workbook Problem 8.17: Second Moment of Area of Structural I-Beam Web ($I_{xx}$)

For a vertical steel web of height $h = 300\text{ mm}$ ($y \in [-150, +150]\text{ mm}$) and thickness $t = 10\text{ mm}$, the flexural bending stiffness depends on the second moment of area $I_{xx}$:
$$I_{xx} = \int_{-150}^{150} t y^2 \, dy = 10 \left[ \frac{y^3}{3} \right]_{-150}^{150} = \frac{20}{3}(3,375,000) = \mathbf{22,500,000\text{ mm}^4} = 2.25 \times 10^7\text{ mm}^4$$

In Euler-Bernoulli beam bending, fibers farthest from the neutral axis carry linearly increasing normal stress $\sigma_x = -My/I_{xx}$, weighting the flexural rigidity by $y^2$. Entering the differential moment integrand `Y {{key:x²}} 10 {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating across the web depth `150 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 150` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: -150.0000, X: 150.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} Y` yields exact parabolic convergence on the first Richardson column, returning error bound $1.85 \times 10^{-10}$ in $Y$ and second moment of area $I_{xx} = \mathbf{22,500,000.0\text{ mm}^4}$ ($2.25 \times 10^7\text{ mm}^4$) in $X$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.85\times 10^{-10}, X: 22500000.0)$.

### Workbook Problem 8.18: Physical-Optics Aperture Integral & Radar Cross Section (RCS)

Physical-optics surface phase integration over a square flat conducting plate of side $a = 0.50\text{ m}$ ($x \in [-0.25, +0.25]\text{ m}$) at X-band radar wavelength $\lambda = 0.03\text{ m}$ ($10\text{ GHz}$) integrates the illuminated surface area $A = \int_{-0.25}^{0.25} a\,dx = 0.25\text{ m}^2$ to determine the peak specular Radar Cross Section:
$$\sigma_{\max} = \frac{4\pi}{\lambda^2}\left[\int_{-a/2}^{a/2} a\,dx\right]^2 = \frac{4\pi A^2}{\lambda^2} = \frac{4\pi (0.25^2)}{0.0009} \approx \mathbf{872.6646\text{ m}^2} \quad (\mathbf{29.41\text{ dBsm}})$$

At normal incidence (broadside flash), every surface patch across the conducting plate scatters in coherent phase, so the Kirchhoff physical-optics integral reduces to integrating the constant transverse width `0.5` in `{{key:EQN}}` over `0.25 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 0.25` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.5000, Y: -0.2500, X: 0.2500)$. Executing `{{key:∫}} X` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.5000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.2500)$ yields the illuminated aperture area $A = 0.2500\text{ m}^2$, and applying the Fraunhofer diffraction gain factor $4\pi A^2/\lambda^2$ (`{{key:x²}} 4 {{key:×}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 0.03 {{key:x²}} {{key:÷}}`, $(T: 0.5000, Z: 0.5000, Y: 0.0000, X: 872.6646)$) reveals why a half-meter flat plate appears to an X-band radar as an $\mathbf{872.6646\text{ m}^2}$ target ($\mathbf{29.41\text{ dBsm}}$ via `{{key:LOG}} 10 {{key:×}}`, $(T: 0.5000, Z: 0.5000, Y: 0.0000, X: 29.4085)$).

### Workbook Problem 8.19: Thermal Expansion Clearance of Cryogenic Propellant Line

When a 304 stainless-steel LOX/LN2 transfer line of length $L = 12.0\text{ m}$ is chilled from ambient $T_1 = 293\text{ K}$ down to liquid nitrogen boiling temperature ($T_2 = 77\text{ K}$), the instantaneous thermal expansion coefficient follows $\alpha(T) = (5.5022 + 0.04158\,T)\times 10^{-6}\text{ K}^{-1}$:
$$\Delta L = L \int_{293}^{77} \alpha(T) \, dT = 12.0 \int_{293}^{77} (5.5022 + 0.04158\,T)\times 10^{-6} \, dT = 12.0 \times (-0.002850) = \mathbf{-34.20\text{ mm}}\text{ (axial contraction)}$$
**Engineering Insight:** Staging initial ambient temperature ($a = 293\text{ K}$) in Register $Y$ and cryogenic terminal temperature ($b = 77\text{ K}$) in Register $X$ before pressing `{{key:∫}} T` automatically produces the negative sign indicating contraction, reminding bellows designers to provide at least $35\text{ mm}$ of axial expansion-joint travel.

Because lattice specific heat and anharmonic phonon vibrations freeze out near liquid-nitrogen temperatures (Debye $T^3$ regime), $\alpha(T)$ varies strongly across the cooldown range. Defining `T 0.04158 {{key:×}} 5.5022 {{key:+}} 1 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}` in `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating in the physical cooling direction `293 {{key:ENTER}} 77` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 293.0000, X: 77.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} T` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.05\times 10^{-13}, X: -0.002850)$ yields the integrated thermal strain $\varepsilon_{\text{th}} = -0.002850$, which scales over the $12\text{ m}$ manifold (`12 {{key:×}} 1000 {{key:×}}`, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.05\times 10^{-13}, X: -34.2000)$) to an axial shrinkage of $\Delta L = \mathbf{-34.20\text{ mm}}$.

### Workbook Problem 8.20: Planetary Atmospheric Jeans Escape Flux

Integrating the Maxwell-Boltzmann molecular velocity distribution above a planet's escape velocity $v_{\text{esc}}$ (with exobase density $n_0 = 1.0 \times 10^{13}\text{ m}^{-3}$, thermal speed $v_{\text{th}} = 6,356.7\text{ m/s}$, and Jeans parameter $\lambda = (v_{\text{esc}}/v_{\text{th}})^2 = 9.50$) yields the outward molecular escape flux at the exobase:
$$F_J = \frac{n_0 v_{\text{th}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}} \int_{\lambda}^{\infty} u e^{-u} \, du = \frac{n_0 v_{\text{th}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}} (1 + \lambda) e^{-\lambda} = \left(1.7932 \times 10^{16}\right)(10.50) e^{-9.50} \approx \mathbf{1.4102 \times 10^{13}\text{ molecules/(m}^2\cdot\text{s)}}$$

In planetary aeronomy, only molecules in the high-energy Maxwell-Boltzmann tail ($u = v^2/v_{\text{th}}^2 \ge \lambda$) located above the collisionless exobase can escape into interplanetary space. Entering the dimensionless energy-tail integrand `U {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}} U {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating from the escape threshold $\lambda = 9.5$ to the negligible upper tail $u = 35$ (`9.5 {{key:ENTER}} 35`, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 9.5000, X: 35.0000)$) via `{{key:∫}} U` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 4.15\times 10^{-12}, X: 0.000786)$ evaluates $(1 + \lambda)e^{-\lambda} = 0.0007863$; scaling by the upward effusive flux $n_0 v_{\text{th}}/(2\sqrt{\pi})$ (`6.3567 {{key:E}} 16 {{key:×}} {{key:π}} {{key:√x}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:÷}}`, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 4.15\times 10^{-12}, X: 1.4102\times 10^{13})$) determines the Jeans escape rate $F_J = \mathbf{1.4102 \times 10^{13}\text{ molecules/(m}^2\cdot\text{s)}}$.

### Workbook Problem 8.21: Torsional Rigidity of an Elliptical Shaft (Saint-Venant Warping Integral)

Integrating Saint-Venant warping shear stress over an elliptical shaft cross-section with semiaxes $a = 40\text{ mm}$ and $b = 25\text{ mm}$ reduces to the normalized cross-sectional integral $\int_{-1}^{1}(1 - u^2)^{3/2}\,du = \frac{3\pi}{8} \approx 1.178097$, scaled by $\frac{8 a^3 b^3}{3(a^2 + b^2)}$:
$$J = \frac{8 a^3 b^3}{3(a^2 + b^2)} \int_{-1}^{1} (1 - u^2)^{3/2} \, du = \frac{\pi a^3 b^3}{a^2 + b^2} = \frac{\pi (64,000)(15,625)}{1,600 + 625} \approx \mathbf{1,411,951.76\text{ mm}^4}$$

Unlike a circular shaft, an elliptical bar under torsion undergoes out-of-plane axial warping $\psi(x,y) \propto xy$, reducing its torsional stiffness below the polar moment of inertia $I_p$. Entering the normalized Prandtl stress-function integrand `U {{key:x²}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}} 1.5 {{key:yˣ}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating over `1 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 1` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: -1.0000, X: 1.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} U` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 2.85\times 10^{-10}, X: 1.178097)$ evaluates the dimensionless geometric shape factor $3\pi/8 = 1.178097$, which scales by the semiaxis prefactor $\frac{8 a^3 b^3}{3(a^2 + b^2)}$ (`8 {{key:×}} 3 {{key:÷}} 40 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:yˣ}} {{key:×}} 25 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:yˣ}} {{key:×}} 40 {{key:x²}} 25 {{key:x²}} {{key:+}} {{key:÷}}`, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.85\times 10^{-10}, X: 1411951.76)$) to the Saint-Venant torsion constant $J = \mathbf{1,411,951.76\text{ mm}^4}$.

### Workbook Problem 8.22: Second-Order Chemical Batch Reactor Residence Time

For an isothermal second-order reaction ($-r_A = k C_{A0}^2 (1-x)^2$) with characteristic rate product $k C_{A0} = \frac{1}{15}\text{ min}^{-1}$ ($0.06667\text{ min}^{-1}$), integrating the design equation from conversion $x = 0$ to $X = 0.75$ gives the required batch residence time:
$$t = \frac{1}{k C_{A0}} \int_0^{0.75} \frac{dx}{(1-x)^2} = 15.0 \left[ \frac{X}{1-X} \right]_{X=0.75} = 15.0 \times \frac{0.75}{0.25} = \mathbf{45.0\text{ minutes}}$$

Because bimolecular collision frequency drops quadratically as reactant $A$ is consumed ($-r_A \propto (1-x)^2$), each additional percentage point of conversion requires disproportionately longer reactor holding time. Entering the Levenspiel batch-design integrand `1 X {{key:-}} {{key:x²}} {{key:1/x}} 15 {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating over `0 {{key:ENTER}} 0.75` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 15.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.7500)$ via `{{key:∫}} X` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 15.0000, Y: 3.12\times 10^{-11}, X: 45.0000)$ yields the exact batch holding time $t = \mathbf{45.0000\text{ minutes}}$, matching the direct stack evaluation of $15 X/(1-X)$ via `0.75 {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}} {{key:÷}} 15 {{key:×}}` $(T: 45.0000, Z: 45.0000, Y: 45.0000, X: 45.0000)$.

### Workbook Problem 8.23: Terzaghi Soil Consolidation Degree Integral

Integrating excess pore-water pressure dissipation $u(z, T_v)/u_0 = \frac{4}{\pi}\sin\!\left(\frac{\pi\zeta}{2}\right) e^{-\pi^2 T_v / 4}$ across normalized clay drainage depth $\zeta = z/H_d \in [0, 2]$ at dimensionless time factor $T_v = 0.50$ yields average settlement consolidation:
$$U(T_v) = 1 - \frac{1}{2H_d}\int_0^{2H_d} \frac{u(z, T_v)}{u_0} \, dz \approx 1 - \frac{8}{\pi^2} e^{-\pi^2 (0.50) / 4} \approx \mathbf{76.40\%}$$

In Karl Terzaghi's one-dimensional consolidation theory, foundation settlement tracks the spatial average of pore-water dissipation across a doubly-drained clay stratum. With radian mode (`{{key:RAD}}`) active and the spatial eigenmode `Z {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:÷}} {{key:SIN}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:π}} {{key:÷}}` loaded into `{{key:EQN}}`, integrating across the normalized layer thickness `0 {{key:ENTER}} 2` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} Z` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.94\times 10^{-11}, X: 0.810569)$ recovers the fundamental Fourier coefficient $8/\pi^2 = 0.810569$. Attenuating by the diffusive decay factor $e^{-\pi^2(0.50)/4}$ and taking the complement (`{{key:π}} {{key:x²}} 0.5 {{key:×}} 4 {{key:÷}} {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}} {{key:×}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}}`, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.94\times 10^{-11}, X: 0.7640)$) establishes that $U(0.50) = \mathbf{76.40\%}$ of ultimate primary settlement has completed.

### Workbook Problem 8.24: Schuler-Tuned Inertial Navigation Period & Gravity Integral

Integrating gravitational restoring torque around Earth's mean radius $R_E = 6,371\text{ km}$ yields the classic Schuler pendulum period $T_S = 2\pi\sqrt{R_E / g_0} = \mathbf{84.4\text{ minutes}}$, while vertical rocket ascent velocity over burn time $t_b = 6.0\text{ s}$ integrates net acceleration $a_{\text{net}}(t) = 10.266667\,t - g_0$ (ideal Tsiolkovsky impulse $\int_0^6 10.266667\,t\,dt = 184.8000\text{ m/s}$ minus the gravity loss integral $\int_0^{t_b} g_0 \, dt = g_0 t_b$):
$$v(t_b) = \int_0^6 \left(10.266667\,t - 9.80665\right) dt = 184.8000 - 58.8399 = \mathbf{125.9601\text{ m/s}}$$

During vertical boost, an inertial measurement unit (IMU) senses specific force $a_{\text{thrust}}(t)$ while navigation computer firmware subtracts gravitational acceleration $g_0$ before integrating velocity. Entering `T 10.266667 {{key:×}} 9.80665 {{key:-}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating over `0 {{key:ENTER}} 6` $(T: 0.0000, Z: -9.8066, Y: 0.0000, X: 6.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} T` $(T: 0.0000, Z: -9.8066, Y: 0.0000, X: 125.9601)$ gives net vertical burnout speed $v = \mathbf{125.9601\text{ m/s}}$, which decomposes directly on the stack into ideal thrust impulse minus integrated gravity loss (`{{res:sc32_08_calc_08:keys}}` $\implies \mathbf{{{res:sc32_08_calc_08:value}}\text{ m/s}}$).

### Workbook Problem 8.25: Supersonic Sears-Haack Minimum Wave-Drag Body

A minimum-drag Sears-Haack fuselage of length $L = 30.0\text{ m}$ ($u = 2x/L \in [-1, +1]$) and maximum mid-body cross-sectional area $S_{\max} = \frac{16 V}{3\pi L} = 2.546479\text{ m}^2$ has cross-sectional area profile $S(u) = S_{\max}(1 - u^2)^{3/2}$. Integrating the cross-sectional area over the fuselage length recovers total volume $V = \frac{L}{2}\int_{-1}^{1} S_{\max}(1 - u^2)^{3/2}\,du = 45.0\text{ m}^3$, and evaluating the von Kármán slender-body wave-drag integral at flight dynamic pressure $q_\infty = 25,000\text{ Pa}$ yields:
$$D_{\text{wave}} = q_\infty \frac{128 V^2}{\pi L^4} = 25,000 \times \frac{128(2025)}{\pi(810,000)} \approx \mathbf{2,546.48\text{ N}}$$
**Engineering Insight:** Because wave drag scales with $L^{-4}$, lengthening the nose-to-tail fineness ratio by just $10\%$ reduces supersonic wave drag by $1 - 1.10^{-4} = 31.7\%$.

By shaping the longitudinal cross-sectional area distribution as $S(u) = S_{\max}(1 - u^2)^{3/2}$, the Sears-Haack body minimizes the von Kármán supersonic acoustic source integral for a fixed enclosed volume $V$. Programming the differential volume element `U {{key:x²}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}} 1.5 {{key:yˣ}} 38.197186 {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating from nose to tail `1 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 1` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 38.1972, Y: -1.0000, X: 1.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} U` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 38.1972, Y: 4.12\times 10^{-10}, X: 45.0000)$ confirms enclosed volume $V = 45.0000\text{ m}^3$; applying the slender-body wave-drag scaling $128 q_\infty V^2 / (\pi L^4)$ (`{{key:x²}} 128 {{key:×}} 25000 {{key:×}} 30 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:yˣ}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} {{key:÷}}`, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 4.12\times 10^{-10}, X: 2546.4791)$) determines the supersonic wave drag $D_{\text{wave}} = \mathbf{2,546.48\text{ N}}$.

### Workbook Problem 8.26: Stratospheric Ozone Column Integration (Dobson Units)

Satellite UV backscatter sounding across the stratosphere ($z \in [5, 35]\text{ km}$ with step $h = 15\text{ km}$) measures ozone concentration $D(5) = 3.0\text{ DU/km}$ near the tropopause, $D(20) = 13.0\text{ DU/km}$ at the stratospheric peak, and $D(35) = 5.0\text{ DU/km}$ in the upper stratosphere, corresponding to the quadratic vertical profile $D(z) = -0.04\,z^2 + 1.6667\,z - 4.3333\text{ DU/km}$. Integrating $D(z)$ via `{{key:∫}} Z` or Romberg's parabolic column $R(1,1) = \frac{h}{3}[D(5) + 4 D(20) + D(35)]$ yields the exact total column abundance:
$$\Omega = \int_{5}^{35} D(z) \, dz = \frac{15}{3}\left[ 3.0 + 4(13.0) + 5.0 \right] = \mathbf{300.0\text{ Dobson Units}} \equiv \mathbf{8.0601 \times 10^{22}\text{ molecules/m}^2}$$
*(where $1\text{ DU} = 2.6867 \times 10^{20}\text{ molecules/m}^2$, equivalent to a $0.01\text{ mm}$ layer of pure ozone at STP).*

Because the first Richardson extrapolation column $R(1,1)$ is algebraically identical to Simpson's $1/3$ rule, Romberg quadrature integrates quadratic atmospheric profiles with zero truncation error at level $n = 1$. Defining `Z 0.04 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}} 1.666667 {{key:+}} Z {{key:×}} 4.333333 {{key:-}}` in `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating across `5 {{key:ENTER}} 35` $(T: 0.0000, Z: -4.3333, Y: 5.0000, X: 35.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} Z` $(T: 0.0000, Z: -4.3333, Y: 0.0000, X: 300.0000)$ yields total ozone abundance $\Omega = \mathbf{300.0\text{ DU}}$, which matches the 3-node Simpson combination `3 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:ENTER}} 13 {{key:×}} {{key:+}} 5 {{key:+}} 15 {{key:×}} 3 {{key:÷}}` $(T: 300.0000, Z: 300.0000, Y: 300.0000, X: 300.0000)$ and converts via `2.6867 {{key:E}} 20 {{key:×}}` $(T: 300.0000, Z: 300.0000, Y: 300.0000, X: 8.0601\times 10^{22})$ to an overhead molecular column of $N_{\text{O3}} = \mathbf{8.0601 \times 10^{22}\text{ molecules/m}^2}$.

### Workbook Problem 8.27: High-Speed Sprint: Romberg Richardson Extrapolation on $\int_0^4 3x^2\,dx$

Evaluate $\int_0^4 3x^2 \, dx = [x^3]_0^4 = 4^3 = \mathbf{64.0}$ via `{{key:∫}} X` and verify how the first Richardson extrapolation column $R(1,1) = \frac{4 R(1,0) - R(0,0)}{3}$ cancels the trapezoidal truncation error on $f(x) = 3x^2$ ($R(0,0) = 96.0$ at $h_0 = 4$, $R(1,0) = 72.0$ at $h_1 = 2$):

Whereas a single trapezoid $R(0,0) = 96.0$ overestimates the concave-up parabola $3x^2$ by $32.0$ and two trapezoids $R(1,0) = 72.0$ overestimate it by $8.0$ (exactly one-fourth the error), Richardson extrapolation weights $R(1,0)$ by $4/3$ and subtracts $R(0,0)/3$ to annihilate the $O(h^2)$ error term completely. Entering `X {{key:x²}} 3 {{key:×}}` into `{{key:EQN}}` and integrating over `0 {{key:ENTER}} 4` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 4.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} X` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 64.0000)$ returns $\mathbf{64.000000}$, identical to the manual Richardson combination `72 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:×}} 96 {{key:-}} 3 {{key:÷}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 64.0000, X: 64.0000)$.
**Analytical Stack Verification:** {{res:sc32_08_calc_05:keys}} $\implies \mathbf{{{res:sc32_08_calc_05:value}}}$.

### Workbook Problem 9.1: Gaussian Normal PDF Peak at the Mean ($Z = 0$)

Calculate the maximum height (mode) of the normal distribution for $\mu = 100, \sigma = 15$ at $x = 100$:

$$f(100) = \frac{1}{15\sqrt{2\pi}} e^0 = \frac{1}{15\sqrt{2\pi}}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_01:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_01:value}}`**

At the distribution centroid $x = \mu$, the quadratic Mahalanobis distance $(x - \mu)^2/\sigma^2$ vanishes ($e^0 = 1$), so the peak probability density is governed solely by the normalization factor $(\sigma\sqrt{2\pi})^{-1}$. Staging $\sigma = 15.0$ via `15 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 15.0000, X: 15.0000)$, forming the Gaussian radical `2 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} {{key:√x}}` ($\sqrt{2\pi} \approx 2.5066$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 15.0000, X: 2.5066)$), multiplying by $\sigma$ via `{{key:×}}` ($\sigma\sqrt{2\pi} \approx 37.5994$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 37.5994)$), and inverting with `{{key:1/x}}` resolves the modal density $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.02659615})$ in 7 keystrokes without auxiliary storage.

---

### Workbook Problem 9.2: Gaussian Normal PDF at $Z = +2.0$ ($x = 130$)

Calculate the probability density for a score two standard deviations above the mean ($x = 130$):

$$f(130) = \frac{1}{15\sqrt{2\pi}} e^{-2^2 / 2} = \frac{e^{-2}}{15\sqrt{2\pi}}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_02:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_02:value}}`**

At two standard deviations above the mean ($Z = +2.0$), the Gaussian ordinate attenuates by $e^{-Z^2/2} = e^{-2} \approx 0.135335$ relative to the mode. Evaluating the exponential attenuation factor `2 {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.1353)$, constructing the dispersion normalizer $15\sqrt{2\pi} \approx 37.5994$ in $X$ while $e^{-2}$ waits in $Y$ (`15 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} {{key:√x}} {{key:×}}`, $(T: 0.1353, Z: 0.1353, Y: 0.1353, X: 37.5994)$), and dividing via `{{key:÷}}` determines the $2\sigma$ tail ordinate $(T: 0.1353, Z: 0.1353, Y: 0.1353, X: \mathbf{0.00359940})$.

### Workbook Problem 9.3: Cumulative Normal Probability Critical Z-Score ($Z = 1.96$)

Evaluate $\Phi(1.96)$ for the two-tailed 95% confidence interval ($\alpha = 0.05$):

$$\int_0^{1.96} e^{-t^2/2} \, dt = 1.1906427$$
Scaling by $1/\sqrt{2\pi}$: $1.1906427 \times 0.39894228 = 0.475000$.
Cumulative probability: $0.500000 + 0.475000 = \mathbf{0.975000}$ ($97.50\%$).

To verify Ronald Fisher's classic $1.96\sigma$ benchmark for a two-sided $95\%$ confidence interval ($\alpha = 0.05$, leaving $2.5\%$ in each tail), keep the unnormalized Gaussian kernel $e^{-x^2/2}$ (`X {{key:x²}} 2 {{key:÷}} {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}}`) in `{{key:EQN}}` mode and integrate over `0 {{key:ENTER}} 1.96` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.9600)$ via `{{key:∫}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 1.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.19064270)$, which places the converged integral $1.19064270$ in Register $X$ and the Romberg truncation estimate ($2.04 \times 10^{-12}$) in Register $Y$. Normalizing by $\sqrt{2\pi}$ (`2 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} {{key:√x}} {{key:÷}}`) isolates the half-interval probability $0.47500000$, and adding the lower half-plane area (`0.5 {{key:+}}`) confirms $\Phi(1.96) = \mathbf{0.97500000}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.97500000})$ ($97.50\%$).

Calculate semester GPA given courses with credits $c_i$ and grades $g_i$:
* Calculus: 4 credits, Grade 3.7
* Physics: 4 credits, Grade 3.3
* Literature: 3 credits, Grade 4.0
* Chemistry Lab: 1 credit, Grade 3.0

Accumulate $(y = \text{credits}, x = \text{grade})$:
1. `4 {{key:ENTER}} 3.7 {{key:Σ+}}`
2. `4 {{key:ENTER}} 3.3 {{key:Σ+}}`
3. `3 {{key:ENTER}} 4.0 {{key:Σ+}}`
4. `1 {{key:ENTER}} 3.0 {{key:Σ+}}`
5. Weighted GPA: `{{key:x̄w}}` $\rightarrow$ **`3.5833`**.

Because `{{key:Σ+}}` accumulates the cross-product $\Sigma(x_i y_i)$ in Register $\Sigma xy$ and the weight sum $\Sigma y_i$ in Register $\Sigma y$, staging credit hours $c_i$ in $Y$ and grade values $g_i$ in $X$ enables `{{key:x̄w}}` to evaluate the credit-weighted GPA $\Sigma(c_i g_i)/\Sigma c_i$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{3.5833})$ in a single keystroke.

### Workbook Problem 9.14: Concrete Compressive Strength Aging

Compressive strength follows logarithmic curing: $\sigma(t) = a + b \ln(t)$.
* $(t = 3\text{ days}, \sigma = 18.0\text{ MPa})$
* $(t = 7\text{ days}, \sigma = 26.5\text{ MPa})$
* $(t = 14\text{ days}, \sigma = 33.2\text{ MPa})$
* $(t = 28\text{ days}, \sigma = 40.0\text{ MPa})$

Stack entry: $(y = \sigma, x' = \ln t)$.
1. `18.0 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
2. `26.5 {{key:ENTER}} 7 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
3. `33.2 {{key:ENTER}} 14 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
4. `40.0 {{key:ENTER}} 28 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
5. Slope: `{{key:m}}` $\rightarrow$ **`9.836 MPa`**
6. Intercept: `{{key:b}}` $\rightarrow$ **`7.261 MPa`**
7. Predict 56-day strength: `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_15:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_15:value}}`**.

Portland cement hydration slows logarithmically as calcium-silicate-hydrate gel diffuses around unhydrated clinker grains ($\sigma(t) = b + m\ln t$). Transforming curing age $t$ via `{{key:LN}}` prior to `{{key:Σ+}}` yields the semi-log hydration rate `{{key:m}}` $= 9.8361\text{ MPa}$, and feeding $\ln(56)$ into the linear predictor (`56 {{key:LN}} {{key:ŷ}}`) extrapolates the 56-day structural cylinder strength $(T: 0.0000, Z: 11.7585, Y: 0.9911, X: 49.6697)$.

* $(G = 500, I_{sc} = 4.54\text{ A})$

### Workbook Problem 9.16: Centrifugal Pump Head Loss Curve

Pump head follows $H = H_0 - A Q^2$. Linearize via $(y = H, x' = Q^2)$:
* $(Q = 10\text{ gpm}, H = 98.0\text{ ft})$
* $(Q = 20\text{ gpm}, H = 92.0\text{ ft})$
* $(Q = 30\text{ gpm}, H = 82.0\text{ ft})$

1. `98.0 {{key:ENTER}} 10 {{key:x²}} {{key:Σ+}}`
2. `92.0 {{key:ENTER}} 20 {{key:x²}} {{key:Σ+}}`
3. `82.0 {{key:ENTER}} 30 {{key:x²}} {{key:Σ+}}`
4. Shutoff head $H_0 = b$: `{{key:b}}` $\rightarrow$ **`100.0 ft`**
5. Loss coefficient $A = -m$: `{{key:m}}` $\rightarrow$ **`-0.020 ft/gpm²`**.

Squaring volumetric discharge $Q$ via `{{key:x²}}` before pressing `{{key:Σ+}}` maps the parabolic centrifugal pump characteristic $H(Q) = H_0 - A Q^2$ into a linear regression against $Q^2$: `{{key:b}}` recovers the zero-flow shutoff head $H_0 = 100.0000\text{ ft}$, while `{{key:m}}` isolates the internal impeller hydraulic resistance slope $m = \mathbf{-0.0200\text{ ft/gpm}^2}$ (with $H_0 = 100.0000\text{ ft}$ lifted into Register $Y$).

### Workbook Problem 9.17: Laminar Pipe Friction vs. Reynolds Number

Laminar Darcy friction $f = 64 / Re \implies \ln f = \ln 64 - \ln Re$.
* $(Re = 400, f = 0.160)$
* $(Re = 800, f = 0.080)$
* $(Re = 1600, f = 0.040)$

1. `.160 {{key:LN}} 400 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
2. `.080 {{key:LN}} 800 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
3. `.040 {{key:LN}} 1600 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
4. Slope: `{{key:m}}` $\rightarrow$ **`-1.000000`**
5. Intercept: `{{key:b}} {{key:eˣ}}` $\rightarrow$ **`64.0000`** ($e^b$).

On a Moody diagram below the critical transition $Re < 2100$, Hagen-Poiseuille viscous shear produces a straight log-log line of slope $-1$. Accumulating $(\ln f_i, \ln Re_i)$ confirms the exact reciprocal exponent `{{key:m}}` $= -1.000000$ and recovers the Poiseuille laminar constant $e^b = \mathbf{64.0000}$ in Register $X$ (with $m = -1.0000$ in Register $Y$) via `{{key:b}} {{key:eˣ}}`.

### Workbook Problem 9.18: Aerodynamic Drag vs. Airspeed

$F_d = C v^2 \implies \ln F_d = \ln C + 2 \ln v$.
* $(v = 10\text{ m/s}, F_d = 25\text{ N})$
* $(v = 20\text{ m/s}, F_d = 100\text{ N})$
* $(v = 30\text{ m/s}, F_d = 225\text{ N})$

1. `25 {{key:LN}} 10 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
2. `100 {{key:LN}} 20 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
3. `225 {{key:LN}} 30 {{key:LN}} {{key:Σ+}}`
4. Slope: `{{key:m}}` $\rightarrow$ **`2.000000`** (Exact quadratic velocity dependence)
5. Coefficient: `{{key:b}} {{key:eˣ}}` $\rightarrow$ **`0.2500 N/(m/s)²`**.

At high Reynolds numbers where pressure form drag dominates skin friction, aerodynamic drag scales quadratically with freestream velocity ($F_d = \frac{1}{2}\rho C_D A v^2 = C v^2$). Regressing $\ln F_d$ against $\ln v$ confirms the velocity exponent `{{key:m}}` $= 2.000000$ and extracts the dimensional drag factor $C = e^b = \mathbf{0.2500\text{ N/(m/s)}^2}$ in Register $X$ (with $m = 2.0000$ in Register $Y$) via `{{key:b}} {{key:eˣ}}`.

Given $L = 2.0\text{ m}, I = 5 \times 10^{-6}\text{ m}^4$:
* $(P = 500\text{ N}, \delta = 0.00127\text{ m})$
* $(P = 1000\text{ N}, \delta = 0.00254\text{ m})$

   `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_16:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_16:value}}`** ($210\text{ GPa}$, Structural Steel).

Regressing tip deflection $\delta$ against applied point load $P$ determines the flexural compliance $m = d\delta/dP = L^3/(3EI) = 2.54 \times 10^{-6}\text{ m/N}$ in Register $X$, which inverts via $E = L^3/(3Im)$ directly on the stack to identify structural steel with Young's modulus $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 2.54 \times 10^{-6}, X: \mathbf{2.0997 \times 10^{11}}\text{ Pa})$ ($210\text{ GPa}$).

* $(t = 1\text{ hr}, C = 45.2\text{ mg/L})$
* $(t = 3\text{ hr}, C = 24.8\text{ mg/L})$
* $(t = 6\text{ hr}, C = 10.1\text{ mg/L})$

Calculate stock volatility Beta ($\beta$) against market benchmark:
* Month 1: Market excess return $x = 2.0\%$, Stock excess return $y = 3.1\%$
* Month 2: $x = -1.5\%, y = -2.2\%$
* Month 3: $x = 4.0\%, y = 5.8\%$
* Month 4: $x = -3.0\%, y = -4.4\%$

2. `-2.2 {{key:ENTER}} -1.5 {{key:Σ+}}`
3. `5.8 {{key:ENTER}} 4.0 {{key:Σ+}}`
4. `-4.4 {{key:ENTER}} -3.0 {{key:Σ+}}`

6. Alpha $\alpha = b$: `{{key:b}}` $\rightarrow$ **`0.045%`**.

In William Sharpe's Capital Asset Pricing Model, regressing an equity's excess return $R_i - R_f$ ($Y$) against the market portfolio's excess return $R_m - R_f$ ($X$) partitions total return into systematic market sensitivity $\beta = \text{Cov}(R_i, R_m)/\text{Var}(R_m)$ (`{{key:m}}`, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{1.4676})$) and idiosyncratic risk-adjusted outperformance, or Jensen's Alpha $\alpha$ (`{{key:b}}` $= 0.045\%$).

### Workbook Problem 9.22: Vector Graphic Scatter Plotting & Trendline Overlay

*Accessible via Right Shift `{{key:↱}}` then `{{key:PLOT}}`*

After accumulating bivariate datasets, pressing `{{key:PLOT}}` transitions the high-contrast dot-matrix display to the 64-point vector curve engine:
* The independent variable $x$ scales along the horizontal axis ($X_{\min} \to X_{\max}$).
* The dependent variable $y$ scales along the vertical axis ($Y_{\min} \to Y_{\max}$).
* Each accumulated point appears as a $2\times 2$ pixel reticle.
* The linear regression line $y = m x + b$ is rendered as an uninterrupted 1-pixel vector trendline across the display field.
* Navigation:
  * Press `{{key:4}}` to pan left, `{{key:6}}` to pan right.
  * Press `{{key:8}}` to pan up, `{{key:2}}` to pan down.
  * Press `{{key:+}}` to zoom in, `{{key:-}}` to zoom out.
  * Press `{{key:C}}` to exit plot mode and return to the RPN stack.

As Francis Anscombe famously demonstrated with Anscombe's Quartet, summary regression statistics ($m, b, r$) alone cannot distinguish a true linear relationship from high-leverage outliers or unmodeled curvature. Launching `{{key:PLOT}}` overlays the least-squares vector trendline $y = mx + b$ directly across the $2\times 2$ pixel data reticles while preserving the operational stack registers $(T, Z, Y, X)$ untouched in background memory; panning (`{{key:4}}`/`{{key:6}}`, `{{key:8}}`/`{{key:2}}`) and zooming (`{{key:+}}`/`{{key:-}}`) inspect residual structure visually before `{{key:C}}` returns to the four-register stack $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0000)$.

### Workbook Problem 9.23: Residual Analysis and Standard Error of the Estimate ($s_e$)

In linear regression $y = m x + b$, the standard error of the estimate measures the dispersion of observed values around the regression line:

$$s_e = \sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{n - 2}} = \sqrt{\frac{\Sigma y^2 - b \Sigma y - m \Sigma xy}{n - 2}}$$

For the pressure sensor dataset from Textbook Showcase Problem 9.1:
* $n = 4$, degrees of freedom $n - 2 = 2$
* $\Sigma y = 12.45$, $\Sigma y^2 = 46.5075$, $\Sigma xy = 373.5$
* $m = 0.1245$, $b = 0.0000$

Compute residual sum of squares:
$$\text{SS}_{\text{res}} = 46.5075 - 0.0000(12.45) - 0.1245(373.5) = 46.5075 - 46.50075 = 0.00675\text{ V}^2$$
$$s_e = \sqrt{\frac{0.00675}{2}} = \sqrt{0.003375} \approx 0.05809\text{ V}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_23:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_23:value}}`**

Because the six statistical registers already hold the exact cross-product moments, the residual sum of squares $\text{SS}_{\text{res}} = \Sigma y^2 - b\Sigma y - m\Sigma xy$ can be evaluated without re-visiting individual observations ($b = 0$ here). Staging $\Sigma y^2$ via `46.5075 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 46.5075, X: 46.5075)$ and subtracting the regression sum of squares $m \Sigma xy$ via `0.1245 {{key:ENTER}} 373.5 {{key:×}} {{key:-}}` isolates $\text{SS}_{\text{res}} = 0.00675\text{ V}^2$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.00675)$; normalizing by $n - 2 = 2$ degrees of freedom (`2 {{key:÷}}`, $s_e^2 = 0.003375$) and taking the square root (`{{key:√x}}`) determines the root-mean-square calibration scatter $s_e = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_23:value}}\text{ V}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: {{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_23:value}})$.
