### Workbook Problem 9.24: Binomial Distribution Probability via Combinatorics `{{key:Cn,r}}`

In an automated manufacturing testing line, component defect probability is $p = 0.03$. In a batch of $n = 20$ components, calculate the probability of observing exactly $k = 2$ defective units:
$$P(X = 2) = \binom{20}{2} (0.03)^2 (0.97)^{18}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_07:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_07:value}}`**; `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_08:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_08:value}}`**

In Bernoulli lot acceptance sampling, the number of distinct defect pairings across $n = 20$ items is counted by the binomial coefficient `20 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:PROB}} {{key:Cn,r}}` ($\binom{20}{2} = \mathbf{190}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 190.0000)$). Multiplying by the defective pair probability $p^2$ via `.03 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}}` ($190 \times 0.0009 = 0.1710$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.1710)$) and weighting by the survival probability of the remaining 18 good units (`.97 {{key:ENTER}} 18 {{key:yˣ}} {{key:×}}`, $0.97^{18} \approx 0.5779$) resolves the exact binomial mass $P(X = 2) = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_08:value}}}$ ($9.88\%$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: {{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_08:value}})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 9.25: Hypergeometric Sampling Without Replacement

In a batch of $N = 50$ aerospace fasteners, $K = 6$ are defective. A quality inspector selects $n = 5$ fasteners at random without replacement. Find the probability that exactly $k = 1$ selected fastener is defective:
$$P(X = 1) = \frac{\binom{K}{k} \binom{N - K}{n - k}}{\binom{N}{n}} = \frac{\binom{6}{1} \binom{44}{4}}{\binom{50}{5}}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_24:keys}}`; `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_25:keys}}`; `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_26:keys}} {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow$ **`0.384426`**

When sampling a finite aerospace lot without replacement ($n/N = 10\%$), each draw alters the defect concentration of the remaining population, requiring the exact hypergeometric ratio of combinatorial counts. Evaluating `6 {{key:ENTER}} 1 {{key:PROB}} {{key:Cn,r}}` ($\binom{6}{1} = 6.0000$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 6.0000)$), multiplying by the conforming combinations `44 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:PROB}} {{key:Cn,r}} {{key:×}}` ($\binom{44}{4} = 135,751 \implies 814,506$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 814506.0000)$), and dividing by the total sample space `50 {{key:ENTER}} 5 {{key:PROB}} {{key:Cn,r}} {{key:÷}}` ($\binom{50}{5} = 2,118,760$) determines $P(X = 1) = \mathbf{0.384426}$ ($38.44\%$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.384426)$.

### Workbook Problem 9.26: Log-Normal Aerosol Droplet Size Distribution

In medical nebulizer sprays, aerosol droplet diameters $D$ follow a log-normal distribution with geometric mean $\mu_{\ln} = \ln(4.5\text{ }\mu\text{m}) = 1.504077$ and geometric standard deviation $\sigma_{\ln} = 0.35$.
Calculate the probability density for a droplet of diameter $D = 5.0\text{ }\mu\text{m}$:
$$f(D) = \frac{1}{D \sigma_{\ln} \sqrt{2\pi}} \exp\left( -\frac{(\ln D - \mu_{\ln})^2}{2\sigma_{\ln}^2} \right)$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_27:keys}}`; `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_28:keys}}`; `{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_10:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_10:value}}`**

Because multiplicative breakup of liquid sheets in a pneumatic nebulizer produces normal scatter in $\ln D$ rather than $D$, the Jacobian transformation $d(\ln D)/dD = 1/D$ introduces an explicit diameter prefactor into the log-normal density. Evaluating the squared log-ratio `5.0 {{key:LN}} 4.5 {{key:LN}} {{key:-}} {{key:x²}}` ($(\ln 5.0 - \ln 4.5)^2 \approx 0.011101$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.011101)$), dividing by $2\sigma_{\ln}^2$ and exponentiating via `.35 {{key:x²}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:÷}} {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}}` ($\exp(-0.045310) \approx 0.955701$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.955701)$), and normalizing by $D\sigma_{\ln}\sqrt{2\pi}$ (`5.0 {{key:ENTER}} .35 {{key:×}} 2 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} {{key:√x}} {{key:×}} {{key:÷}}`) resolves $f(5.0) = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_10:value}}\ \mu\text{m}^{-1}}$ $(T: 0.9557, Z: 0.9557, Y: 0.9557, X: {{res:sc32_09_statistics_curve_fitting_seq_10:value}})$.

## Chapter 10: Computer Science, Bases & 36-Bit Integers — Extended Engineering & Competition Problems {#chapter-10}

*Accompanies [Chapter 10 of Thinking in the Stack](../manuals/sc32/10-computer-science-bases.md) (24 Worked Engineering Problems)*

### Workbook Problem 10.1: IPv4 CIDR /27 Subnet (30 Usable Hosts)

A server has IP `10.20.30.142 /27`.
* $10 = \text{0Ah}, 20 = \text{14h}, 30 = \text{1Eh}, 142 = \text{8Eh} \implies \text{0A141E8Eh}$.
* Subnet Mask `/27`: $27$ ones, $5$ zeros $\implies$ block size $2^5 = 32_{10} = \text{20h}$ ($255.255.255.224$).

| Octet 4 Representation | Upper 3 Subnet Bits | Lower 5 Host Bits | Hex / Decimal Value |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :--- |
| **Host Octet 4** | `100` | `01110` | `8Eh` ($142_{10}$) |
| **Subnet Mask (`/27`)** | `111` | `00000` | `E0h` ($224_{10}$) |
| **Network Base (`÷R 20h × 20h`)** | `100` | `00000` | `80h` ($128_{10}$) |
| **Broadcast (`+ 1Fh`)** | `100` | `11111` | `9Fh` ($159_{10}$) |

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:**
1. `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_07:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_07:value}}`** (`10.20.30.128`)
2. Wildcard mask: $32 - 27 = 5$ bits $\implies 2^5 - 1 = 31_{10} = \text{1Fh}$.
3. Broadcast: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_08:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_08:value}}`** (`10.20.30.159`)
4. Usable host range: **`10.20.30.129` through `10.20.30.158`** ($30$ usable hosts).

In CIDR block allocation, a `/27` prefix reserves 27 upper network bits and leaves $32 - 27 = 5$ host bits, partitioning each `/24` into eight 32-address (`20h`) blocks. Staging the packed address `0A141E8E {{key:ENTER}}` (`10.20.30.142`) in `{{key:HEX}}` mode $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{A141E8Eh}, X: \text{A141E8Eh})$ and dividing by the block stride `20 {{key:÷R}}` splits the 27-bit network prefix quotient $\text{50A0F4h}$ into Register $X$ and the 5-bit host index $\text{Eh}$ ($14_{10}$) into Register $Y$ $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{Eh}, X: \text{50A0F4h})$. Multiplying by the stride `20 {{key:×}}` aligns the network base `A141E80h` (`10.20.30.128`), and adding the 5-bit wildcard mask `1F {{key:+}}` ($2^5 - 1 = 31_{10}$) establishes the directed broadcast address $\mathbf{\text{A141E9Fh}}$ (`10.20.30.159`).

---

### Workbook Problem 10.2: IPv4 Point-to-Point /30 Subnet (WAN Link)

A WAN router serial link connects two routers using `172.16.5.9 /30`.
* $172 = \text{ACh}, 16 = \text{10h}, 5 = \text{05h}, 9 = \text{09h} \implies \text{AC100509h}$.
* Subnet Mask `/30`: $30$ ones, $2$ zeros $\implies$ block size $2^2 = 4_{10} = \text{4h}$ ($255.255.255.252$).

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:**
1. `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_09:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_09:value}}`** (`172.16.5.8`)
2. Wildcard: $2^2 - 1 = 3 = \text{03h}$.
3. Broadcast: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_10:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_10:value}}`** (`172.16.5.11`)
4. Router A interface: `172.16.5.9`, Router B interface: `172.16.5.10` ($2$ usable hosts).

Point-to-point WAN links traditionally use `/30` prefixes (stride $2^2 = 4\text{h}$) to conserve IPv4 address space while providing exactly two routable endpoints between network and broadcast boundaries. Entering `AC100509 {{key:ENTER}}` in `{{key:HEX}}` mode and partitioning with `4 {{key:÷R}}` separates host offset $1\text{h}$ in Register $Y$ from the 30-bit prefix quotient $\text{2B040142h}$ in Register $X$ $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 1\text{h}, X: \text{2B040142h})$; scaling back by `4 {{key:×}}` recovers the wire network ID $\mathbf{\text{AC100508h}}$ (`172.16.5.8`), and adding the 2-bit wildcard `3 {{key:+}}` determines the link broadcast address $\mathbf{\text{AC10050Bh}}$ (`172.16.5.11`).

### Workbook Problem 10.3: IPv4 Host Route (/32) and Loopback

A BGP loopback interface is assigned `198.51.100.1 /32`.
* In Hex: IP is `C6336401h`, Mask is `FFFFFFFFh` ($255.255.255.255$).
* Network ID = Host IP = Broadcast = `198.51.100.1` (`C6336401h`).
* Usable hosts: $2^{32-32} = 2^0 = \mathbf{1\text{ host}}$.

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` & `{{key:DEC}}` Modes:**
1. Load Host IP: `C6336401 {{key:ENTER}}` $\rightarrow$ `C6336401h`
2. Wildcard mask: `FFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}} FFFFFFFF {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow$ `0h` (`0.0.0.0`)
3. Broadcast Address: `{{key:+}}` $\rightarrow$ **`C6336401h`** (`198.51.100.1`)
4. Host Capacity: Switch to `{{key:DEC}}`, `2 {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:yˣ}}` $\rightarrow$ **`1`**.

Autonomous system border routers advertise `/32` loopback interfaces into BGP and OSPF so routing tables track a virtual node that never goes down when a single physical port flaps. With `C6336401 {{key:ENTER}}` staged on the stack $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{C6336401h}, X: \text{C6336401h})$, complementing the 32-bit host mask (`FFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}} FFFFFFFF {{key:-}}`) yields a zero wildcard offset `0h`, confirming via `{{key:+}}` that network, host, and broadcast collapse into the single address $\mathbf{\text{C6336401h}}$ ($2^0 = \mathbf{1}$ via `{{key:BASE}} {{key:DEC}} 2 {{key:ENTER}} 0 {{key:yˣ}}`).

### Workbook Problem 10.4: CIDR Route Aggregation (Supernetting)

An ISP wants to summarize four contiguous subnets into a single route:
* Subnet 1: `192.168.16.0 /24` (`C0A81000h`)
* Subnet 2: `192.168.17.0 /24` (`C0A81100h`)
* Subnet 3: `192.168.18.0 /24` (`C0A81200h`)
* Subnet 4: `192.168.19.0 /24` (`C0A81300h`)

Inspect the third octet:
$16 = 00010000_2$, $17 = 00010001_2$, $18 = 00010010_2$, $19 = 00010011_2$.
The upper 6 bits ($000100_2$) are identical across all four routes!
New network mask length: $16 + 6 = \mathbf{22\text{ bits}}$ (`/22`).
Supernet Mask: `255.255.252.0` (`FFFFFC00h`).
Summarized Route: **`192.168.16.0 /22`**.

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:**
1. Base Network ID: `C0A81000 {{key:ENTER}}` $\rightarrow$ `C0A81000h` (`192.168.16.0`)
2. Supernet block span: `4 {{key:ENTER}} 100 {{key:×}}` $\rightarrow$ `400h` ($1024_{10}$ addresses)
3. Wildcard Mask: `1 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow$ `3FFh` (`0.0.3.255`)
4. Broadcast Address: `{{key:+}}` $\rightarrow$ **`C0A813FFh`** (`192.168.19.255`)
5. Usable Hosts: `3FF {{key:ENTER}} 1 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow$ **`3FEh`** ($1022_{10}$ hosts).

CIDR route aggregation (RFC 4632) prevents global routing-table explosion by folding contiguous power-of-two `/24` blocks into a shorter prefix. Staging the aligned base network `C0A81000 {{key:ENTER}}` and evaluating the 4-block span `4 {{key:ENTER}} 100 {{key:×}}` (`400h` $= 1024_{10}$ addresses, $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: \text{C0A81000h}, Y: 4\text{h}, X: 400\text{h})$) followed by `1 {{key:-}}` derives the 10-bit supernet wildcard mask `3FFh` (`0.0.3.255`). Adding the wildcard to the waiting base address via `{{key:+}}` resolves the summarized broadcast boundary $\mathbf{\text{C0A813FFh}}$ (`192.168.19.255`), while `3FF {{key:ENTER}} 1 {{key:-}}` confirms $\mathbf{\text{3FEh}}$ ($1022_{10}$) usable host addresses.

### Workbook Problem 10.5: Wildcard Mask Generation via 32-Bit Complement Subtraction

In Cisco ACLs, calculate the wildcard mask for `/26` ($255.255.255.192$):
1. In `{{key:HEX}}`: Mask is `FFFFFFC0h`.
2. Subtract from the 32-bit all-ones mask `FFFFFFFFh`: key `FFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}} FFFFFFC0 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow$ `3Fh`.
3. In `{{key:DEC}}`: `3Fh` = **`63`**.
4. Dotted decimal wildcard: `0.0.0.63`.

Because a bitwise one's complement on an $N$-bit unsigned word is algebraically identical to subtracting from $2^N - 1$, subtracting the `/26` netmask `FFFFFFC0h` from the 32-bit all-ones constant `FFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{FFFFFFFFh}, X: \text{FFFFFFFFh})$ via `FFFFFFC0 {{key:-}}` $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{3Fh})$ isolates the inverted access-control wildcard `3Fh`, which `{{key:BASE}} {{key:DEC}}` displays as $\mathbf{63}$ (`0.0.0.63`).

### Workbook Problem 10.6: Embedded GPIO Pin Bit-Clearing (`-`)

Clear Bit 11 (Disable motor enable line, currently $1$) in register holding `0x00002F80`. Bit 11 weight: $2^{11} = 2048_{10} = \text{800h}$.

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:** `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_12:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_12:value}}`**

When a target bit $k$ is known to be asserted ($1$), subtracting its positional weight $2^k$ executes a borrow-free bitwise clear (`R & ~(1 << k)`). Staging `2F80 {{key:ENTER}}` in `{{key:HEX}}` mode and subtracting Bit 11's weight $2^{11} = \text{800h}$ via `800 {{key:-}}` clears the motor-enable bit cleanly without disturbing adjacent flags, leaving $\mathbf{\text{2780h}}$ in Register $X$ $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{2780h})$.

### Workbook Problem 10.7: Multi-Bit Field Insertion (Read-Modify-Write)

In a peripheral timer control register currently holding `0x4521`, update the 4-bit prescaler field at bits [11:8] (currently `5h`) with value `0xA` ($10_{10}$), without altering any of the surrounding configuration bits.

| Insertion Stage | Binary Nibbles (Bits 15–0) | Hexadecimal Value |
| :--- | :---: | :---: |
| **Original Timer Register** | `0100 0101 0010 0001` | `4521h` |
| **Subtract Old Field (`- 500h`)** | `0100 0000 0010 0001` | `4021h` |
| **Shifted New Field (`A × 100h`)** | `0000 1010 0000 0000` | `0A00h` |
| **Updated Register (`+`)** | `0100 1010 0010 0001` | `4A21h` |

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:**
1. Clear existing field: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_17:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_17:value}}`
2. Prepare shifted new field: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_18:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_18:value}}`
3. Merge fields: `{{key:+}}` $\rightarrow$ **`4A21h`**

Firmware read-modify-write sequences (`R = (R & ~MASK) | (VAL << SHIFT)`) translate cleanly onto the hexadecimal stack: subtracting the old field `4521 {{key:ENTER}} 500 {{key:-}}` zeroes bits [11:8] to leave the masked register `4021h` in Register $X$ $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{4021h})$, left-shifting the new nibble via `A {{key:ENTER}} 100 {{key:×}}` forms `A00h` in $X$ while `4021h` waits in $Y$ $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: \text{4021h}, X: \text{A00h})$, and pressing `{{key:+}}` merges the updated control word $\mathbf{\text{4A21h}}$.

### Workbook Problem 10.8: RGB565 to RGB888 Color Unpacking & Expansion

Given packed RGB565 word `0xE5B2`, extract individual color channels using `{{key:÷}}` and `{{key:÷R}}` and expand them to standard 8-bit values ($0 \dots 255$):
* Red channel (bits 15–11): divide by $2^{11} = \text{800h}$
* Green channel (bits 10–5): take remainder modulo $\text{800h}$ via `{{key:÷R}} {{key:x⇄y}}`, then divide by $2^5 = \text{20h}$
* Blue channel (bits 4–0): take remainder modulo $\text{20h}$ via `{{key:÷R}} {{key:x⇄y}}`

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` & `{{key:DEC}}` Modes:**
1. Extract Red: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_22:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_22:value}}`** ($28_{10}$)
   * Expand in `{{key:DEC}}`: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_23:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_23:value}}`**
2. Extract Green: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_24:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_24:value}}`** ($45_{10}$)
   * Expand in `{{key:DEC}}`: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_25:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_25:value}}`**
3. Extract Blue: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_26:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_26:value}}`** ($18_{10}$)
   * Expand in `{{key:DEC}}`: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_27:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_27:value}}`**
* Unpacked TrueColor: **`RGB(230, 182, 148)`** (`#E6B694`).

Because `{{key:÷R}}` simultaneously returns the upper bitfield quotient in Register $X$ and the lower bitfield remainder in Register $Y$, a 16-bit RGB565 pixel can be unpacked either channel-by-channel (`E5B2 {{key:ENTER}} 800 {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow \mathbf{\text{1Ch}} = 28_{10} \implies R = \mathbf{230}$; `E5B2 {{key:ENTER}} 800 {{key:÷R}} {{key:x⇄y}} 20 {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow \mathbf{\text{2Dh}} = 45_{10} \implies G = \mathbf{182}$; `E5B2 {{key:ENTER}} 20 {{key:÷R}} {{key:x⇄y}}` $\rightarrow \mathbf{\text{12h}} = 18_{10} \implies B = \mathbf{148}$) or in a **single-entry cascade**: keying `E5B2 {{key:ENTER}} 800 {{key:÷R}} {{key:x⇄y}} 20 {{key:÷R}}` unpacks all three channels onto the stack at once (`Z: 1Ch` Red, `Y: 12h` Blue, `X: 2Dh` Green) with `E5B2` typed only once!

### Workbook Problem 10.9: RGBA4444 16-Bit Texture Color Packing

In embedded OpenGL ES graphics engines, 16-bit RGBA4444 allocates 4 bits per channel:
Pack channels $R=14$ (`Eh`), $G=9$ (`9h`), $B=3$ (`3h`), $A=15$ (`Fh`):
$$(R \times \text{1000h}) + (G \times \text{100h}) + (B \times \text{10h}) + A$$

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:** `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_28:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_28:value}}`**

In 4-bit-per-channel RGBA4444 sprite textures, each channel aligns with a single hexadecimal digit weight ($16^3, 16^2, 16^1, 16^0$). Shifting Red via `E {{key:ENTER}} 1000 {{key:×}}` ($\text{E000h}$, $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{E000h})$) and accumulating Green (`9 {{key:ENTER}} 100 {{key:×}} {{key:+}}` $\rightarrow \text{E900h}$), Blue (`3 {{key:ENTER}} 10 {{key:×}} {{key:+}}` $\rightarrow \text{E930h}$), and Alpha (`F {{key:+}}`) assembles the packed 16-bit texel $\mathbf{\text{E93Fh}}$ in Register $X$.

### Workbook Problem 10.10: Two's Complement Negation Proof ($(2^{36} - 1 - X) + 1$)

Verify that subtracting $X$ from all ones (`FFFFFFFFFh`, 9 hex digits for 36 bits) and adding $1$ produces the exact mathematical two's complement negation `{{key:+/-}}`:
* Key in $42$ in `{{key:DEC}}` mode and press `{{key:+/-}}`: produces **`-42`**.
* Switch to `{{key:BASE}} {{key:HEX}}`: displays as `FFFFFFFD6h` ($36$-bit two's complement representation, $2^{36} - 42$).
* Verify by one's complement subtraction: `FFFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}} 2A {{key:-}} 1 {{key:+}}` $\rightarrow$ `FFFFFFFD6h` (`-42` in `{{key:DEC}}`).

In an $N$-bit two's complement ALU ($N = 36$ on the SC-32), additive inverse is defined modulo $2^{36}$ via $-X \equiv (2^{36} - 1 - X) + 1 \pmod{2^{36}}$. Negating `42 {{key:+/-}}` in `{{key:DEC}}` mode yields $\mathbf{-42}$, whose underlying 36-bit word appears in `{{key:BASE}} {{key:HEX}}` as $\mathbf{\text{FFFFFFFD6h}}$—identical to forming the 9-nibble one's complement `FFFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}} 2A {{key:-}}` ($\text{FFFFFFFD5h}$) and incrementing by the carry-in `1 {{key:+}}`.

### Workbook Problem 10.11: Arithmetic Right Shift via Signed Integer Division

For signed negative integer $-64$:
* In `{{key:DEC}}`: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_35:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_35:value}}`** (Exact arithmetic right shift by 2 bits, sign-extended).

Whereas a logical right shift (`>>`) pads vacated upper bits with zeros, an arithmetic right shift (`>>>` or `ASR`) replicates the sign bit (Bit 35) to preserve negative two's complement magnitudes. Staging `64 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}}` in `{{key:DEC}}` mode and dividing by $2^2 = 4$ via `4 {{key:÷}}` yields $\mathbf{-16}$ $(T: 0, Z: 0, Y: 0, X: -16)$, which `{{key:BASE}} {{key:HEX}}` confirms as the sign-extended 36-bit word $\mathbf{\text{FFFFFFFFF0h}}$.

### Workbook Problem 10.12: Parity Bit Generation

Data byte `0x3B` ($00111011_2$, five ones $\implies$ odd parity).
For even parity, the MSB parity bit (Bit 7, weight $2^7 = 128_{10} = \text{80h}$) must be set to $1$:
`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_37:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_37:value}}`**.

Because the 7-bit ASCII payload `3Bh` ($00111011_2$) contains an odd population count of five `1` bits, asserting the MSB parity bit ($2^7 = \text{80h}$) via `3B {{key:ENTER}} 80 {{key:+}}` brings total population weight to six `1` bits, forming the even-parity UART frame byte $\mathbf{\text{BBh}}$ ($10111011_2$).

### Workbook Problem 10.13: Longitudinal Redundancy Check (LRC)

Calculate LRC across message bytes `0x02`, `0x41`, `0x42`.

`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_38:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_38:value}}`.

LRC = $(256 - \text{Sum}) \bmod 256$: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_39:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_39:value}}`**.

In Intel HEX and Modbus ASCII serial framing, the Longitudinal Redundancy Check (LRC) byte is the two's complement of the modulo-256 payload sum, ensuring that the sum of payload plus LRC equals `00h` $(\bmod\text{ 100h})$ at the receiver. Summing the three frame bytes `2 {{key:ENTER}} 41 {{key:+}} 42 {{key:+}}` yields `85h` $(T: 0\text{h}, Z: 0\text{h}, Y: 0\text{h}, X: \text{85h})$, and subtracting from `100h` (`100 {{key:ENTER}} 85 {{key:-}}`, or `100 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}}` without re-typing `85`) generates the transmitted LRC byte $\mathbf{\text{7Bh}}$.

### Workbook Problem 10.14: Ethernet FCS 32-Bit Inversion & Frame Check Sequence Post-Conditioning

In IEEE 802.3 Ethernet frame transmission, the final 32-bit CRC remainder (Frame Check Sequence, FCS) is post-conditioned prior to transmission by inverting all 32 bits (one's complement). Given an Ethernet polynomial check state of `0xEDB88320`:
1. Invert the 32-bit word by subtracting from the all-ones mask `FFFFFFFFh`:
$$FFFFFFFF_{16} - EDB88320_{16} = \mathbf{12477CDF}_{16} \ (306,678,751_{10})$$

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:**
1. Load 32-bit inversion mask: `FFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}}` $\rightarrow$ `FFFFFFFFh`
2. Subtract Ethernet generator polynomial: `EDB88320 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow$ **`12477CDFh`**
3. Switch radix to inspect in decimal: `{{key:BASE}} {{key:DEC}}` $\rightarrow$ **`306678751`**.

In IEEE 802.3 CRC-32 hardware, post-inverting the shift-register state against `FFFFFFFFh` ensures that appending trailing zero bytes to a valid frame alters its CRC. Subtracting the reversed generator polynomial `EDB88320h` from `FFFFFFFF {{key:ENTER}}` via `EDB88320 {{key:-}}` produces the one's-complement word $\mathbf{\text{12477CDFh}}$, which `{{key:BASE}} {{key:DEC}}` renders losslessly as $\mathbf{306,678,751}$.

### Workbook Problem 10.15: SPI Clock Prescaler Selection

A SPI peripheral runs from a $75.0\text{ MHz}$ bus clock. The connected flash memory specifies a maximum operating frequency of $10.0\text{ MHz}$. The SPI prescaler offers powers of two: $2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128$.
Determine the optimal prescaler divider and actual SPI operating frequency.

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_53:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ $7.5$.

Dividing the $75.0\text{ MHz}$ peripheral bus clock by the $10.0\text{ MHz}$ NOR-flash ceiling yields the minimum divider ratio $7.5000$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 7.5000)$. Rounding up to the next binary prescaler tap $2^{\lceil \log_2 7.5 \rceil} = \mathbf{8}$ (`8 {{key:ENTER}}`) and dividing the bus clock via `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_54:keys}}` establishes the safe operating SPI clock $f_{\text{SPI}} = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_54:value}}\text{ Hz}}$ ($9.375\text{ MHz}$).

### Workbook Problem 10.16: I2C Fast-Mode Plus SCL Timing Register Calculations

Target I2C clock frequency $f_{\text{SCL}} = 1.0\text{ MHz}$ ($1000\text{ ns}$ cycle time) derived from a $150.0\text{ MHz}$ core clock period ($T_{\text{clk}} = \frac{1}{150\times 10^6} \approx 6.6667\text{ ns}$).
Total clocks per SCL period: $150\text{ cycles}$.
In Fast-Mode Plus, asymmetric duty cycle allocates $t_{\text{LOW}} \ge 500\text{ ns}$ and $t_{\text{HIGH}} \ge 260\text{ ns}$.
Allocating $\frac{1}{3}$ high and $\frac{2}{3}$ low:
* $t_{\text{HIGH}} = 50\text{ cycles} \times 6.6667\text{ ns} = \mathbf{333.3\text{ ns}}$ ($> 260\text{ ns}$)
* $t_{\text{LOW}} = 100\text{ cycles} \times 6.6667\text{ ns} = \mathbf{666.7\text{ ns}}$ ($> 500\text{ ns}$).

**Keystrokes:**
1. Total cycles: `150 {{key:ENTER}} 1 {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow$ $150$ cycles
2. High phase clocks: `3 {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow$ $50$ cycles
3. Timing durations: `1000 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow$ **`333.3 ns`** ($t_{\text{HIGH}}$); `2 {{key:×}}` $\rightarrow$ **`666.7 ns`** ($t_{\text{LOW}}$).

Open-drain I2C buses require an asymmetric 1:2 high-to-low clock ratio at $1\text{ MHz}$ (Fast-Mode Plus) so active pull-down transistors hold `SCL` low long enough ($t_{\text{LOW}} \ge 500\text{ ns}$) after passive pull-up RC rise times. Dividing the $150\text{-cycle}$ period (`150 {{key:ENTER}} 1 {{key:÷}}`) by `3 {{key:÷}}` assigns $50\text{ cycles}$ to `SCLL_H` and $100\text{ cycles}$ to `SCLL_L`; partitioning the $1000\text{ ns}$ bit period (`1000 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:÷}}`) confirms $t_{\text{HIGH}} = \mathbf{333.3\text{ ns}} > 260\text{ ns}$ and `2 {{key:×}}` confirms $t_{\text{LOW}} = \mathbf{666.7\text{ ns}} > 500\text{ ns}$.

### Workbook Problem 10.17: Fixed-Point Q16.16 Arithmetic Multiplication

In digital signal processing without FPU acceleration, numbers are formatted as signed Q16.16 (16 integer bits, 16 fractional bits, scaling factor $2^{16} = 65,536$).
Multiply $A = 3.5$ and $B = 2.25$:
* $A = 3.5 \times 65536 = 229,376 = \text{38000h}$
* $B = 2.25 \times 65536 = 147,456 = \text{24000h}$
* Product formula: $C = (A \times B) \gg 16 = (A \times B) / 65536$.

**Keystrokes in `{{key:HEX}}` Mode:** `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_45:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_45:value}}`**

Because multiplying two Q16.16 fixed-point numbers $(A \cdot 2^{16})(B \cdot 2^{16}) = AB \cdot 2^{32}$ doubles the binary-point scaling to Q32.32, a 32-bit ALU would overflow on the intermediate product $\text{38000h} \times \text{24000h} = \text{7E0000000h}$. The SC-32's 36-bit integer datapath accommodates the full 35-bit intermediate product (`38000 {{key:ENTER}} 24000 {{key:×}}`) without truncation, and right-shifting by 16 bits (`10000 {{key:÷}}`) normalizes the result back to Q16.16 word $\mathbf{\text{7E000h}}$ ($516,096_{10} / 65,536 = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_46:value}}}$).

### Workbook Problem 10.18: Permutations ($nPr$) in Cryptography S-Box Design

Calculate the total number of ways to construct an ordered 4-element substitution key from a pool of 16 distinct nibbles:

$$P(16, 4) = \frac{16!}{(16 - 4)!} = 16 \times 15 \times 14 \times 13 = \mathbf{43,680}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_47:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_47:value}}`**

In block-cipher substitution-box (S-Box) construction, selecting an ordered sequence of $r = 4$ distinct 4-bit nibbles without replacement from the 16-element hexadecimal alphabet spans the falling factorial $16^{\underline{4}} = P(16, 4)$. Keying `16 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:PROB}} {{key:Pn,r}}` evaluates the exact keyspace cardinality $P(16, 4) = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_47:value}}}$ ($43,680$).

### Workbook Problem 10.19: Combinations ($nCr$) and Hamming Sphere

In a $[7, 4]$ Hamming code, calculate the number of possible 2-bit error patterns:

$$\binom{7}{2} = \frac{7!}{2! \times 5!} = \mathbf{21}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_48:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_48:value}}`**

In Richard Hamming's $[7, 4]$ single-error-correcting block code, the surface area of the Hamming sphere of radius $t = 2$ around any 7-bit codeword counts all unordered pairs of corrupted bit positions. Evaluating `7 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:PROB}} {{key:Cn,r}}` returns $\binom{7}{2} = \mathbf{{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_48:value}}}$ ($21$ distinct double-bit error patterns).

### Workbook Problem 10.20: Factorials and Stirling's Approximation Comparison

Calculate $15!$:
Exact: `{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_49:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_49:value}}`**.

Stirling's approximation: $\sqrt{2\pi n} (n/e)^n \approx 1.3004 \times 10^{12}$ (Error $< 0.6\%$).

Evaluating `15 {{key:PROB}} {{key:x!}}` computes the exact permutation count of a 15-element array, $\mathbf{{{res:sc32_10_computer_science_bases_seq_49:value}}}$ ($1,307,674,368,000$), whereas James Stirling's asymptotic leading term $\sqrt{2\pi n}(n/e)^n \approx 1.30043 \times 10^{12}$ underestimates the exact factorial by the series correction factor $e^{-1/(12n)} \approx 1 - \frac{1}{180}$ ($0.55\%$).

### Workbook Problem 10.21: Pseudo-Random Number Generation (`RAND`)

Generate 3 uniform random floats:
`{{key:PROB}} {{key:RAND}}` $\rightarrow$ $0.724194$
`{{key:PROB}} {{key:RAND}}` $\rightarrow$ $0.183022$
`{{key:PROB}} {{key:RAND}}` $\rightarrow$ $0.941581$.

Because `{{key:PROB}} {{key:RAND}}` is a non-consuming generator that enables stack lift before pushing each uniform variate $U_k \in [0, 1)$ into Register $X$, three consecutive invocations populate a 3-dimensional Monte Carlo sample vector directly across Registers $Z, Y,$ and $X$: the first press generates $U_1 = \mathbf{0.724194}$ in $X$, the second lifts $U_1 \to Y$ while placing $U_2 = \mathbf{0.183022}$ in $X$, and the third lifts $U_1 \to Z, U_2 \to Y$ while placing $U_3 = \mathbf{0.941581}$ in $X$.

### Workbook Problem 10.22: Pseudo-Random Die Roll Simulation

Simulate a standard 6-sided die roll:
`{{key:PROB}} {{key:RAND}} 6 {{key:×}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:PARTS}} {{key:IP}}` $\rightarrow$ Integer in $1 \dots 6$.

Mapping a continuous uniform variate $U \in [0, 1)$ (`{{key:PROB}} {{key:RAND}}`, e.g., $0.724194$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.724194)$) onto a discrete equiprobable integer set $\{1, \dots, 6\}$ uses the affine floor transformation $\lfloor 6U + 1 \rfloor$: scaling by the cardinality `6 {{key:×}}` ($4.345164 \in [0, 6)$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 4.345164)$), shifting the origin `1 {{key:+}}` ($5.345164 \in [1, 7)$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 5.345164)$), and truncating via `{{key:PARTS}} {{key:IP}}` yields the unbiased die face $\mathbf{5}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 5.0000)$.

Revenues grew from $\$42.50\text{M}$ in fiscal year 2024 to $\$51.85\text{M}$ in fiscal year 2025. Calculate the annual percentage growth rate:

$$\%CHG = \frac{51.85 - 42.50}{42.50} \times 100 = \mathbf{+22.00\%}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_09:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_09:value}}`**.

### Workbook Problem 11.4: Weighted Average Cost of Capital (WACC)

Equity capital $\$60.0\text{M}$ ($60\%$) at required equity return $K_e = 12.0\%$; Debt capital $\$40.0\text{M}$ ($40\%$) at pre-tax interest rate $K_d = 6.0\%$; Corporate income tax rate $t = 25\%$:
$$\text{WACC} = \left(\frac{E}{V}\right) K_e + \left(\frac{D}{V}\right) K_d (1 - t)$$

Under Modigliani-Miller corporate tax-shield accounting, interest expense is tax-deductible, reducing the effective cost of debt by $(1 - t)$. Computing the tax-shielded debt rate via `{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_19:keys}}` ($4.50\%$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 4.50)$) and weighting by the $40\%$ debt share (`.40 {{key:×}}` $= 1.80\%$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1.80)$), then evaluating the $60\%$ equity component via `{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_20:keys}}` ($7.20\%$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 1.80, X: 7.20)$) and summing with `{{key:+}}` establishes the blended corporate hurdle rate $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{9.00\%})$.

### Workbook Problem 11.5: Rule of 72 Doubling Time Precision

An investment yields $8.0\%$ annually. Compare the Rule of 72 mental estimate with exact compound TVM:
* Rule of 72 estimate: $72 / 8 = \mathbf{9.00\text{ years}}$.
* Exact TVM Doubling: `8 {{key:I%YR}} 1 {{key:+/-}} {{key:PV}} 0 {{key:PMT}} 2 {{key:FV}} {{key:N}}` $\rightarrow$ **`9.01 years`**.
* Error is under $0.11\%$.

Luca Pacioli's classic Rule of 72 (`72 {{key:ENTER}} 8 {{key:÷}}` $= 9.00\text{ years}$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 9.00)$) approximates $N = \ln(2)/\ln(1+i) \approx 69.31/i + 0.35$, making $72/i$ remarkably accurate near $i = 8\%$. Programming `8 {{key:I%YR}} 1 {{key:+/-}} {{key:PV}} 0 {{key:PMT}} 2 {{key:FV}}` $(T: 8.00, Z: -1.00, Y: 0.00, X: 2.00)$ and solving `{{key:N}}` yields the exact doubling horizon $\ln(2)/\ln(1.08) = \mathbf{9.0065\text{ years}}$ $(T: 8.00, Z: -1.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{9.01}\text{ years})$.

### Workbook Problem 11.6: Continuous Compounding ($A = P e^{rt}$)

Deposit $\$10,000.00$ in a treasury note yielding $5.50\%$ compounded continuously for 10 years:

$$A = 10,000 \times e^{0.055 \times 10} = 10,000 \times e^{0.55}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_21:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_21:value}}`**.

In the continuous compounding limit $\lim_{m \to \infty}(1 + r/m)^{mt} = e^{rt}$, evaluating the dimensionless yield-time exponent `.055 {{key:ENTER}} 10 {{key:×}}` ($rt = 0.55$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 0.55)$), exponentiating via `{{key:eˣ}}` ($e^{0.55} \approx 1.733253$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1.73)$), and scaling by initial principal `10000 {{key:×}}` determines the terminal value $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{\$17332.53})$.

### Workbook Problem 11.7: Effective Annual Rate (EAR) from Nominal APR

A revolving credit line charges nominal $21.99\%$ APR compounded daily ($m = 365$):

$$\text{EAR} = \left(1 + \frac{0.2199}{365}\right)^{365} - 1$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_22:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_22:value}}`**.

Converting a nominal APR into its true Effective Annual Rate (APY) reveals the compounding drag of daily interest accrual: dividing the nominal rate by the compounding frequency `.2199 {{key:ENTER}} 365 {{key:÷}} 1 {{key:+}}` forms the daily growth factor $1 + r/365 = 1.00060247$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1.000602)$, raising to the 365th power via `365 {{key:yˣ}}` yields $1.245870$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1.25)$, and `1 {{key:-}} 100 {{key:×}}` isolates the effective annual rate $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{24.59\%})$.
*(**Single-Entry Compounding Choreography:** Because `1 {{key:+}}` overwrites `LASTX` with `1`, you can avoid typing the compounding frequency `365` twice by duplicating it into Register $Y$ upfront: `365 {{key:ENTER}} .2199 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:yˣ}} 1 {{key:-}} 100 {{key:×}}` keeps `365` waiting in Register $Y$ while forming $1 + 0.2199/365$, so `365` is entered only once!)*

### Workbook Problem 11.21: Cash Conversion Cycle (CCC)

Days Inventory Outstanding $DIO = 45\text{ days}$; Days Sales Outstanding $DSO = 38\text{ days}$; Days Payables Outstanding $DPO = 52\text{ days}$:

$$\text{CCC} = DIO + DSO - DPO = 45 + 38 - 52 = \mathbf{31\text{ days}}$$

In working-capital treasury management, the Cash Conversion Cycle measures how many days cash remains tied up in inventory and customer receivables beyond trade supplier credit terms: `45 {{key:ENTER}} 38 {{key:+}} 52 {{key:-}}` resolves $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{31}\text{ days})$.

### Workbook Problem 11.22: Enterprise Value (EV) Calculation

Market capitalization $\$450\text{M}$, total debt $\$120\text{M}$, cash and equivalents $\$35\text{M}$:

$$\text{EV} = \text{Market Cap} + \text{Debt} - \text{Cash} = 450 + 120 - 35 = \mathbf{\$535.00M}$$

In mergers-and-acquisitions valuation, Enterprise Value represents the net takeover price of the operating business—acquiring all equity plus assuming gross debt, less liquid cash on the balance sheet (`450 {{key:ENTER}} 120 {{key:+}} 35 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow (T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{\$535.00M})$).

Annual tuition $\$35,000$ for 4 years ($N=4$, beginning of year `BEGIN` mode) at $5\%$ investment return:
Toggled to `BEGIN` mode: `4 {{key:N}} 5 {{key:I%YR}} 35000 {{key:PMT}} 0 {{key:FV}} {{key:PV}}` $\rightarrow$ **`-$130,417.84`**.

Because university bursars collect tuition at the start of each academic year (an *annuity due*), toggling `BEGIN` mode and storing `4 {{key:N}} 5 {{key:I%YR}} 35000 {{key:PMT}} 0 {{key:FV}}` $(T: 4.00, Z: 5.00, Y: 35000.00, X: 0.00)$ before pressing `{{key:PV}}` discounts only the three subsequent installments, yielding the required freshman-day endowment $(T: 4.00, Z: 5.00, Y: 35000.00, X: \mathbf{-\$130417.84})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 11.25: Net Present Value (NPV) Comparison of Capital Projects

A company evaluates two mutually exclusive industrial automation projects with a $12.0\%$ hurdle rate:
**Project Alpha:** Initial Cost $\$80,000.00$; Inflows: $\$35,000/\text{yr}$ for 3 years.
**Project Beta:** Initial Cost $\$120,000.00$; Inflows: $\$52,000/\text{yr}$ for 3 years:

$$\text{PV Annuity Factor} = \frac{1 - (1.12)^{-3}}{0.12} = \frac{1 - 0.71178}{0.12} \approx 2.40183$$
* Project Alpha: $35,000 \times 2.40183 - 80,000 = \$84,064.10 - \$80,000 = \mathbf{+\$4,064.10}$.
* Project Beta: $52,000 \times 2.40183 - 120,000 = \$124,895.22 - \$120,000 = \mathbf{+\$4,895.22}$.
Project Beta delivers $\$831.12$ more net present value to shareholders.

Because `N`, `I%YR`, and `FV` remain persistent across TVM evaluations, discounting Project Alpha (`3 {{key:N}} 12 {{key:I%YR}} 35000 {{key:+/-}} {{key:PMT}} 0 {{key:FV}} {{key:PV}}` $\rightarrow \$84,064.10$, $(T: 3.00, Z: 12.00, Y: -35000.00, X: 84064.10)$, less `80000 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow (T: 3.00, Z: 12.00, Y: -35000.00, X: \mathbf{+\$4064.10})$) requires only updating `PMT` for Project Beta (`52000 {{key:+/-}} {{key:PMT}} {{key:PV}}` $\rightarrow \$124,895.22$, $(T: 12.00, Z: -35000.00, Y: 4064.09, X: 124895.22)$, less `120000 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow (T: 3.00, Z: 12.00, Y: -52000.00, X: \mathbf{+\$4895.22})$).

---

### Workbook Problem 11.26: Capital Budgeting Profitability Index (PI)

Using the present value of inflows from Workbook Problem 11.25:

$$\text{Profitability Index (PI)} = \frac{\text{PV of Future Inflows}}{\text{Initial Investment}}$$
* Project Alpha: $84,064.10 \div 80,000.00 = \mathbf{1.0508}$ ($1.05\text{x}$)
* Project Beta: $124,895.22 \div 120,000.00 = \mathbf{1.0408}$ ($1.04\text{x}$)
Although Project Beta has higher absolute NPV, Project Alpha delivers higher capital efficiency per dollar invested!

When capital rationing constrains total CapEx budget, ranking projects by Profitability Index $\text{PI} = \text{PV}_{\text{inflows}}/I_0$ (`84064.10 {{key:ENTER}} 80000 {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow (T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{1.0508}\text{x})$ for Alpha versus `124895.22 {{key:ENTER}} 120000 {{key:÷}}` $\rightarrow (T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 1.05, X: \mathbf{1.0408}\text{x})$ for Beta) identifies which project generates more present value per dollar of scarce capital.

---

### Workbook Problem 11.28: Staircase Stringer Layout with 1/16" Carpentry Cuts

Total vertical rise is $105\text{-}1/2^{\prime\prime}$ ($105.5^{\prime\prime}$). Target riser height is $7\text{-}1/4^{\prime\prime}$ ($7.25^{\prime\prime}$):
1. Riser count: `{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_42:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ $14.55 \implies$ Round to **`15 risers`**.
2. Exact riser height: `{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_43:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_11_financial_tvm_seq_43:value}}`**.
3. Decompose to nearest $1/16^{\prime\prime}$:
   Fractional part: `7 {{key:-}} 16 {{key:×}}` $\rightarrow$ $0.5333 \approx 1/16^{\prime\prime} \implies$ Cut stringers at **`7-1/16"`** (exact rise $7.0625^{\prime\prime} \times 15 = 105.94^{\prime\prime}$, adjusted on bottom tread).

Dividing total floor-to-floor rise by the target riser height (`105.5 {{key:ENTER}} 7.25 {{key:÷}}` $= 14.55$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 14.55)$) indicates **`15 risers`**; dividing `105.5 {{key:ENTER}} 15 {{key:÷}}` yields the exact unit rise $7.0333^{\prime\prime}$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 14.55, X: 7.03)$, and converting the fractional part to sixteenths (`7 {{key:-}} 16 {{key:×}}` $= 0.5333/16^{\prime\prime}$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 14.55, X: 0.53)$) specifies a **`7-1/16"`** stringer gauge layout.

### Workbook Problem 11.29: SaaS Unit Economics — LTV : CAC Ratio in One Chain

A B2B software enterprise generates Average Revenue Per User $\text{ARPU} = \$250.00/\text{month}$ at an $80.0\%$ gross margin, with monthly revenue churn $c = 2.5\%$ ($0.025$) and Customer Acquisition Cost $\text{CAC} = \$1,600.00$:
$$\text{LTV} = \frac{\text{ARPU} \times \text{GM}\%}{\text{Churn}} = \frac{250 \times 0.80}{0.025} = \$8,000.00, \qquad \frac{\text{LTV}}{\text{CAC}} = \frac{8000}{1600} = \mathbf{5.00\text{x}}$$
**Boardroom RPN Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_11_exec_saas_ltv_cac:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_11_exec_saas_ltv_cac:value}}`**.

Modeling customer retention as a geometric survival series with monthly hazard rate $c = 0.025$ gives an expected customer lifetime of $1/c = 40\text{ months}$. Keying `250 {{key:ENTER}} 80 {{key:%}}` isolates monthly gross margin contribution $(\$200.00/\text{mo})$ in Register $X$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 250.00, X: 200.00)$, dividing by churn `0.025 {{key:÷}}` capitalizes that stream into Customer Lifetime Value ($\text{LTV} = \$8,000.00$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 250.00, X: 8000.00)$), and dividing by `1600 {{key:÷}}` yields the unit-economics multiple $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 250.00, X: \mathbf{5.00}\text{x})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 11.30: Operating Breakeven Volume ($Q_{\text{BE}}$)

A manufacturing line carries annual fixed overhead of $F = \$420,000.00$. Each unit sells for $P = \$85.00$ with variable unit cost $V = \$35.00$ (contribution margin $\$50.00/\text{unit}$):
$$Q_{\text{BE}} = \frac{F}{P - V} = \frac{420,000}{85 - 35}$$
**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_11_exec_breakeven_units:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_11_exec_breakeven_units:value}}`**.

In cost-volume-profit (CVP) accounting, each unit sold contributes $P - V$ toward absorbing fixed overhead $F$: staging `420000 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 420000.00, X: 420000.00)$, forming the unit contribution margin `85 {{key:ENTER}} 35 {{key:-}}` ($\$50.00/\text{unit}$ in $X$ while $F$ waits in $Y$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 420000.00, X: 50.00)$), and dividing via `{{key:÷}}` determines the operating breakeven volume $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{8400}\text{ units})$.

### Workbook Problem 11.31: Commercial Real Estate Net Operating Income (NOI) & Cap Rate

An apartment building generates Gross Scheduled Rent of $\$112,500.00/\text{year}$ with a $28.0\%$ operating expense + vacancy ratio, offered at an asking price of $\$1,250,000.00$:
$$\text{NOI} = 112,500 \times (1 - 0.28) = \$81,000.00, \qquad \text{Cap Rate} = \frac{\text{NOI}}{\text{Price}} \times 100$$
**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_11_realtor_cap_rate:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_11_realtor_cap_rate:value}}`**.

Deducting the $28\%$ vacancy and operating-expense reserve via `112500 {{key:ENTER}} 28 {{key:%}}` $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 112500.00, X: 31500.00)$ followed by `{{key:-}}` isolates Net Operating Income ($\text{NOI} = \$81,000.00$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 81000.00)$); dividing by the $\$1,250,000$ acquisition price (`1250000 {{key:÷}}` $= 0.0648$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 0.0648)$) and scaling by `100 {{key:×}}` determines the unlevered capitalization rate $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{6.48}\%)$.

Before executing Richard Brent's root finder ({{key:SOLVE}}), plot the polynomial to visually bracket roots:

$$f(x) = x^3 - 3x^2 - 9x + 5 = 0$$

1. Program in `{{key:EQN}}`: `X 3 {{key:yˣ}} 3 X {{key:x²}} {{key:×}} {{key:-}} 9 X {{key:×}} {{key:-}} 5 {{key:+}}`

3. Press `{{key:PLOT}}`.

| Plot Telemetry | Crosshair Readout | Curve Feature |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |

| **Crosshair Cursor** | `X = 0.500000000`, `Y = -0.125000000` | Pre-root bracket inspection prior to `{{key:SOLVE}}` |

The plot visually reveals:
* Local maximum near $x = -1.0$: $f(-1) = 10.0$
* Local minimum near $x = 3.0$: $f(3) = -22.0$
* Three distinct roots: Root 1 in $[-3, -2]$, Root 2 in $[0, 1]$, Root 3 in $[4, 5]$.
Tracing crosshair to $x = 0.50$ and pressing `{{key:ENTER}}` pushes $0.50$ onto the stack. Pressing `{{key:SOLVE}} X` converges to root $x = 0.5185$ in 3 iterations.

### Workbook Problem 12.4: High-Pass RC Filter Frequency Response

The high-pass filter frequency response is given by:

$$|H(f)| = \frac{f / f_c}{\sqrt{1 + (f / f_c)^2}}$$
For cutoff frequency $f_c = 1000\text{ Hz}$:
1. Program: `F 1000 {{key:÷}} {{key:STO}} A {{key:x²}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:√x}} {{key:RCL}} A {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}`
2. Domain: `0 {{key:STO}} "XMIN"`, `5000 {{key:STO}} "XMAX"`
3. Press `{{key:PLOT}}`.
The curve begins at $0$ at DC ($f=0$), crosses $0.707107$ at $f = 1000\text{ Hz}$, and asymptotically approaches $1.00$ at high frequencies.
**Octave-Above-Cutoff Check ($f = 2000\text{ Hz}$):** {{res:sc32_12_plot_06:keys}} $\implies \mathbf{{{res:sc32_12_plot_06:value}}}$.

In a first-order RC high-pass section, the capacitor blocks DC ($|H(0)| = 0$) and passes high-frequency ac components above the corner frequency $f_c = \frac{1}{2\pi R C} = 1000\text{ Hz}$. Evaluating the normalized frequency ratio at one octave above cutoff (`2000 {{key:ENTER}} 1000 {{key:÷}} {{key:STO}} A` $\rightarrow f/f_c = 2.00$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 2.000000)$), forming the impedance modulus $\sqrt{1 + (f/f_c)^2} = \sqrt{5}$ via `{{key:x²}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:√x}}` $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 2.236068)$, and dividing via `{{key:RCL}} A {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}` establishes the octave-above-cutoff attenuation factor $2/\sqrt{5}$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{0.894427}$, or $-0.97\text{ dB})$.
*(**Zero-Register Stack Alternative:** Duplicating the normalized frequency ratio on the stack via `2000 {{key:ENTER}} 1000 {{key:÷}} {{key:ENTER}} {{key:x²}} 1 {{key:+}} {{key:√x}} {{key:÷}}` keeps $f/f_c = 2.00$ waiting in Register $Y$ while $\sqrt{1 + (f/f_c)^2}$ forms in Register $X$, resolving $\mathbf{0.894427}$ without touching storage register `A`!)*

### Workbook Problem 12.8: Radioactive Exponential Decay Curve

Cobalt-60 activity decays according to $A(t) = 100 \times e^{-0.1315 t}$ ($t$ in years):
Plot over $t \in [0, 25\text{ years}]$:
Trace at $t = 5.27\text{ years}$ confirms $A = 50.0\%$ (the half-life).

With decay constant $\lambda = \frac{\ln 2}{t_{1/2}} = 0.1315\text{ yr}^{-1}$, evaluating the exponent at $t = 5.27\text{ yr}$ (`.1315 {{key:+/-}} 5.27 {{key:×}}` $= -0.693005 \approx -\ln 2$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: -0.693005)$), exponentiating via `{{key:eˣ}}` ($0.500072$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 0.500072)$), and scaling by `100 {{key:×}}` confirms the $50\%$ half-life crossing $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{50.01}\%)$.

### Workbook Problem 12.9: Logistic Population Growth S-Curve (Sigmoidal)

Ecological population growth with carrying capacity $K = 1000$, growth rate $r = 0.5$, initial population $P_0 = 50$:

$$P(t) = \frac{K}{1 + \left(\frac{K - P_0}{P_0}\right) e^{-r t}} = \frac{1000}{1 + 19 e^{-0.5 t}}$$
Plot over $t \in [0, 20]$: curve shows initial exponential growth, an inflection point at $P = 500$ ($t = \ln(19)/0.5 \approx 5.89$), and asymptotic approach to $1000$.

Pierre-François Verhulst's logistic equation $\frac{dP}{dt} = rP(1 - P/K)$ achieves its maximum absolute growth rate $\frac{rK}{4}$ exactly at the half-saturation inflection time $t_{\text{inf}} = \frac{\ln((K - P_0)/P_0)}{r} = \frac{\ln 19}{0.5} \approx 5.88894$: evaluating the damping term `.5 {{key:+/-}} 5.88894 {{key:×}} {{key:eˣ}} 19 {{key:×}}` yields $19 e^{-r t_{\text{inf}}} = 1.000000$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1.000000)$, adding `1 {{key:+}}` forms the denominator $2.000000$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 2.000000)$, and dividing into carrying capacity `1000 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}` confirms the inflection ordinate $K/2$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{500.000000})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 12.10: Weber-Fechner Acoustic Decibel Curve

Sound pressure level $L_p = 20 \log_{10}(P / P_0)$ where $P_0 = 20\text{ }\mu\text{Pa}$:
Logarithmic compression displays the human ear's vast dynamic range across acoustic pressures.

Because human auditory perception responds logarithmically to acoustic pressure amplitude, dividing a $20,000\text{ }\mu\text{Pa}$ ($0.02\text{ Pa}$) sound wave by the $20\text{ }\mu\text{Pa}$ hearing threshold via `20000 {{key:ENTER}} 20 {{key:÷}}` isolates the amplitude ratio $P/P_0 = 1000.00$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1000.00)$, taking the base-10 logarithm `{{key:LOG}}` yields $3.000000\text{ bels}$ of amplitude $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 3.000000)$, and scaling by `20 {{key:×}}` converts to sound pressure level $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{60.00}\text{ dB})$.

### Workbook Problem 12.11: Statistical Residual Plot ($y_i - \hat{y}_i$)

To verify that linear regression errors are normally distributed and homoscedastic, plot residuals:

$$e_i = y_i - \hat{y}(x_i)$$
* $e_1 = 1.25 - 0.1245(10) = 1.25 - 1.245 = \mathbf{+0.005\text{ V}}$
* $e_2 = 2.45 - 0.1245(20) = 2.45 - 2.490 = \mathbf{-0.040\text{ V}}$
* $e_3 = 3.80 - 0.1245(30) = 3.80 - 3.735 = \mathbf{+0.065\text{ V}}$
* $e_4 = 4.95 - 0.1245(40) = 4.95 - 4.980 = \mathbf{-0.030\text{ V}}$
Residual points scatter randomly above and below the zero-error axis, confirming absence of systematic non-linear bias.

Auditing regression residuals $e_i = y_i - \hat{y}_i$ ensures that transducer non-linearity is not masquerading under a high correlation coefficient ($r = 0.999565$). Staging the first measured output `1.25 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1.250000, X: 1.250000)$, evaluating the fitted value `10 {{key:ENTER}} 0.1245 {{key:×}}` ($\hat{y}_1 = 1.245000$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.000000, Y: 1.250000, X: 1.245000)$), and subtracting via `{{key:-}}` isolates $e_1 = y_1 - \hat{y}_1$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{+0.005000}\text{ V})$, while `2.45 {{key:ENTER}} 20 {{key:ENTER}} 0.1245 {{key:×}} {{key:-}}` yields $e_2$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0050, X: \mathbf{-0.040000}\text{ V})$.
*(**Zero-Re-Entry Residual & $SS_{\text{res}}$ Choreography:** Because the four calibration pairs from Textbook Showcase Problem 12.6 are already active in the statistical registers, you never need to re-type the slope `0.1245`: keying `1.25 {{key:ENTER}} 10 {{key:ŷ}} {{key:R↓}} {{key:-}}`—or parking the slope `0.1245` across the upper stack via `0.1245 {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}}`—evaluates each fitted voltage directly. Better still, squaring each residual in place (`{{key:x²}}`) as soon as it is formed accumulates the Residual Sum of Squares $SS_{\text{res}} = \sum e_i^2 = 0.005^2 + (-0.040)^2 + 0.065^2 + (-0.030)^2 = \mathbf{0.006750\text{ V}^2}$ directly on the stack without re-entering a single residual!)*

### Workbook Problem 12.12: Lissajous Figure 1:2 Harmonic Ratio

Signal frequencies $f_x = 100\text{ Hz}, f_y = 200\text{ Hz}$:

$$x(t) = \sin(t), \quad y(t) = \sin(2t)$$
The parametric trajectory traces a figure-8 ("infinity" symbol $\infty$).

Jules Antoine Lissajous's 1:2 harmonic curve satisfies the algebraic lemniscate relation $y^2 = 4x^2(1 - x^2)$, crossing the origin twice per fundamental cycle to form a figure-eight. In `{{key:RAD}}` mode, duplicating $t = \pi/6$ via `{{key:π}} 6 {{key:÷}} {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.523599, X: 0.523599)$, evaluating the second-harmonic ordinate `{{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:SIN}}` ($y = \sin(\pi/3) = \sqrt{3}/2 \approx 0.866025$ in $X$ while $t$ waits in $Y$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.523599, X: 0.866025)$), and swapping `{{key:x⇄y}} {{key:SIN}}` resolves the fundamental abscissa $x = \sin(\pi/6) = \mathbf{0.500000}$ in $X$ while preserving $y = 0.866025$ in $Y$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.866025, X: 0.500000)$.

### Workbook Problem 12.13: Acoustic Cardioid Microphone Polar Pattern

In studio acoustics, a cardioid directional microphone exhibits polar sensitivity over $\theta \in [0^\circ, 360^\circ]$:

$$r(\theta) = 2(1 - \cos\theta)$$

This profile produces a heart-shaped cardioid with a complete null ($r = 0$) at $\theta = 0^\circ$, side sensitivity $r = 2.0$ at $\theta = \pm 90^\circ$, and peak rear sensitivity at $\theta = 180^\circ$ ($r = 4.0$).

**Polar-to-Rectangular Vector Check:** To convert any $(x, y) = (1, 10)$ coordinate on a traced curve into polar magnitude $r = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$: {{res:sc32_12_plot_08:keys}} $\implies \mathbf{{{res:sc32_12_plot_08:value}}}$.

Combining an omnidirectional pressure element with a figure-eight velocity-gradient element creates the uniaxial cardioid pattern $r(\theta) = 2(1 - \cos\theta)$. Converting any traced Cartesian point $(x, y) = (1, 10)$ into radial distance via `10 {{key:x²}}` ($100.000000$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 100.000000)$), `1 {{key:x²}}` ($1.000000$ in $X$ with $100.000000$ in $Y$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 100.000000, X: 1.000000)$), `{{key:+}}` ($101.000000$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 101.000000)$), and `{{key:√x}}` resolves the Euclidean radius $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{10.049876})$—or in a single hardware operation via `10 {{key:ENTER}} 1 {{key:▸θ,r}}`!

### Workbook Problem 12.15: Gaussian Error Function ($\text{erf}$) Diffusion Profile

In Fickian heat and mass diffusion, concentration across a semi-infinite slab follows the Gaussian error function:

$$\text{erf}(x) = \frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}} \int_0^x e^{-t^2} dt$$

The sigmoidal curve transitions smoothly from $-1.0$ at $-\infty$ to $+1.0$ at $+\infty$, passing through $(0, 0)$ with central inflection slope $\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}} \approx \mathbf{1.128379}$ (`{{key:π}} {{key:√x}} 2 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}`).

Solving Fick's second law $\frac{\partial C}{\partial t} = D \frac{\partial^2 C}{\partial z^2}$ via Boltzmann's similarity variable $x = \frac{z}{2\sqrt{Dt}}$ yields the error-function profile whose steepest concentration gradient occurs at the interface $x = 0$: evaluating `{{key:π}} {{key:√x}}` ($\sqrt{\pi} \approx 1.772454$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 1.772454)$), staging `2 {{key:x⇄y}}` $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 2.000000, X: 1.772454)$, and dividing via `{{key:÷}}` establishes the Maclaurin leading-term slope $2/\sqrt{\pi}$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{1.128379})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 12.16: Wien Displacement Blackbody Radiation Curve

Planck's spectral radiance for a tungsten filament at $T = 3000\text{ K}$ obeys:

$$B(\lambda, T) \propto \frac{\lambda^{-5}}{e^{hc/(\lambda k_B T)} - 1}, \qquad \lambda_{\max} = \frac{b}{T}$$

The curve displays the steep short-wavelength ultraviolet cutoff (resolving the classical Rayleigh-Jeans catastrophe) and a peak spectral emission at $\lambda_{\max} = \frac{2897.77\,\mu\text{m}\cdot\text{K}}{3000\text{ K}} = \mathbf{0.9659\,\mu\text{m}}$ in the near-infrared.

Differentiating Max Planck's spectral radiance distribution with respect to wavelength yields the transcendental root $x = \frac{hc}{\lambda_{\max} k_B T} \approx 4.965114$, which defines Wilhelm Wien's displacement constant $b = 2897.77\,\mu\text{m}\cdot\text{K}$: dividing `2897.77 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 2897.770000, X: 2897.770000)$ by the filament temperature `3000 {{key:÷}}` locates the emission peak in the near-infrared at $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{0.965923}\,\mu\text{m})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 12.17: Jakob Bernoulli's Lemniscate

In polar coordinates, the locus of points whose product of distances from two foci is constant follows:

$$r^2 = 4 \cos(2\theta)$$

This relation renders a symmetric figure-eight ribbon spanning $r \in [0, 2.0]$ over $\theta \in [-45^\circ, +45^\circ]$ and $[135^\circ, 225^\circ]$, while regions where $\cos(2\theta) < 0$ are automatically clipped as imaginary branches by the vector plotter.

Jakob Bernoulli introduced the lemniscate ("decorated with ribbons") in *Acta Eruditorum* (1694) as the elastic curve of a bent rod and the locus of constant focal-distance product: evaluating at $\theta = 30^\circ$ via `30 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:×}}` ($2\theta = 60.0^\circ$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.000000, X: 60.000000)$), `{{key:COS}}` ($\cos 60^\circ = 0.500000$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 0.500000)$), and `{{key:√x}} 2 {{key:×}}` determines the polar radius $r = 2\sqrt{\cos 60^\circ} = \sqrt{2}$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{1.414214})$.

### Workbook Problem 12.18: Constant-Pitch Archimedean Spiral

In mechanical scroll compressors and vinyl groove geometry, the polar trajectory expands linearly with rotation angle $\theta$ (in radians):

$$r(\theta) = 0.5\,\theta$$

Each full $2\pi$ revolution increases the radius by a constant pitch $\Delta r = 0.5(2\pi) = \pi \approx \mathbf{3.1416\text{ mm}}$. Verify linear spiral growth across multiple revolutions on the dot-matrix grid.

Unlike a logarithmic spiral whose turn spacing grows geometrically, Archimedes' spiral $r(\theta) = a\theta$ maintains constant radial separation $\Delta r = 2\pi a$ between adjacent involute wraps: evaluating one full revolution `{{key:π}} 2 {{key:×}}` ($2\pi \approx 6.283185\text{ rad}$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.000000, X: 6.283185)$) and scaling by `0.5 {{key:×}}` confirms the uniform scroll pitch $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{3.141593}\text{ mm})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 12.19: Supersonic Prandtl-Meyer & Mach Cone Angle Curve

The supersonic shock wave half-angle $\mu(M)$ against Mach number $M \in [1.0, 5.0]$ in Degrees mode (`M {{key:1/x}} {{key:ASIN}}`) follows:

$$\mu(M) = \arcsin\left(\frac{1}{M}\right)$$

The curve starts at a normal sonic wave $\mu(1.0) = 90.0^\circ$, sweeps sharply through $\mu(2.0) = \mathbf{30.0^\circ}$, and narrows to a slender hypersonic cone $\mu(5.0) = \mathbf{11.537^\circ}$. Map compressible flow shock angle decay from sonic boom to hypersonic flight.

When a vehicle exceeds the speed of sound $a$, spherical acoustic wavefronts coalesce along Ernst Mach's conical envelope of half-angle $\sin\mu = a/v = 1/M$: at Mach 2, `2 {{key:1/x}}` ($0.500000$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 0.500000)$) followed by `{{key:ASIN}}` yields $\mu(2) = \mathbf{30.000000}^\circ$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{30.000000}^\circ)$, while at hypersonic Mach 5, `5 {{key:1/x}}` ($0.200000$ in $X$ with $30.0^\circ$ preserved in $Y$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 30.000000, X: 0.200000)$) followed by `{{key:ASIN}}` narrows the Mach cone to $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 30.000000, X: \mathbf{11.536959}^\circ)$.

### Workbook Problem 12.20: Thin-Airfoil Aerodynamic Lift Slope ($C_L$ vs. $\alpha$)

Classical thin-airfoil theory predicts section lift coefficient (for angle of attack $\alpha$ in radians, or $\frac{2\pi^2}{180}\alpha^\circ \approx 0.10966\,\alpha^\circ$):

$$C_L(\alpha) = 2\pi \alpha$$

Plotting over $\alpha \in [-5^\circ, +15^\circ]$ illustrates the linear inviscid lift regime up to $C_L(10^\circ) \approx \mathbf{1.0966}$ prior to boundary-layer flow separation.

Max Munk and Hermann Glauert's thin-airfoil theory establishes that any symmetric cambered section in inviscid subsonic flow exhibits a universal lift-curve slope of $\frac{dC_L}{d\alpha} = 2\pi\text{ rad}^{-1} = \frac{2\pi^2}{180}\text{ deg}^{-1} \approx 0.109662\text{ deg}^{-1}$: staging $\alpha = 10^\circ$ via `10 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 10.000000, X: 10.000000)$ and multiplying by `0.10966 {{key:×}}` predicts the pre-stall section lift coefficient $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{1.096600})$.

### Workbook Problem 12.21: Van der Waals Real Gas Isotherms

Real gas pressure across molar volume $V$ follows:

$$P(V) = \frac{R T}{V - b} - \frac{a}{V^2}$$

Isotherms below critical temperature $T_c$ display characteristic van der Waals cubic loops, illustrating liquid-gas phase coexistence.

Johannes Diderik van der Waals corrected the ideal gas law for finite molecular excluded volume ($b = 0.04\text{ m}^3/\text{mol}$) and intermolecular London dispersion attraction ($a = 0.36\text{ Pa}\cdot\text{m}^6/\text{mol}^2$): evaluating kinetic thermal pressure `8.314 {{key:ENTER}} 300 {{key:×}}` ($RT = 2494.20$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.000, X: 2494.20)$) divided by free molar volume `.10 {{key:ENTER}} .04 {{key:-}}` ($V - b = 0.060000$ in $X$ with $2494.20$ in $Y$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 2494.20, X: 0.060000)$) via `{{key:÷}}` gives $41570.00\text{ Pa}$ $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 41570.00)$; computing the cohesive pressure correction `.36 {{key:ENTER}} .10 {{key:x²}} {{key:÷}}` ($36.00\text{ Pa}$ in $X$ with $41570.00$ in $Y$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 41570.00, X: 36.00)$) and subtracting via `{{key:-}}` yields the net real-gas pressure $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{41534.00}\text{ Pa})$.

### Workbook Problem 12.26: Stefan-Boltzmann Radiative Heat Emission

Power $P(T) = \varepsilon \sigma A T^4$:
Quartic curve demonstrates why radiative heat transfer dominates at high temperatures ($T > 1000\text{ K}$).

Integrating Planck's spectral distribution over all wavelengths and hemisphere solid angles yields the Josef Stefan and Ludwig Boltzmann $T^4$ radiation law: raising billet temperature to the fourth power `1200 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:yˣ}}` ($T^4 = 2.0736 \times 10^{12}\text{ K}^4$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 2.0736\text{E}12)$), multiplying by $\sigma = 5.67037 \times 10^{-8}\text{ W}\cdot\text{m}^{-2}\cdot\text{K}^{-4}$ (`5.67037 {{key:E}} 8 {{key:+/-}} {{key:×}}` $\rightarrow 117580.8\text{ W/m}^2$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 117580.8)$), and scaling by surface emissivity and area (`.85 {{key:×}} .50 {{key:×}}`) determines the radiative heat flux $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{49971.84}\text{ W} \approx 50.0\text{ kW})$—note that `1200 {{key:x²}} {{key:x²}}` computes $T^4$ in two single-cycle squarings!

### Workbook Problem 12.27: Newton's Law of Cooling Exponential Asymptote

Plotting $T(t) = T_{\text{env}} + (T_0 - T_{\text{env}}) e^{-k t}$ for a quenched steel billet ($T_0 = 800\text{ K}$, $T_{\text{env}} = 300\text{ K}$, $k = 0.05\text{ s}^{-1}$) over $t \in [0, 100\text{ s}]$ (`T 0.05 {{key:×}} {{key:+/-}} {{key:eˣ}} 500 {{key:×}} 300 {{key:+}}`) illustrates rapid initial heat rejection followed by asymptotic approach to the $300\text{ K}$ ambient floor.

When the Biot number $\text{Bi} = \frac{h L_c}{k_{\text{solid}}} < 0.1$, internal thermal gradients are negligible and lumped-capacitance quenching decays exponentially with time constant $\tau = 1/k = 20\text{ s}$: evaluating at $t = 20\text{ s}$ (`20 {{key:ENTER}} 0.05 {{key:×}} {{key:+/-}}` $\rightarrow -kt = -1.000000$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.000000, X: -1.000000)$), exponentiating via `{{key:eˣ}}` ($e^{-1} \approx 0.367879$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 0.367879)$), scaling by the initial $500\text{ K}$ thermal excess (`500 {{key:×}}` $\rightarrow 183.939721\text{ K}$, $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: 183.939721)$), and adding the $300\text{ K}$ bath temperature (`300 {{key:+}}`) confirms the one-time-constant billet temperature $(T: 0.00, Z: 0.00, Y: 0.00, X: \mathbf{483.939721}\text{ K})$.

### Workbook Problem 13.1: Torricelli Efflux Tank Draining Velocity

A cylindrical fluid storage tank maintains an open liquid column height $h = 5.0\text{ meters}$. Standard acceleration of gravity $g = 9.80665\text{ m/s}^2$. Determine the efflux velocity $v$:
$$v = \sqrt{2 g h} = \sqrt{2 \times 9.80665 \times 5.0} = \sqrt{98.0665} \approx \mathbf{9.90285\text{ m/s}}$$

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_13_applied_stem_case_studies_seq_05:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_13_applied_stem_case_studies_seq_05:value}}`**.

Evangelista Torricelli's 1643 efflux theorem—a direct specialization of Daniel Bernoulli's streamline energy balance $\rho g h = \frac{1}{2}\rho v^2$—states that liquid jets exit a bottom orifice at the free-fall speed acquired across head height $h$: evaluating `2 {{key:ENTER}} 9.80665 {{key:×}} 5 {{key:×}}` ($2gh = 98.0665\text{ m}^2/\text{s}^2$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 98.0665)$) and extracting the square root via `{{key:√x}}` determines the discharge velocity $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{9.9029}\text{ m/s})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.2: Darcy-Weisbach Friction Head Loss in Piping

Water at $20^\circ\text{C}$ flows at velocity $v = 2.40\text{ m/s}$ through a commercial steel pipe with length $L = 85.0\text{ m}$ and inner diameter $D = 0.15\text{ m}$. Darcy friction factor $f = 0.0192$:
$$h_f = f \frac{L}{D} \frac{v^2}{2g} = 0.0192 \times \frac{85.0}{0.15} \times \frac{2.40^2}{2 \times 9.80665} \approx \mathbf{3.1952\text{ meters}}$$

In Julius Weisbach and Henry Darcy's pipe-friction formulation, viscous boundary-layer shear dissipates mechanical energy in proportion to the dimensionless resistance coefficient $f(L/D)$ times the velocity head $v^2/(2g)$: evaluating `.0192 {{key:ENTER}} 85 {{key:×}} .15 {{key:÷}}` ($f L/D = 10.8800$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 10.8800)$) and chaining `2.4 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:÷}} 9.80665 {{key:÷}}` resolves the viscous head loss $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{3.1952}\text{ m})$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.3: Infill Grid Volume Integration & Spool Consumption

A rectangular drone chassis housing has outer bounding box dimensions $140\text{ mm} \times 90\text{ mm} \times 35\text{ mm}$. The part is sliced with 3 solid perimeter walls ($2.0\text{ mm}$ thick) consuming $85,000\text{ mm}^3$, while the internal cavity ($355,000\text{ mm}^3$) is filled with a $25\%$ gyroid infill density ($\rho_{\text{infill}} = 0.25$). Technical polymer density is $\rho = 1.25\text{ g/cm}^3$ ($0.00125\text{ g/mm}^3$):
$$V_{\text{total}} = V_{\text{walls}} + \rho_{\text{infill}} \times V_{\text{cavity}} = 85,000 + (0.25 \times 355,000) = 85,000 + 88,750 = \mathbf{173,750\text{ mm}^3}$$
$$M_{\text{part}} = V_{\text{total}} \times \rho = 173,750 \times 0.00125 \approx \mathbf{217.19\text{ grams}}$$

Triply periodic minimal surfaces such as Alan Schoen's gyroid partition internal core cavities into isotropic structural lattices at a fraction of solid mass: weighting the cavity volume by the infill fraction and adding the solid shell (`355000 {{key:ENTER}} .25 {{key:×}} 85000 {{key:+}}` $\rightarrow V_{\text{total}} = 173,750\text{ mm}^3$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 173750.0)$) before scaling by `.00125 {{key:×}}` determines the exact spool mass budget $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{217.1875}\text{ g})$.

### Workbook Problem 13.4: Ideal Brayton Gas Turbine Cycle Thermal Efficiency

An industrial gas turbine operates on the ideal Brayton cycle with compressor pressure ratio $r_p = 14.5$ and air specific heat ratio $\gamma = 1.40$:

$$\eta_{\text{Brayton}} = 1 - \frac{1}{r_p^{(\gamma - 1)/\gamma}} = 1 - \frac{1}{(14.5)^{(0.40/1.40)}} \approx \mathbf{53.41\%}$$

In George Brayton's open gas-turbine cycle, isentropic compression across pressure ratio $r_p$ raises absolute air temperature by the Poisson factor $T_2/T_1 = r_p^{(\gamma-1)/\gamma}$: evaluating `14.5 {{key:ENTER}} .40 {{key:ENTER}} 1.40 {{key:÷}} {{key:yˣ}}` ($2.1465$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.1465)$) and chaining `{{key:1/x}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}}` determines the ideal Brayton thermal efficiency $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.5341}$, or $53.41\%)$.

### Workbook Problem 13.5: Composite Wall Conduction Heat Flux

A furnace wall consists of $L_1 = 0.15\text{ m}$ firebrick ($k_1 = 1.2\text{ W/m}\cdot\text{K}$) and $L_2 = 0.05\text{ m}$ insulating mineral wool ($k_2 = 0.04\text{ W/m}\cdot\text{K}$). Inner temperature $T_{\text{in}} = 850^\circ\text{C}$, outer temperature $T_{\text{out}} = 50^\circ\text{C}$:
$$R_{\text{total}} = \frac{L_1}{k_1} + \frac{L_2}{k_2} = \frac{0.15}{1.2} + \frac{0.05}{0.04} = 0.125 + 1.250 = \mathbf{1.375\text{ m}^2\text{K/W}}$$
$$q'' = \frac{\Delta T}{R_{\text{total}}} = \frac{850 - 50}{1.375} = \frac{800}{1.375} \approx \mathbf{581.82\text{ W/m}^2}$$

By Jean-Baptiste Joseph Fourier's law of heat conduction across series planar layers, thermal resistances $R_i = L_i/k_i$ add directly like series electrical resistors: summing the firebrick and mineral-wool resistances via `.15 {{key:ENTER}} 1.2 {{key:÷}} .05 {{key:ENTER}} .04 {{key:÷}} {{key:+}}` ($R_{\text{total}} = 1.3750\text{ m}^2\text{K/W}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.3750)$) and dividing into the overall thermal drop `850 {{key:ENTER}} 50 {{key:-}} {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}}` determines the steady-state heat flux $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{581.8182}\text{ W/m}^2)$.

### Workbook Problem 13.6: Rankine Steam Power Cycle Thermal Efficiency

In a subcritical Rankine steam cycle, high-pressure steam enters the turbine at enthalpy $h_1 = 3,450.0\text{ kJ/kg}$ and expands to condenser pressure with enthalpy $h_2 = 2,180.0\text{ kJ/kg}$. The feedwater pump consumes work $w_p = 15.0\text{ kJ/kg}$ to pressurize liquid from $h_3 = 190.0\text{ kJ/kg}$ to boiler inlet state $h_4 = 205.0\text{ kJ/kg}$.
1. Net turbine work output:
   $$w_{\text{net}} = (h_1 - h_2) - w_p = (3450.0 - 2180.0) - 15.0 = 1270.0 - 15.0 = \mathbf{1,255.0\text{ kJ/kg}}$$
2. Heat input supplied by the boiler:
   $$q_{\text{in}} = h_1 - h_4 = 3450.0 - 205.0 = \mathbf{3,245.0\text{ kJ/kg}}$$
3. Thermal efficiency:
   $$\eta_{\text{Rankine}} = \frac{w_{\text{net}}}{q_{\text{in}}} = \frac{1255.0}{3245.0} \approx \mathbf{38.67\%}$$

Evaluating net shaft work `3450 {{key:ENTER}} 2180 {{key:-}} 15 {{key:-}}` ($w_{\text{net}} = 1,255.0\text{ kJ/kg}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1255.0000)$), computing boiler enthalpy addition `3450 {{key:ENTER}} 205 {{key:-}}` ($q_{\text{in}} = 3,245.0\text{ kJ/kg}$ in $X$ while $w_{\text{net}}$ waits in $Y$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 1255.0000, X: 3245.0000)$), and dividing via `{{key:÷}} 100 {{key:×}}` resolves the Rankine cycle efficiency $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{38.6749}\%)$—or by duplicating throttle enthalpy $h_1 = 3450$ upfront (`3450 {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}} 2180 {{key:-}} 15 {{key:-}} {{key:x⇄y}} 205 {{key:-}} {{key:÷}} 100 {{key:×}}`), `3450` is entered only once!

### Workbook Problem 13.7: Otto Cycle Internal Combustion Efficiency vs. Compression Ratio

The theoretical thermal efficiency of an internal combustion Otto cycle engine depends solely on compression ratio $r = V_{\max} / V_{\min}$ and air specific heat ratio $\gamma = 1.40$:
$$\eta_{\text{Otto}} = 1 - \frac{1}{r^{\gamma - 1}}$$
For a high-performance racing engine with compression ratio $r = 11.5$:
$$\eta_{\text{Otto}} = 1 - \frac{1}{11.5^{0.40}} = 1 - \frac{1}{2.65839} = 1 - 0.376167 = \mathbf{62.35\%}$$

In Nikolaus Otto's four-stroke spark-ignition cycle, isentropic compression across volume ratio $r = 11.5$ elevates the cycle temperature ratio to $r^{\gamma-1}$: keying `11.5 {{key:ENTER}} .40 {{key:yˣ}}` ($11.5^{0.40} \approx 2.6563$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.6563)$) and chaining `{{key:1/x}} 1 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}}` determines the air-standard Otto efficiency $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.6235}$, or $62.35\%)$.

### Workbook Problem 13.8: 2nd-Order Butterworth Crossover Filters

Crossover frequency $f_c = 2500\text{ Hz}$, driver nominal impedance $R = 8.0\,\Omega$:

$$L = \frac{R \sqrt{2}}{2\pi f_c} = \frac{8 \sqrt{2}}{2\pi \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{0.720253\text{ mH}}$$
$$C = \frac{1}{2\pi \sqrt{2} R f_c} = \frac{1}{2\pi \sqrt{2} \times 8 \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{5.62698\text{ }\mu\text{F}}$$

Stephen Butterworth's maximally flat second-order crossover ($Q = 1/\sqrt{2} \approx 0.7071$) requires reactive impedance $X_L = \sqrt{2}R$ and capacitive reactance $X_C = R/\sqrt{2}$ at the crossover frequency $f_c$: evaluating `8 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:√x}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:÷}} {{key:π}} {{key:÷}} 2500 {{key:÷}}` determines the series woofer inductance $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{7.2025 \times 10^{-4}}\text{ H}$, or $0.7203\text{ mH})$, and evaluating `2 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:√x}} {{key:×}} 8 {{key:×}} 2500 {{key:×}} {{key:1/x}}` determines the shunt tweeter capacitance $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 7.2025 \times 10^{-4}, X: \mathbf{5.6270 \times 10^{-6}}\text{ F}$, or $5.63\,\mu\text{F})$—or even faster, since $C = \frac{L}{2 R^2} = \frac{L}{128}$, simply dividing $L$ by `128 {{key:÷}}` directly on the stack yields $C = \mathbf{5.6270\,\mu\text{F}}$ in a single division!

### Workbook Problem 13.9: 4th-Order Linkwitz-Riley Crossover ($24\text{ dB/octave}$)

For $f_c = 2500\text{ Hz}, R = 8.0\,\Omega$:

$$L_{LR4} = \frac{R}{\pi f_c} = \frac{8.0}{\pi \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{1.01859\text{ mH}}$$
$$C_{LR4} = \frac{1}{4\pi R f_c} = \frac{1}{4\pi \times 8.0 \times 2500} \approx \mathbf{3.97887\text{ }\mu\text{F}}$$

Siegfried Linkwitz and Russ Riley demonstrated in 1976 that cascading two Butterworth filters produces $-6\text{ dB}$ acoustic amplitude and zero phase difference at $f_c$, summing to a flat unity acoustic wavefront: keying `8.0 {{key:π}} {{key:÷}} 2500 {{key:÷}}` resolves the LR4 series inductance $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{1.0186 \times 10^{-3}}\text{ H}$, or $1.019\text{ mH})$, and keying `4 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 8 {{key:×}} 2500 {{key:×}} {{key:1/x}}` (or dividing $L_{LR4}$ by $4R^2 = 256\,\Omega^2$ via `256 {{key:÷}}`!) resolves the shunt capacitance $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0010, X: \mathbf{3.9789 \times 10^{-6}}\text{ F}$, or $3.98\,\mu\text{F})$.

### Workbook Problem 13.10: Complex AC Impedance Addition via {{key:CMPLX}}

Branch 1 impedance $Z_1 = 8 + 4i\,\Omega$ ($Y = 8, X = 4$).  
Branch 2 impedance $Z_2 = 4 - 8i\,\Omega$ ($Y = 4, X = -8$).  
Total series impedance: $Z_{\text{total}} = (8 + 4i) + (4 - 8i) = \mathbf{12 - 4i\,\Omega}$.

In Charles Proteus Steinmetz's AC phasor calculus, series impedances combine vectorially across real resistance and imaginary reactance: staging $Z_2 = 4 - 8i$ and $Z_1 = 8 + 4i$ across the four registers via `4 {{key:ENTER}} 8 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 8 {{key:ENTER}} 4` $(T: 4.0000, Z: -8.0000, Y: 8.0000, X: 4.0000)$ and executing `{{key:CMPLX}} {{key:+}}` sums the real parts $(T + Y)$ into $Y$ and imaginary parts $(Z + X)$ into $X$ in a single cycle $(T: 4.0000, Z: 4.0000, Y: \mathbf{12.0000}, X: \mathbf{-4.0000})$.

### Workbook Problem 13.11: Zobel Network Dynamic Impedance Equalizer

Dynamic loudspeaker voice coils exhibit rising inductive impedance at high frequencies ($Z(f) = R_e + j\omega L_e$). A Zobel network placed in parallel with the driver flattens the impedance back to pure resistance $R_e$:
$$R_z = 1.25 \times R_e, \quad C_z = \frac{L_e}{R_z^2}$$
For a woofer with DC voice coil resistance $R_e = 6.4\,\Omega$ and voice coil inductance $L_e = 0.85\text{ mH}$ ($0.00085\text{ H}$):
1. Zobel resistor: $R_z = 1.25 \times 6.4 = \mathbf{8.00\,\Omega}$.
2. Zobel capacitor:
   $$C_z = \frac{0.00085}{8.0^2} = \frac{0.00085}{64.0} \approx \mathbf{13.281\text{ }\mu\text{F}}$$

Otto Zobel's constant-resistance Boucherot cell neutralizes voice-coil inductive rise so passive crossovers see a resistive termination: computing `6.4 {{key:ENTER}} 1.25 {{key:×}}` establishes the damping resistor $R_z$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{8.0000}\,\Omega)$, squaring in place via `{{key:x²}}` ($R_z^2 = 64.0\,\Omega^2$), and chaining `.00085 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:÷}} 1 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:×}}` determines the conjugate Zobel capacitance $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{13.2813}\,\mu\text{F})$.

### Workbook Problem 13.12: Snell's Law & Total Internal Reflection Critical Angle

Light propagates from optical glass ($n_1 = 1.52$) into air ($n_2 = 1.00$). Determine the critical angle $\theta_c$:

$$\theta_c = \arcsin\left(\frac{n_2}{n_1}\right) = \arcsin\left(\frac{1.00}{1.52}\right) = \arcsin(0.657895) \approx \mathbf{41.14^\circ}$$

By Willebrord Snellius's refraction law $n_1 \sin\theta_1 = n_2 \sin\theta_2$, total internal reflection commences when the transmitted ray grazes the interface at $\theta_2 = 90^\circ$ ($\sin\theta_2 = 1$): dividing the refractive indices `1 {{key:ENTER}} 1.52 {{key:÷}}` ($\sin\theta_c \approx 0.6579$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.6579)$) and pressing `{{key:ASIN}}` in Degrees mode determines the critical angle $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{41.1395}^\circ)$—or in just two operations via `1.52 {{key:1/x}} {{key:ASIN}}`!

### Workbook Problem 13.13: Numerical Aperture & Light Acceptance Angle of Optical Fiber

A multi-mode step-index silica optical fiber has core refractive index $n_{\text{core}} = 1.482$ and cladding refractive index $n_{\text{clad}} = 1.460$:
1. Numerical Aperture ($NA$):
   $$NA = \sqrt{n_{\text{core}}^2 - n_{\text{clad}}^2} = \sqrt{1.482^2 - 1.460^2} = \sqrt{2.196324 - 2.131600} = \sqrt{0.064724} \approx \mathbf{0.2544}$$
2. Maximum acceptance half-angle in air ($\theta_{\max}$):
   $$\theta_{\max} = \arcsin(NA) = \arcsin(0.254409) \approx \mathbf{14.74^\circ}$$

Ernst Abbe's numerical aperture $NA = \sqrt{n_{\text{core}}^2 - n_{\text{clad}}^2}$ governs both the light-gathering cone and modal dispersion of a step-index waveguide: evaluating `1.482 {{key:x²}} 1.460 {{key:x²}} {{key:-}} {{key:√x}}` determines $NA = \mathbf{0.2544}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{0.2544})$, and pressing `{{key:ASIN}}` in Degrees mode resolves the acceptance half-cone $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{14.7389}^\circ)$.

### Workbook Problem 13.14: Thick Lens Equivalent Focal Length (Gullstrand Formula)

A thick biconvex crown glass lens has front radius $R_1 = 50.0\text{ mm}$, rear radius $R_2 = -80.0\text{ mm}$, center thickness $d = 12.0\text{ mm}$, and refractive index $n = 1.520$. Gullstrand's lens maker formula states:
$$\frac{1}{f} = (n - 1) \left[ \frac{1}{R_1} - \frac{1}{R_2} + \frac{(n - 1) d}{n R_1 R_2} \right]$$
1. Front surface power: $(n - 1) / R_1 = 0.520 / 50 = 0.010400\text{ mm}^{-1}$.
2. Rear surface power: $-(n - 1) / R_2 = -0.520 / (-80) = 0.006500\text{ mm}^{-1}$.
3. Thickness coupling term:
   $$\frac{(n - 1)^2 d}{n R_1 R_2} = \frac{0.520^2 \times 12.0}{1.520 \times 50.0 \times (-80.0)} = \frac{3.2448}{-6080.0} = -0.00053368\text{ mm}^{-1}$$
4. Total optical power: $P = 0.010400 + 0.006500 - 0.00053368 = 0.01636632\text{ mm}^{-1}$.
5. Equivalent focal length: $f = 1 / P \approx \mathbf{61.10\text{ mm}}$.

Allvar Gullstrand's paraxial thick-lens equation accounts for principal-plane separation across axial thickness $d = 12.0\text{ mm}$: summing the two surface dioptric powers `.520 {{key:ENTER}} 50 {{key:÷}} .520 {{key:ENTER}} 80 {{key:÷}} {{key:+}}` ($0.016900\text{ mm}^{-1}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0169)$), deducting the thickness coupling term `.520 {{key:x²}} 12 {{key:×}} 1.52 {{key:ENTER}} 50 {{key:×}} 80 {{key:×}} {{key:÷}} {{key:-}}` ($P = 0.016366\text{ mm}^{-1}$, $(T: 0.0169, Z: 0.0169, Y: 0.0169, X: 0.0164)$), and inverting via `{{key:1/x}}` yields the equivalent focal length $(T: 0.0169, Z: 0.0169, Y: 0.0169, X: \mathbf{61.1011}\text{ mm})$—and by noting that $\frac{1}{50} + \frac{1}{80} = (50 \parallel 80)^{-1}$, you can evaluate the bracketed geometry via `50 {{key:ENTER}} 80 {{key:x∥y}} {{key:1/x}}`!
