### Workbook Problem 13.15: Hohmann Transfer Orbit $\Delta v$ (LEO to GEO)

Earth gravitational parameter $\mu = 3.986004 \times 10^{14}\text{ m}^3/\text{s}^2$.  
Initial LEO radius $r_1 = 6.678 \times 10^6\text{ m}$ ($h = 300\text{ km}$).  
Target GEO radius $r_2 = 4.2164 \times 10^7\text{ m}$ ($h = 35,786\text{ km}$).  
Transfer semi-major axis $a = (r_1 + r_2)/2 = 2.4421 \times 10^7\text{ m}$.
1. Initial circular velocity: $v_{c1} = \sqrt{\mu / r_1} = \mathbf{7,725.82\text{ m/s}}$.
2. Transfer ellipse insertion: $v_{tx1} = \sqrt{\mu(2/r_1 - 1/a)} = \mathbf{10,151.62\text{ m/s}}$.
3. Periapsis impulse: $\Delta v_1 = 10,151.62 - 7,725.82 = \mathbf{2,425.80\text{ m/s}}$.
4. Apoapsis circularization: $\Delta v_2 = 3,074.66 - 1,609.91 = \mathbf{1,464.75\text{ m/s}}$.
5. Total mission $\Delta v$: $\Delta v_{\text{total}} = 2,425.80 + 1,464.75 = \mathbf{3,890.55\text{ m/s}}$ ($3.891\text{ km/s}$).

**Keystrokes (Delta-V Impulses & Total Mission Budget):** `10151.62 {{key:ENTER}} 7725.82 {{key:-}} 3074.66 {{key:ENTER}} 1609.91 {{key:-}} {{key:+}}` $\rightarrow$ **`3890.550000`**

### Workbook Problem 13.16: Rocket Motor Burnout Velocity via Romberg Quadrature

Integrate thrust acceleration $a(t) = 4t^2 - 0.5t^3$ from $t = 0$ to $t = 6.0\text{ seconds}$:

$$\Delta v = \int_0^6 (4t^2 - 0.5t^3) dt = \left[ \frac{4}{3}t^3 - \frac{1}{8}t^4 \right]_0^6 = \frac{4}{3}(216) - \frac{1}{8}(1296) = 288 - 162 = \mathbf{126.0\text{ m/s}}$$
Executed in `{{key:EQN}}` via {{key:∫}}: engine converges to exactly **`126.0000 m/s`**.

When solid-propellant grain regression causes thrust acceleration $a(t) = 4t^2 - 0.5t^3$ to vary non-linearly over the burn duration, integrating $a(t)$ in `{{key:EQN}}` (`4 T {{key:x²}} {{key:×}} 0.5 T 3 {{key:yˣ}} {{key:×}} {{key:-}}`) across the burn window `0 {{key:ENTER}} 6` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 6.0000)$ via `{{key:∫}} T` applies Richardson extrapolation to resolve the exact burnout velocity increment $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{126.0000}\text{ m/s})$.

### Workbook Problem 13.17: Gravitational Escape Velocity from Earth and Mars

Planetary surface escape velocity is given by $v_{\text{esc}} = \sqrt{\frac{2\mu}{r}}$:
1. Earth ($\mu = 3.986004 \times 10^{14}\text{ m}^3/\text{s}^2, r = 6.378 \times 10^6\text{ m}$):
   $$v_{\text{esc},\oplus} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 3.986004 \times 10^{14}}{6.378 \times 10^6}} \approx \mathbf{11,180.00\text{ m/s}} \quad (\mathbf{11.18\text{ km/s}})$$
2. Mars ($\mu_{\text{Mars}} = 4.282837 \times 10^{13}\text{ m}^3/\text{s}^2, r_{\text{Mars}} = 3.3895 \times 10^6\text{ m}$):
   $$v_{\text{esc,Mars}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 4.282837 \times 10^{13}}{3.3895 \times 10^6}} \approx \mathbf{5,026.91\text{ m/s}} \quad (\mathbf{5.03\text{ km/s}})$$

Setting total specific mechanical energy $\mathcal{E} = \frac{1}{2}v^2 - \frac{\mu}{r} = 0$ on a parabolic escape trajectory yields $v_{\text{esc}} = \sqrt{2\mu/r} = \sqrt{2}\,v_{\text{circ}}$: evaluating `2 {{key:ENTER}} 3.986004 {{key:E}} 14 {{key:×}} 6.378 {{key:E}} 6 {{key:÷}}` ($2\mu/r \approx 1.2500 \times 10^8\text{ m}^2/\text{s}^2$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.2500 \times 10^8)$) and extracting the square root via `{{key:√x}}` determines Earth's surface escape velocity $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: \mathbf{11180.00}\text{ m/s}$, or $11.18\text{ km/s})$.

### Workbook Problem 13.18: Meridian Convergence & True vs. Magnetic Azimuth

A geodetic survey line in a mid-latitude UTM zone has a measured magnetic azimuth $\alpha_{\text{mag}} = 142.35^\circ$. The local magnetic declination is $\delta = 4.12^\circ\text{ E}$ (East declination is added), and grid convergence angle is $\gamma = -1.25^\circ$:
$$\alpha_{\text{true}} = \alpha_{\text{mag}} + \delta = 142.35^\circ + 4.12^\circ = \mathbf{146.47^\circ}$$
$$\alpha_{\text{grid}} = \alpha_{\text{true}} - \gamma = 146.47^\circ - (-1.25^\circ) = \mathbf{147.72^\circ}$$

Reducing a field compass bearing to a State Plane Lambert conformal conic grid azimuth requires two sequential angular rotations: staging the magnetic azimuth `142.35 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 142.3500, X: 142.3500)$, adding easterly geomagnetic declination `4.12 {{key:+}}` to obtain geodetic true north azimuth $\alpha_{\text{true}} = \mathbf{146.47^\circ}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 146.4700)$, and subtracting meridian convergence `1.25 {{key:+/-}} {{key:-}}` to establish grid azimuth $\alpha_{\text{grid}} = \mathbf{147.72^\circ}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 147.7200)$.

### Workbook Problem 13.19: Bowditch Compass Rule Linear Misclosure Balancing

A field traverse leg has raw measured length $L_1 = 250.0\text{ ft}$ along a total perimeter $L_{\text{total}} = 1,250.0\text{ ft}$. The total survey departure misclosure is $E_x = +0.15\text{ ft}$. Calculate the Bowditch proportional departure correction $C_{x1}$:
$$C_{x1} = - E_x \times \left( \frac{L_1}{L_{\text{total}}} \right) = -0.15 \times \left( \frac{250.0}{1250.0} \right) = -0.15 \times 0.20 = \mathbf{-0.030\text{ ft}}$$

Nathaniel Bowditch's 1807 Compass Rule distributes linear traverse misclosure in proportion to each leg's fraction of total perimeter length: negating the departure misclosure `.15 {{key:+/-}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -0.1500)$, weighting by leg length `250 {{key:×}}` ($-37.50\text{ ft}^2$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -37.5000)$), and dividing by total traverse perimeter `1250 {{key:÷}}` determines the leg departure adjustment $C_{x1} = \mathbf{-0.030\text{ ft}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -0.0300)$.

### Workbook Problem 13.20: Uniform Distributed Load Maximum Bending Moment

A simply supported floor girder spans $L = 8.0\text{ meters}$ supporting a combined dead and live distributed load $w = 24.5\text{ kN/m}$ ($24,500\text{ N/m}$). Calculate the maximum bending moment $M_{\max}$ at midspan:
$$M_{\max} = \frac{w L^2}{8} = \frac{24500 \times 8.0^2}{8} = 24500 \times 8 = \mathbf{196,000\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}} \quad (\mathbf{196.0\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}})$$

Integrating the linear shear diagram $V(x) = w(L/2 - x)$ from the support to midspan $x = L/2$ yields the parabolic bending-moment maximum $M_{\max} = \frac{wL^2}{8}$: staging the distributed load `24500 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 24500.0000, X: 24500.0000)$, multiplying by squared span `8 {{key:x²}} {{key:×}}` ($1,568,000.0\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1568000.0000)$), and dividing by `8 {{key:÷}}` resolves the peak midspan moment $M_{\max} = \mathbf{196,000\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}}$ ($196.0\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 196000.0000)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.21: Cantilever Beam Maximum Tip Deflection Under Uniform Load

A balcony cantilever beam of length $L = 2.5\text{ m}$ carries uniform distributed load $w = 3,200\text{ N/m}$. The beam is structural steel with $E = 200.0\text{ GPa}$ ($200 \times 10^9\text{ Pa}$) and second moment of area $I = 1.65 \times 10^{-5}\text{ m}^4$. Maximum tip deflection is:
$$\delta_{\max} = \frac{w L^4}{8 E I} = \frac{3200 \times 2.5^4}{8 \times (200 \times 10^9) \times (1.65 \times 10^{-5})} = \frac{3200 \times 39.0625}{26,400,000} \approx \mathbf{0.004735\text{ m}} \quad (\mathbf{4.74\text{ mm}})$$

Because cantilever tip deflection under uniform loading scales with the fourth power of overhang length ($L^4$), small span increases produce dramatic compliance penalties: raising the span `2.5 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:yˣ}}` ($39.0625\text{ m}^4$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 39.0625)$), scaling by `3200 {{key:×}}` ($125,000.0\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}^3$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 125000.0000)$), and dividing sequentially by `8 {{key:÷}} 200 {{key:E}} 9 {{key:÷}}` ($7.8125 \times 10^{-11}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 7.8125 \times 10^{-11})$) and `1.65 {{key:E}} 5 {{key:+/-}} {{key:÷}}` determines the free-end deflection $\delta_{\max} = \mathbf{0.004735\text{ m}}$ ($4.74\text{ mm}$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0047)$—note that `2.5 {{key:x²}} {{key:x²}}` computes $2.5^4$ in two single-cycle squarings!

---

### Workbook Problem 13.22: Standard Gibbs Free Energy & Equilibrium Constant

For the chemical synthesis of ammonia ($\text{N}_2 + 3\text{H}_2 \rightleftharpoons 2\text{NH}_3$) at temperature $T = 298.15\text{ K}$, the thermodynamic equilibrium constant is $K_p = 5.8 \times 10^5$. Calculate the standard Gibbs free energy change $\Delta G^\circ$:
$$\Delta G^\circ = - R T \ln K_p = - 8.314462 \times 298.15 \times \ln(5.8 \times 10^5) \approx \mathbf{-32,897.4\text{ J/mol}} \quad (\mathbf{-32.90\text{ kJ/mol}})$$

J. Willard Gibbs's chemical-potential isotherm $\Delta G^\circ = -RT\ln K_p$ links macroscopic reaction equilibrium to standard molar free energy: taking the natural logarithm `5.8 {{key:E}} 5 {{key:LN}}` ($13.2708$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 13.2708)$), scaling by thermal energy `8.314462 {{key:×}} 298.15 {{key:×}}` ($32,897.70\text{ J/mol}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 32897.7010)$), and negating/converting to kilojoules via `{{key:+/-}} 1000 {{key:÷}}` confirms a strongly exergonic Haber-Bosch synthesis at $298.15\text{ K}$: $\Delta G^\circ = \mathbf{-32.90\text{ kJ/mol}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -32.8977)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.23: Nernst Electrochemical Cell Potential

A galvanic Daniell cell ($\text{Zn} + \text{Cu}^{2+} \rightleftharpoons \text{Zn}^{2+} + \text{Cu}$) has standard cell potential $E^\circ = 1.100\text{ V}$ with $n = 2\text{ electrons transferred}$ at $T = 298.15\text{ K}$. Faraday constant $F = 96,485.33\text{ C/mol}$. The electrolyte concentrations are $[\text{Zn}^{2+}] = 0.50\text{ M}$ and $[\text{Cu}^{2+}] = 0.010\text{ M}$ (reaction quotient $Q = 0.50 / 0.010 = 50.0$):
$$E = E^\circ - \frac{R T}{n F} \ln Q = 1.100 - \frac{8.314462 \times 298.15}{2 \times 96485.33} \ln(50.0) = 1.100 - (0.012845 \times 3.912023) \approx \mathbf{1.0497\text{ V}}$$

Walther Nernst's electromotive equation quantifies how product ion accumulation ($Q = [\text{Zn}^{2+}]/[\text{Cu}^{2+}] = 50.0$) depresses galvanic cell voltage below the standard potential $E^\circ$: evaluating the molar free-energy shift `50 {{key:LN}} 8.314462 {{key:×}} 298.15 {{key:×}}` ($RT\ln Q = 9,697.74\text{ J/mol}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 9697.7358)$), dividing by charge transfer $nF$ via `2 {{key:÷}} 96485.3 {{key:÷}}` ($\Delta E = 0.050255\text{ V}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.0503)$), and subtracting from $E^\circ$ via `1.100 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}}` determines the terminal cell electromotive force $E = \mathbf{1.0497\text{ V}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.0497)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.24: Carbon-14 Archeological Radiocarbon Dating

Living plant tissue maintains a steady Carbon-14 activity of $A_0 = 15.3\text{ disintegrations/min}\cdot\text{g of carbon}$. An ancient timber specimen excavated from a ceremonial structure exhibits an activity of $A(t) = 9.4\text{ dpm/g}$. Using the true physical **Cambridge $^{14}\text{C}$ half-life** $t_{1/2} = 5,730\text{ years}$ ($\lambda = \ln 2 / 5730 \approx 0.00012097\text{ yr}^{-1}$; note that uncalibrated archaeological laboratory reports use the conventional $5,568\text{-year}$ Libby half-life prior to `IntCal20` calibration):
$$t = - \frac{1}{\lambda} \ln\left(\frac{A(t)}{A_0}\right) = - \frac{5730}{\ln 2} \ln\left(\frac{9.4}{15.3}\right) = -8266.64 \times (-0.487144) \approx \mathbf{4,027.0\text{ years}}$$

Willard Libby's radiocarbon chronometer measures the time elapsed since an organism ceased exchanging atmospheric $^{14}\text{CO}_2$: evaluating the logarithmic activity depletion `9.4 {{key:ENTER}} 15.3 {{key:÷}} {{key:LN}}` ($\ln(9.4/15.3) = -0.487144$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -0.4871)$), scaling by the mean lifetime $\tau = t_{1/2}/\ln 2$ via `5730 {{key:×}} 2 {{key:LN}} {{key:÷}}` ($-4,027.014\text{ yr}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -4027.0141)$), and reversing sign with `{{key:+/-}}` dates the timber specimen to $t = \mathbf{4,027.0\text{ years}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 4027.0141)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.25: Gamma Ray Shielding & Lead Half-Value Layer (HVL)

Cobalt-60 emits energetic gamma photons ($1.17\text{ MeV}$ and $1.33\text{ MeV}$) with linear attenuation coefficient $\mu = 0.650\text{ cm}^{-1}$ in lead shielding.
1. Half-Value Layer thickness:
   $$\text{HVL} = \frac{\ln 2}{\mu} = \frac{0.693147}{0.650} \approx \mathbf{1.0664\text{ cm}}$$
2. Required thickness $x$ to reduce radiation intensity by a factor of 100 ($I / I_0 = 0.01$):
   $$x = - \frac{\ln(0.01)}{\mu} = \frac{4.605170}{0.650} \approx \mathbf{7.0849\text{ cm}}$$

By the Beer-Lambert exponential attenuation law $I(x) = I_0 e^{-\mu x}$, attenuating a narrow gamma beam to $1\%$ transmission ($I/I_0 = 0.01$, or two Tenth-Value Layers) requires optical depth $\mu x = -\ln(0.01) = 4.605170$: keying `.01 {{key:LN}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -4.6052)$, dividing by the linear attenuation coefficient `.650 {{key:÷}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -7.0849)$, and pressing `{{key:+/-}}` specifies a lead shield thickness of $x = \mathbf{7.0849\text{ cm}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 7.0849)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.26: Decibel Addition of Incoherent Environmental Noise Sources

An industrial shop floor operates three loud machines with sound pressure levels: Machine 1: $L_1 = 88.0\text{ dBA}$, Machine 2: $L_2 = 91.0\text{ dBA}$, Machine 3: $L_3 = 85.0\text{ dBA}$. Determine the combined acoustic noise level $L_{\Sigma}$:
$$L_{\Sigma} = 10 \log_{10} \left( 10^{88/10} + 10^{91/10} + 10^{85/10} \right) = 10 \log_{10}(6.3096 \times 10^8 + 1.2589 \times 10^9 + 3.1623 \times 10^8) \approx \mathbf{93.43\text{ dBA}}$$

Because uncorrelated machinery noise sources sum in acoustic intensity ($W/\text{m}^2$) rather than decibels, antilogging each bel level ($L_i/10$) via `8.8 {{key:10ˣ}} 9.1 {{key:10ˣ}} {{key:+}}` ($1.8899 \times 10^9$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.8899 \times 10^9)$) plus `8.5 {{key:10ˣ}} {{key:+}}` ($2.2061 \times 10^9$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.2061 \times 10^9)$) and re-compressing to decibels via `{{key:LOG}} 10 {{key:×}}` determines the composite shop-floor exposure $L_{\Sigma} = \mathbf{93.44\text{ dBA}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 93.4363)$.

---

### Workbook Problem 13.27: Inverse Square Law Acoustic Attenuation with Distance

An outdoor emergency warning siren emits sound level $L_1 = 105.0\text{ dB}$ measured at distance $r_1 = 10.0\text{ meters}$. Assuming spherical propagation without ground reflections, determine sound pressure level $L_2$ at distance $r_2 = 120.0\text{ meters}$:
$$L_2 = L_1 - 20 \log_{10}\left(\frac{r_2}{r_1}\right) = 105.0 - 20 \log_{10}\left(\frac{120.0}{10.0}\right) = 105.0 - 20 \log_{10}(12.0) = 105.0 - 21.5836 \approx \mathbf{83.42\text{ dB}}$$

In free-field spherical spreading, acoustic intensity falls off as $1/r^2$ ($-6.02\text{ dB}$ per distance doubling): evaluating the divergence loss `120 {{key:ENTER}} 10 {{key:÷}} {{key:LOG}} 20 {{key:×}}` ($21.5836\text{ dB}$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 21.5836)$) and deducting it from the reference level via `105 {{key:x⇄y}} {{key:-}}` determines the far-field sound pressure level $L_2 = \mathbf{83.42\text{ dB}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 83.4164)$.

### Workbook Problem 13.28: Cross-Discipline Isomorphism of the Parallel Key (`{{key:x∥y}}`)

Because the SC-32 elevates $x \parallel y = \frac{1}{1/x + 1/y} = \frac{xy}{x+y}$ to a primary function key, a practicing engineer solves five distinct physical domains with the exact same 3-keystroke gesture:
1. **Parallel Resistors ($R_1 \parallel R_2$):** `100 {{key:ENTER}} 250 {{key:x∥y}}` $\rightarrow$ $71.43\text{ }\Omega$.
2. **Series Capacitors ($C_1 = 47\text{ }\mu\text{F}$ in series with $C_2 = 22\text{ }\mu\text{F}$):**
**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_13_eng_series_capacitors:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_13_eng_series_capacitors:value}}`**.
3. **Series Mechanical Springs ($k_1$ in series with $k_2$):** $k_{\text{eq}} = k_1 \parallel k_2$.
4. **Parallel Thermal Spreading Paths ($\theta_{JA,1} \parallel \theta_{JA,2}$):** $\theta_{\text{eq}} = \theta_1 \parallel \theta_2$.
5. **Gaussian Thin Lens Focal Length ($\frac{1}{f} = \frac{1}{d_o} + \frac{1}{d_i}$):** $f = d_o \parallel d_i$.

Staging $C_1 = 47.0\text{ }\mu\text{F}$ via `47 {{key:ENTER}}` $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 47.0000, X: 47.0000)$ and combining with $C_2 = 22.0\text{ }\mu\text{F}$ via `22 {{key:x∥y}}` executes the harmonic product-over-sum operation $\frac{C_1 C_2}{C_1 + C_2}$ in a single hardware cycle to resolve the series equivalent capacitance $C_{\text{eq}} = \mathbf{14.99\text{ }\mu\text{F}}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 14.9855)$.

### Workbook Problem 14.3: Torus Surface Area

Major radius $R = 15.0\text{ cm}$, tube radius $r = 3.2\text{ cm}$. Find $A = 4 \pi^2 R r$.

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_16:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_16:value}}`** (3-sig-fig target: **`1.89E3`** (Full: `1894.964`)).

By Pappus of Alexandria's first centroid theorem, revolving a circle of circumference $2\pi r$ around a path of circumference $2\pi R$ sweeps out surface area $A = (2\pi r)(2\pi R) = 4\pi^2 Rr$: forming `15 {{key:ENTER}} 3.2 {{key:×}} 4 {{key:×}}` ($4Rr = 192.0\text{ cm}^2$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 192.0000)$) and multiplying by `{{key:π}} {{key:x²}} {{key:×}}` determines $A = \mathbf{1,894.96\text{ cm}^2}$ ($1.89 \times 10^3\text{ cm}^2$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1894.9640)$.

### Workbook Problem 14.11: Point-to-Line Perpendicular Distance

Distance from $(2, 3)$ to line $3x + 4y - 12 = 0$:

$$d = \frac{|3(2) + 4(3) - 12|}{\sqrt{3^2 + 4^2}} = \frac{|6 + 12 - 12|}{\sqrt{25}} = \frac{6}{5} = \mathbf{1.20}$$
**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_27:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_27:value}}`** (3-sig-fig target: **`1.20`**).

By Hesse's normal form of a line, substituting $(x_0, y_0) = (2, 3)$ into $Ax + By + C$ and dividing by the normal vector length $\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}$ gives the orthogonal distance: evaluating `3 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:×}} 4 {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:×}} {{key:+}} 12 {{key:-}}` ($6.0000$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 6.0000)$), forming the $(3, 4, 5)$ normal norm `3 {{key:x²}} 4 {{key:x²}} {{key:+}} {{key:√x}}` ($5.0000$ in $X$ with $6.0000$ in $Y$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 6.0000, X: 5.0000)$), and dividing via `{{key:÷}}` yields $d = \mathbf{1.20}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.2000)$.

### Workbook Problem 14.13: Curve Arc Length

Length of $y = x^{1.5}$ from $x = 0$ to $x = 4$:

$$L = \int_0^4 \sqrt{1 + 2.25 x} \, dx = \left[ \frac{8}{27} (1 + 2.25 x)^{1.5} \right]_0^4 = \frac{8}{27} (10^{1.5} - 1) \approx \mathbf{9.0734}$$
**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_05:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_05:value}}`** (3-sig-fig target: **`9.07`**).

Evaluating the arc-length radicand $1 + (y')^2 = 1 + 2.25x$ at $x = 4$ (`4 {{key:ENTER}} 2.25 {{key:×}} 1 {{key:+}}` $\rightarrow 10.0000$), raising to the $3/2$ power and subtracting the lower-limit value (`10 {{key:ENTER}} 1.5 {{key:yˣ}} 1 {{key:-}}` $\rightarrow 30.6228$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 10.0000, X: 30.6228)$), and scaling by `8 {{key:×}} 27 {{key:÷}}` resolves $L = \mathbf{9.0734}$ ($9.07$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 10.0000, X: 9.0734)$—or directly via Romberg quadrature (`1 2.25 X {{key:×}} {{key:+}} {{key:√x}}` in `{{key:EQN}}`, then `0 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:∫}} X`).

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_47:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_47:value}}`** (3-sig-fig target: **`29.0`** ($29.030\text{ kPa})$).

Under isothermal hydrostatic equilibrium $\frac{dP}{dh} = -\frac{P}{H}$, evaluating the scale-height exponent `10500 {{key:+/-}} {{key:ENTER}} 8400 {{key:÷}}` ($-h/H = -1.2500$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: -1.2500)$), exponentiating via `{{key:eˣ}}` ($0.2865$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 0.2865)$), and scaling by standard atmosphere `101.325 {{key:×}}` resolves $P = \mathbf{29.0301\text{ kPa}}$ ($29.0\text{ kPa}$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 29.0301)$.

### Workbook Problem 14.34: Golden Ratio Multi-Nested Radical

Evaluate the infinite nested radical $Q = \sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + \dots}}}} = \phi = \frac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}$.

**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_50:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_50:value}}`** (3-sig-fig target: **`1.62`** (Full mantissa: `1.618033989`)).

Squaring both sides of the self-similar radical $Q = \sqrt{1 + Q}$ yields the golden-ratio quadratic $Q^2 - Q - 1 = 0$, whose positive root $\phi = \frac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}$ evaluates in 4 tokens: `5 {{key:√x}}` ($2.2361$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.2361)$) followed by `1 {{key:+}} 2 {{key:÷}}` resolves $\phi = \mathbf{1.6180}$ ($1.62$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.6180)$.

### Workbook Problem 14.36: Surface Area of Cylindrical Battery Pod via Stats Mode `{{key:Σ+}}`

An applied physics problem asks for the total surface area of a cylindrical battery pod: Radius $r = 15\text{ mm}$, Height $h = 65\text{ mm}$:

$$A_{\text{total}} = 2(\pi r^2) + 2\pi r h = 2\pi (15^2) + 2\pi(15)(65) = 450\pi + 1950\pi = 2400\pi \approx 7,539.82\text{ mm}^2$$

Clearing the statistical registers (`{{key:CLEAR}} {{key:CLΣ}}`), accumulating the twin end-cap areas `15 {{key:x²}} {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 2 {{key:×}} {{key:Σ+}}` ($1,413.72\text{ mm}^2$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 1.0000)$) and lateral shell area `2 {{key:π}} {{key:×}} 15 {{key:×}} 65 {{key:×}} {{key:Σ+}}` ($6,126.11\text{ mm}^2$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.0000)$), and recalling `{{key:SUMS}} {{key:Σx}}` resolves the total surface area $\Sigma x = \mathbf{7,539.82\text{ mm}^2}$ (3-sig-fig target: **`7.54E3`**, $7.54 \times 10^3\text{ mm}^2$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 7539.8224)$—or in factored form $2\pi r(r + h)$, by keying $h = 65$, adding $r = 15$, and recalling $r = 15$ from `LASTX` so the radius is entered only once.

### Workbook Problem 14.39: Infix Operator Precedence vs Native RPN Stack Equivalence

Compare algebraic infix parsing requiring nested parentheses with natural RPN execution for two standard tournament expressions:
1. $(12 - 4) \times (6 + 2) \div 4 = \mathbf{16.0}$  
2. $3 + 4 \times 5 = \mathbf{23.0}$  

*RPN Keystrokes:*  
- Expression 1: `{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_09:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_09:value}}`** (Evaluates intermediate differences and sums directly on the stack without a single parenthesis)  
- Expression 2: `{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_10:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_uil_contest_playbook_seq_10:value}}`** (Multiplying 4 and 5 first, then adding 3, completely mirrors operator precedence without requiring parentheses).  

In Jan Łukasiewicz's postfix logic, operator position alone encodes tree topology without delimiters: evaluating `12 {{key:ENTER}} 4 {{key:-}}` ($8.0000$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 8.0000)$), `6 {{key:ENTER}} 2 {{key:+}}` ($8.0000$ in $X$ with $8.0000$ lifted to $Y$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 8.0000, X: 8.0000)$), and `{{key:×}} 4 {{key:÷}}` yields $\mathbf{16.0000}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 16.0000)$, while `4 {{key:ENTER}} 5 {{key:×}} 3 {{key:+}}` evaluates $\mathbf{23.0000}$ $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 16.0000, X: 23.0000)$.

### Workbook Problem 14.40: Nested Horner's Method for Cubic Polynomials

Evaluate $P(x) = 3x^3 - 5x^2 + 4x - 7$ at $x = 2.5$.
*Speed Optimization Insight:* Evaluating polynomials term-by-term with powers ($x^3, x^2$) wastes time and invites syntax errors. Rewriting in nested Horner form $((3x - 5)x + 4)x - 7$ allows continuous left-to-right streaming on the stack with zero parentheses:  
**Keystrokes:** `{{res:sc32_14_captain_horner_polynomial:keys}}` $\rightarrow$ **`{{res:sc32_14_captain_horner_polynomial:value}}`** (3-sig-fig target: **`18.6`** (Full mantissa: `18.625`)).

William George Horner's 1819 nested synthetic division reduces a degree-$n$ polynomial to $n$ multiply-add pairs: evaluating `3 {{key:ENTER}} 2.5 {{key:×}} 5 {{key:-}}` ($3x - 5 = 2.5000$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 2.5000)$), chaining `2.5 {{key:×}} 4 {{key:+}}` ($(3x - 5)x + 4 = 10.2500$, $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 10.2500)$), and finishing with `2.5 {{key:×}} 7 {{key:-}}` resolves $P(2.5) = \mathbf{18.6250}$ ($18.6$) $(T: 0.0000, Z: 0.0000, Y: 0.0000, X: 18.6250)$—and by pre-filling the stack with $x = 2.5$ (`2.5 {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}} {{key:ENTER}} 3 {{key:×}} 5 {{key:-}} {{key:×}} 4 {{key:+}} {{key:×}} 7 {{key:-}}`), Register $T$ feeds $2.5$ into every Horner stage automatically so `2.5` is typed only once!
